FOUNDATIONS OF STRUCTURE AND FUNCTION

Lucía Reed López

[PDF p. 2]

Basic concepts of waves

IMAGING TECHNIQUES

1st image: normal x-ray radiograph, where we can observe the lungs and the heart, which will provide info about the size of the heart. X-ray radiographs are helpful but dangerous as they are ionizing. They can cause damage to DNA. It’s the most dangerous imaging system, but the simplest and cheapest (along with MRI).

  • 2nd image: x-ray CT scan (computed tomography) . It has the advantage that it provides very precise and accurate information, regarding location and size, about the different organs of the body.

  • 3rd image: MRI (magnetic resonance imaging). It relies on a magnetic field with a very high intensity (1.5 teslas-5 teslas, 10^4 times the magnetic field of the earth) . There is a limit to the number of magnetic fields a person can be submitted to without suffering consequences. In MRI we can differentiate all the different tissues, allowing us to observe the thickness of the walls of the heart.

  • 4th image: Ultrasound. It’s the less risky of the techniques. It also has the advantage that it can be observed in real time.

We have to evaluate the risks and the benefits we will obtain by using each imaging technique. The objective is to balance out the risks and the benefits.

Depending on what we want to observe, we will use one technique or another and assume

more or less risk.

We will focus on EM waves (there is an electromagnetic field being propagated) and pressure waves (which are the base of Ultrasound) .

ELECTROMAGNETIC WAVES (EM)

A wave is an oscillation of a physical magnitud that propagates in time and space, transporting energy.

There is a source of waves, where the creation of

the wave occurs. The created wave propagates (it will need to be characterized), and once it reaches the target, it can go through it creating a transmitted wave, be absorbed or be sent back creating a reflected wave.

[PDF p. 3]

There are 2 main families of waves: X-rays and ultrasounds

  • X-rays (radiography or CTs): It’s based on

the transmitted wave.

  • Ultrasounds: It’s based on the reflected

wave

We can find 2 other families which are closely

related:

  • MRI: (the most complex system concerning medical physics). There is a process which occurs, known as resonance: a person receives EM radiation (of the order of a microwave ) and absorbs it, and after a time “t”, the amount of radiation accumulated by the person is emitted, revealing an image. It’s a 2 step process.

  • PET: a drug with a specific isotope is introduced in the patient, and it emits radioactivity depending on the amount absorbed by each organ. These isotopes can only be absorbed by cancer cells, so it’s very useful and precise to detect

tumors.

PERIOD, WAVELENGTH AND FREQUENCY

The trajectory of a wave is a straight line. A source radiates waves, forming a radius from the source to the objective. Magnitude H (positive or negative, depending on whether it oscillates up or down) has the property of periodicity. we move the same distance r each time, and we reach

the same H value. This distance “r” which reflects

periodicity is known as wavelength.

Period (T): For a specific point r1, we can analyze the behaviour of the wave in relation to time: there is a periodicity in time, a time t after which the wave is found at the same H. This periodic magnitude is known as Period. Measured in seconds. Angular frequency (ω) is equal to 2π/T

  • Wavelength ( λ ): reflects the periodicity in space. Measured in meters . Wavenumber “K” is equal to 2π λ/ .

  • Frequency equals 1/T. It’s the inverse value of the Period, measured in Hz or s-1.

[PDF p. 4]

HARMONIC WAVES

Physical magnitude P: P(x,t) = p0 sin (wt - kx + φ0)

This is the most simple equation that describes the oscillation of a wave.

p0= A (Amplitude)

P(x,t) = indicates it oscillates in space

and time

● How can we characterize a source?

The source generates waves, which propagate energy. The most important point to characterize the source is how much energy is produced per second. This is known as the Power (P) of the source.

  • How much of the energy arrives at each point?

The energy is distributed on the whole surface, so only a small part will reach the patient. We need to define a magnitude which describes how much energy reaches each point in space . In other words, how the power is distributed in space. This magnitude is known as Intensity (I) of the wave.

In order to characterize the source, we use P.

In order to characterize a wave, we use I.

REFLECTED W., TRANSMITTED W. AND ABSORPTION

A part of the wave will be transmitted, and a part will be reflected. The intensity is a magnitude that has to be preserved during this process. Therefore, the intensity (energy) is split into the reflection and transmission waves. This property is conserved in any interphase. Each time there is an interphase, one part is transmitted and the other, reflected.

● REFLECTED INTENSITY

    - For **X-rays and MRI**, the **reflected intensity** is very very **small,** so we can

reject it. - But in ultrasounds the intensity of the reflected wave is much bigger, it’s important as it is the wave that will give us an image.

We can distinguish two different types of reflection:

  • Specular reflection: if the direction of the reflected waves are parallel (smooth surfaces)

  • Diffuse reflection: if the directions of reflected waves follow different

trajectories (unequal surfaces).

[PDF p. 5]

● ABSORPTION AND TRANSMITTED INTENSITY

2 different situations:

  1. Incoming wave acts on material B with intensity Io, and it’s density makes

If = ½ Io

  1. If we double the thickness/density of the material, we would think that each half is absorbed by each material. But this is wrong. If2 = ½ If = ¼ Io

If we have 1 layer, 2^-1 ; 2 layers, 2^-2; 3 layers 2^-3,… etc.

The take home message is that if a given thickness reduces the intensity by a factor k, doubling the thickness of the material does not mean that the radiation is reduced by 2k or that the radiation absorbed is 2(1 − k). Rather, the wave leaving the material has undergone an exponential process, k^ 2 . In conclusion, the absorption of a wave is an exponential process.

This exponential behavior is characteristic of a large number of wave processes involving interaction with matter. In other cases, we will look at other physical magnitudes that are absorbed according to an equation of type:

The constant α is called the absorption or attenuation coefficient and depends on the physical process considered (the type of wave) and the material. The constant P0 represents the value of the variable before entry into the absorbing material. The expression describes the value taken by P after passing through a

thickness x of the material.

The coefficient is a direct indicator of how strong the interaction is : a high value means that the material absorbs a lot, and a low value means that it absorbs little.

DISPERSION

The original direction of the wave can be modified after passing through a non-homogeneous material. This is known as dispersion . It appears due to the interaction

between the atoms in the material and the wave. We can

find:

Normal scattering, if the degree of dispersion is very small. The beam broadens around its original trajectory.

  • Back scattering, which is NOT the same as reflection . Reflection appears in the interphase and back scattering, inside the material. It occurs when the degree of dispersion is high. Therefore, a significant part of the beam can rebound backwards producing backscattering.

[PDF p. 6]

[PDF p. 7]

Radioactivity

RADIOACTIVE SOURCES

Radioactivity consists in the emission by some nuclei of energy in the form of an electromagnetic wave or of subatomic particles, in order to obtain a more stable state. We can find natural and artificial sources of radioactivity.

  • Natural sources: natural radioactivity comes from isotopes present in nature. These isotopes have

unstable nuclei which will emit radiation.

Nuclei are not static, they are always moving. If they move too fast, they can become unstable. These isotopes can decay into other elements or isotopes to become stable, through a nuclear reaction.

  • Artificial sources: they are created mainly by using electricity. A charged particle is accelerated through a device with a differential voltage (dV). This potential difference exerts a force on the electron, causing it to be accelerated. Its velocity increases a lot, and it’s released through one of the plates, allowing it to escape. This newly accelerated particle can now interact with matter. In this way, X-rays are formed. Artificial sources can be switched off, stopping the x-ray emission.

WAVE-PARTICLE DUALITY

Particles and waves are different perspectives of a single entity, which sometimes manifests itself as a particle and others as a wave. They don’t exist in an independent manner. This is referred to as wave-particle duality.

Radioactivity can be produced from a source of waves or particles.

  • Waves: different types

- Natural waves: gamma rays

- Artificial waves: X rays

● Particles:

- protons

- positrons (e+)

- electrons (e-)

- Alpha particles (He nucleus)

[PDF p. 8]

PHOTOELECTRIC EFFECT

The energy of EM radiation waves travels in small packets of energy, particles known as photons. The energy per photon is determined by the frequency of the radiation:

If the frequency of the incident wave is higher than a threshold value (E ion), then there are electrons that are ejected from the metal. This phenomenon is called the photo-electric effect . In other words, if the incident radiation has a frequency inferior to the

threshold, electrons are not emitted whatever the intensity of the wave. However, above the threshold frequency, the greater the intensity, the more electrons are dislodged. If, on the other hand, we increase the frequency of the radiation, the more kinetic energy in the ejected electrons.

Conclusion : when we increase the intensity of the wave, we modify the number of photons which reach the target, NOT the energy of the photons (which remains unchanged). The final kinetic energy will remain the same, but more electrons will be dislodged (Increasing the intensity means more bonds are broken within tissue when the electrons reach the patient). If, on the other hand, we increase the frequency and therefore the energy of the incident photons, the kinetic energy will increase but the number of electrons will remain the same.

Radiation that can dislodge electrons, and therefore ionize atoms, is referred to as ionizing radiation. By exclusion, all other types of radiation are non-ionizing. In the case of the EM spectrum, the transition between ionizing and non-ionizing occurs in the UV region.

  • Radiation that has energy of over 10 eV per photon is considered to be ionizing radiation. Ultraviolet light, X rays, and γ rays are ionizing.
  • Radiation that has energy under 10 eV: visible light, infrared, microwaves, and radio waves are therefore non-ionizing.

TYPES OF IONIZING RADIATION

Ionizing radiation can come from the nuclei of certain chemical elements that emit it naturally, or it can be generated artificially, by accelerating charged particles until they acquire high energy or by generating photons with high ionizing power. Focusing on natural sources of ionizing radiation, there are 3 types:

[PDF p. 9]

  • α radiation: radioactive process in which an unstable nucleus decays into a more

stable state, releasing a Helium nucleus (alpha-particle). In this process, E is released and conserved : the initial nucleus delivers energy to the resulting nucleus and the alpha particle (energy along with electrical charge are conserved). Most of

the E is transferred to the helium nucleus.

In all the different radioactive alpha decays, the energy delivered to each alpha particle is the same. It’s therefore a monoenergetic process: all the particles have the same energy. This is what is known as monoenergetic radiation (this also occurs in a gamma process).

  • γ radiation: a nucleus oscillates, and during this oscillation it releases energy in the

form of photons and in a monoenergetic way. The original nuclei had “excess” energy that has been released in the form of EM radiation (we can imagine that the nucleons were at a level of energy higher than some baseline level, and that they have undergone de-excitation).

The initial nucleus is said to be in a metastable situation. The different states

constitute isomers of the element.

If photons of the same energy are created in other ways , from artificial sources , they are referred to as X rays.

  • β radiation: we refer to “beta -” radiation as beta radiation. This type of radiation is

composed of electrons deriving from the nucleus, which arise from the disintegration of a neutron into a proton. In this type of radiation, the emitted electrons are not found to be monoenergetic; the electron will sometimes receive more or less energy. Their energy can have any value between zero and a maximum value: there is a continuous spectrum of energy, because any intermediate value can exist. Therefore, this type of radiation is polyenergetic. The total energy released will be an average of all the energies.

[PDF p. 10]

How come the process isn’t monoenergetic? This non-monoenergetic characteristic cannot be explained by a reaction in which an electron is the only product. To explain it, Pauli postulated that another particle (one with neutral charge and little mass) was created. This particle was called a neutrino,

which is now referred to as electron antineutrino.

β + radiation is a process very similar to β- radiation. But in this case, an electron antiparticle is emitted, known as a positron. It’s identical to an electron, but with an opposite charge (+). The positron has become important in a method of diagnosis in medicine, the positron emission tomography (PET scan).

[PDF p. 11]

Interaction of radiation with matter

PROCESSES OF DECAY

  • Alpha process: situation where an isotope will decay producing an alpha particle, which corresponds to 2n + 2p = a Helium nucleus. A particle is created.

  • Gamma process: Nucleons are found in a configuration with large amounts of energy. This energy is released in the form of EM radiation (photon packets). There is no modification in the nature of the initial particle . Photons are created.

  • Beta process: particles are created which didn’t previously exist on the initial particle, and are released.

    - **Beta + :** proton decays into neutron + positron + neutrino
- **Beta - :** neutron decays into proton + electron + antineutrino

These types of decay are ionizing radiations which come

from natural sources .

The energy released is the one that will be introduced into the patient. It will be absorbed by the surrounding tissue. Therefore, we need to know the energy which is being released into our patient.

MONOENERGETIC AND POLYENERGETIC RADIATIONS

Alpha and gamma radiations are monoenergetic processes. For a large set of atoms, all the different ones will decay producing the same amount of energy.

Beta processes on the other hand are polyenergetic (continuous spectrum): if we have a set of n atoms and we compare the energy of a beta particle for 2 different radioactive decays, the energy released will be different for beta 1 particle than for beta 2.

Alpha and gamma particle histograms present a sharp distribution. Beta particle histogram presents a broad distribution of energies. We can determine an average value for each distribution.

[PDF p. 12]

HOW TO PREDICT THE BEHAVIOUR OF A RADIOACTIVE SOURCE

A radioactive disintegration is a stochastic process, meaning that it’s impossible to predict the behaviour of a single nuclide. We don’t know when it’s going to disintegrate. But we do know that the probability of disintegration must be the same for all the nuclides of the same isotope. The evolution of the number of atoms which remain follows an exponential decay.

If we have a sample with N 0 nuclides, then the number of nuclei that have not disintegrated N(t) after time t will be given by:

This exponential curve is determined by the decay constant λ (which is specific to each element) or by the mean lifetime (TAU) τ0 = 1/ λ, which corresponds to the inverse value of the decay constant.

  • Mean lifetime ( τ0) : The mean lifetime represents the time at which the number of nuclides which haven’t disintegrated is N 0 /e (in other words, 37% ). It can also be interpreted as the mean of the survival times of the nuclides of the sample. It varies enormously for different isotopes.

- Half-life (t ½ ): It’s NOT the same as the mean lifetime. For this big group of atoms, we can determine the half life, which represents the time it will take for half of the atoms to decay (half of the isotopes have decayed and the other half remain stable). Only N 0 /2 (half) remain non-disintegrated (in other words, 50% ).

Activity (A) : the activity of a sample indicates how many atoms disintegrate per second. It’s value depends on the number of atoms (N) present in the sample. It’s measured in Becquerels (disintegration per second) or in Curies.

Knowing the Activity, we can know the energy released per second by a radioactive source. The Activity of a sample can be obtained quite easily if we know one of the three values. (landa, half life or mean lifetime).

[PDF p. 13]

ARTIFICIAL SOURCES . X-RAY TUBES

All clinical devices that generate high-energy photons function on the basis of the same principle. It’s all carried out in 2 stages:

1. Electrons are accelerated in an X-ray tube until they have high kinetic energy. For this, we employ 2 plates with a high voltage difference between them (∆𝑉). Any charged particle placed between the two will be affected by an EM force. We place an electron, and due to the setup of the plates, the exerted EM force points in a specific direction. We can calculate the Kinetic energy of the electron as it leaves the plates:

The energy released can be

calculated as:

(I = electrical current, measured in Amps)

2. The released electrons are made to collide with a target made of a very dense material (heavy material), which implies there will be a high probability of interaction between electrons and atoms from the material. The impact stops the electrons, and the energy is

transferred.

As a result of these interactions, there is a radioactive process in which high energy photons are generated (same as in gamma radiation).

When the electron approaches an atom inside this heavy material, it will interact with each of the particles of the atom (through weak, strong or catastrophic interactions) . As it passes close to other charged particles, it will be subjected to forces which will cause its deviation . On deviation, the e- emits some of its kinetic energy in the form of EM radiation. Its velocity therefore drops. This process is known as braking radiation or Bremsstrahlung radiation. In addition to the interaction, the e- can be completely stopped if it undergoes direct impact, or it can collide with an orbital electron, knocking it out if the atom (both eleave), In this case, the remaining gap will be filled by an electron of a superior layer, releasing energy in the form of a photon. The type of interaction will depend on the trajectory . After each interaction, energy will be released in the form of EM radiation (photons).

  • Big distance = electron is slightly deviated, releasing a small amount of energy in the form of photons.

  • When the electron passes closely, the deviation will be much stronger, generating a much higher energy photons.

These first two interactions are both Bremsstrahlung interactions.

[PDF p. 14]

  • If the electron collides with the nucleus, there is a direct hit which results in a

catastrophic interaction, in which the electron is completely destroyed.

  • In the last case, the incoming electron collides with an orbital electron . Said electron is displaced and a gap is left; therefore, an upper level electron transitions to the lower level, releasing energy (photons) in the process.

Ephot = h · frequency

For gamma radiation, frequency = Ephoton/ h For X ray frequency, f = Ephoton / h

They are the same/equivalent, but gamma particles originate from a natural source and X-rays from artificial sources. It’s impossible to tell them both apart.

INTERACTION OF RADIATION WITH MATTER

  1. EM RADIATION

The most important characteristic of EM radiation is the energy per photon. For very low frequencies on the spectrum, we have radio waves, then microwaves, infra-red, visible light, UV light, X-rays and gamma rays. In the middle of the UV band, there is a value (10 eV) that separates the spectrum into two parts:

  • Non-ionizing radiation (can be measured with classical physics).

We analyze 1 single process. The EM field is propagating; when it reaches the atom, the charged particles begin to vibrate and oscillate at the same frequency as the field. The increase in frequency increases the temperature of this material. Can be explained by classical point of view.

  • Ionizing radiation (measured by taking into account quantum physics) . We analyze 3

different processes. For ionizing radiation, we cannot use the concept of continuous wave. We have to apply the wave-particle duality principle, and divide it into photons. There is 1 photon per interaction. In general terms, this results in the extraction of an electron located in a specific orbital.

E k = E photon - E ionization

[PDF p. 15]

  • Photoelectric effect: this phenomenon can only be explained by quantum mechanics. In the EM wave, energy travels in packets (photons) which carry a specific amount of energy. The interaction occurs between 1 photon and 1

electron.

Main characteristics: this behaviour appears for lower energies (close to UV limits) ; after the interaction, the photon is destroyed ; the electron is dislodged and ejected, and it’s E k = E photon - E ionization . No other products are released, only the electron.

    - **Compton effect/Compton scattering:** interaction once more

between a single photon and single electron. But the energy carried by the photon is so large, it’s impossible to transfer it to a single electron.

Main characteristics: this situation occurs for intermediate

energies. The high energy photon will transfer part of its energy to the e-, another will be derived to surpass ionization energy, and the remaining energy is released as a new photon (though now it carries less energy than the original). In this situation, the result isn’t only an electron, but an electron + a new photon.

    - **Pair production:** the interaction occurs between **1 photon and 1 e-.** In the

beginning we have a high energy photon, which after a spontaneous process, is split into 2 new particles with mass: an electron and a positron. The large amount of energy is transformed into a small amount of mass. Main characteristics: pair production occurs when the energy of the photon is at least 1.022 MeV (mega). The energy is high enough to spontaneously produce a positron-electron pair. Therefore, 2 new elements are formed

that didn’t exist before.

One of the particles is present in nature (electron), the other not (positron) ; therefore, after the pair production, another process occurs: the positron will move away and collide with an electron in another position of the material. Both particles cancel each other out, transforming themselves into two photons each with energy of 511 keV. These two photons will propagate in opposite directions. This process of annihilation of positrons is the basis of positron emission tomography, although in this technology, the positrons derive from radiopharmaceuticals that generate β + radiation.

Overview:

The photoelectric effect implies the destruction of the photon. Low energy photons

Compton effect results in the production of 1 photon. Intermediate energy photon

Pair-production results in 2 photons. High energy photon (1.022 MeV)

In the last 2 processes (compton and pair production), there is scattering of the trajectory of the released elements, which will be a problem.

[PDF p. 16]

● Why are these processes important?

Depending on the energy of the incident photon, we will observe one process or another. For energies up to 30 keV (low energy), the dominant effect is the photo-electric; above this level, the dominant process is the Compton effect up to 20 MeV; from this point, pair-production becomes dominant. (this is true when the medium in which the interaction occurs is water; for each different tissue, the value changes slightly).

In clinical applications, this absorption process is exponential: the greater the thickness of the material, the greater the absorption. Thus, we can calculate the intensity of the transmitted beam from the incident beam I 0:

Where µ total is the attenuation coefficient that takes into account all of the processes

discussed above.

It is possible to determine the attenuation coefficient independently for each of the processes. If we combine them, we can obtain the attenuation coefficient for X-rays as a function of energy:

Depending on the energy of the incident photons and therefore, the attenuation coefficient will differ with energy.

  • In the case of photoelectric effect, the attenuation coefficient decreases very quickly as energy increases. In addition to this, the absorption coefficient (in other words, the probability of interaction through photo-electric effect) depends on the cube of the atomic number of the material, Z. Therefore, for a beam of photons of given energy, the probability of interaction through the photoelectric effect depends on Z 3 . X-rays in this range of energies are good candidates for identifying the chemical composition of different tissues and differentiating them, as they create good contrast. The photoelectric effect is the one which has more interaction with matter, as photons with low energy are more absorbed than those with high energy.

  • In contrast, in the energy range of the Compton effect there is no clear dependence on Z or on the energy of the photon. But because of the nature of the process ( deviating photons), there is scattering of the beams. X-rays in this range are not good candidates for medical imaging, as the scattering of the beam results in a blurred image. It’s resolved and avoided by placing a led grid to stop the scatter radiation, recovering a much clearer image.

[PDF p. 17]

Both photoelectric and Compton scatter contribute to the total attenuation of a beam as it passes through material. The relative contribution of photoelectric and Compton interactions depends on a few factors:

  • As the x-ray photon energy increases:

    Significant decrease in photoelectric interactions; Compton scatter becomes the predominant cause of attenuation at higher energies.

    • There is a reduction in the total attenuation (more photons are transmitted through the material).

  • As the atomic number increases:

    There is no change in Compton interactions.

- Many more photoelectric interactions.

Greater attenuation of the x-ray photons (less penetration).

  • As the tissue mass density increases:

    • There is an increase in both Compton and photoelectric

interactions.

  • Greater attenuation of the x-ray photons (less penetration).
  1. CHARGED PARTICLES

When charged particles penetrate a material, they find themselves surrounded by other particles that are also charged. Therefore, they experience Coulomb-forces. A charged particle that interacts with matter can lose energy through 3 process:

  1. Emitting photons: on interacting with other charged particles, the trajectory changes (the particle is deviated) and photons are emitted. Bremsstrahlung interactions.

  2. Collisions with electrons, knocking them out of their orbit.

  3. Collisions with atomic nuclei.

An electron is very small and has very little mass (light particle) , and so it is very probable that in these interactions it will deviate appreciably from its trajectory, while the trajectory of protons or alpha particles (heavy particles) with which it collides will hardly be modified. The penetration length in the case of interaction with charged particles is known as range. It represents the distance that the charged particles can travel.

The result in both cases (electrons and protons) is that each of the incident particles will generate a cascade of charged particles and photons around the trajectory that the initial particle has followed.

[PDF p. 18]

  • In the case of electrons : all three types of process occur (the most likely ones being bremsstrahlung and electron-collision). As soon as an electron enters into matter, it is affected by other particles and will interact with them, deviating from its trajectory and releasing part of its energy to generate photons and new electrons. The photons and new electrons will continue interacting, deviating and in turn giving up more energy to generate even more photons and electrons until the remaining photons and electrons can no longer ionize more atoms. Finally, the remaining energy will be deposited as heat. We observe a whole cascade of electrons. The energy is deposited in a more or less droplet-shaped volume around the initial trajectory of the electron: the energy is dispersed.

- In the case of protons: the most common interaction is Coulomb interaction with electrons. The procedure is similar in the case of protons, but in this case the energy is deposited along the length of the initial trajectory. Protons do not deviate from the initial trajectory and do not disperse in the same way.

The absorption of charged particles is NOT exponential. There is a maximum distance where we can expect to find an interaction of these electrons and protons with matter.

  • In the case of electrons: the energy deposited by a beam of electrons decreases rapidly with the depth

traveled.

  • In the case of protons and alpha particles: the maximum energy absorbed occurs at a given depth and then drops off rapidly to zero. The peak is referred to as the Bragg peak.

DOSIMETRY. BEHAVIOUR OF THE ABSORBED ENERGY INSIDE THE CELL

The Absorbed Dose is the parameter that defines the effect of radiation on a given medium. It’s calculated as the absorbed energy per unit mass.

The corresponding unit for Dose is Joule/Kg = Gray (Gy)

However, the dose doesn’t measure the biological effects on the patient, which depends on many things such as the type of particle, tissue in which the dose is deposited,.. The first thing to take into account is the type of effects that we can expect to find in an organism subjected to ionizing radiation: Stochastic effects and Deterministic effects.

[PDF p. 19]

  • Stochastic e�ects: a stochastic effect is a random effect that appears for lower doses

of radiation, where an increase of dose brings about an increase in the probability of the effect occurring, but not an increase in the seriousness of the effect. The seriousness of the effect will be independent of the dose. This is the case of a DNA mutation induced by radiation. Stochastic effects are purely probabilistic, and have no threshold.

  • Deterministic e�ects: a deterministic effect is that in which the seriousness

increases with the number of incident particles. An example is erythema, which can appear on the skin with radiotherapy treatment, or sun burns. Deterministic effects appear for higher doses. Increasing the dose increases the seriousness of the possible effects.

As said, the absorbed dose considers only the physics of the process, and is measured in Grays. Therefore, Grays don’t take into account the biological effects.

To measure the biological effect, we use:

Equivalent dose (H) takes into account the relative damage that each type of radiation can cause compared with that of a beam of photons that deposits the same amount of energy. W R is a weighting factor that has no units and that depends on each type of radiation.

  • Effective dose (E) (takes into account that different tissues react to radiation. W T is a weighting factor that is different for different tissues) .

The units for both equivalent and effective dose are Sievert [H] = Sv.

[PDF p. 20]

Conventional X-ray

Radiology workshop I

(Bibliography: “Learning radiology” (W. Herring); Webpage: Radiopaedia)

Radiology is a medical specialty, which deals with generating images of the inside of the body employing different techniques. These images are read and interpreted by radiologists, for diagnosis and treatment of disease (although to a lesser extent)

History

X-rays were discovered by W.C. Roentgen in 1895, for which he received a nobel prize. On the 8th of November, 1895, his wife put her hand under the machine he was working on, revealing the first X-ray (Bertha’s hand). It was the first time a part of a human body was able to be seen through imaging.

Since then, radiology has hugely evolved, and we can now evaluate bone, vasculature, organs,.. through many imaging techniques. We can see what is going on inside a patient non-invasively.

Generation of X-rays

For conventional X-ray, we use an X-ray tube to form the rays.

  • Phase 1: Generation of electrons: after generating an induced current in the X-ray tube, we can accelerate electrons by using a differential potential.
  • Phase 2: Displacement of electrons: The cathode emits electrons into the vacuum and the anode collects them, establishing a flow of electrical current through the tube (known as beam). A high differential potential will accelerate the electrons, which

collide with the anode material.

Phase 3: Generation of photons: the photons generated by the impact of electrons on the anode will produce the x-ray beam. They escape through a small window (used to cause a unique direction) and impact on the area of interest, in this case, the human body. The rest of the X-ray beam which doesn’t escape through the window is absorbed by the metal housing surrounding the device.

[PDF p. 21]

Conventional X-ray

Area of radiology which uses X-rays to obtain images through a flat digital detector.

X-rays go through the patient and are detected by a flat digital detector. The energy which manages to go through the patient is quantified by the detector, which translates the photons into an electrical signal, and from there, into a digital image.

Radiographic densities. Grayscale

The grayscale emerges according to the amount of radiation detected. It’s inversely proportional to the radiation absorbed by the material which is irradiated. This differential absorption allows us to produce an image.

  • Structures which retain the x-ray will show as white (bone).
  • Structures and spaces which don’t retain X-rays are seen as darker/black.

Grayscale (according to the amount of radiation detected):

There are 4 densities (or 5, if we consider metal) considering the scale of grey. Therefore, bones appear as white, while cavities are seen as dark spaces. The detectability of a lesion on the radiography will be based on the contrast it generates with respect to the surrounding

tissue.

CHEST X-RAY

There is a dedicated system for chest x-rays

Usually taken from the back to the front (posterior-anterior, PA). Why is this done? To avoid magnification effect: the beam of electrons is like a fan. If the person is very close to the source, the structures will be magnified when projected over the detector. By doing its posterior-anterior position, the heart is further from the source and therefore the heart is seen without enlargement, maintaining its normal size. By doing PA, we avoid magnification of the cardiac

silhouette.

  • Distance is fixed: 150-180 cm, to avoid mistakes when reading the image

High kV

  • Low dose

  • PA (posterior anterior view) and lateral views can be

taken

[PDF p. 22]

Anatomy of a chest x-ray

  • In the centre, we can observe the heart . ⅓ of the heart is on the right, and ⅔ on the

left.

On the sides of the heart, we can observe the lungs, which are seen in black as they

contain air.

  • The ribs are arched and curved from the back to the front. Therefore the anterior ribs

can be seen as more curved.

  • In the lower part we would find the diaphragm .
  • On the right hand side, the liver, and the stomach can be seen on the left (seen darker as it contains a cavity, the gastric chamber).

Importance of PA view and lateral view

A mass is found in the mid-chest, specifically in the mid portion of the right lung. But through the lateral view we can additionally observe that it’s

found at the back. Therefore the mass is in the

back of the right lower lobe. This shows the importance of obtaining more than one perspective.

Strategies to assess the quality of the x-ray

- Inspiration: depending on the inspiration, the lungs and diaphragm will be observed in very different ways. The x-ray has to be taken while the patient is taking a deep breath in. This is assessed through the rib-diaphragm relationship (6th ant rib-10th posterior).

- Alignment: to see that the person is straight, and not oblique, we observe that the distance from the clavicles to spinous processes is

the same

- Radiation: adaptation to the thickness of the patient and evaluation of grey areas. Higher thickness will imply higher attenuation.

[PDF p. 23]

Portable X-ray systems

Used when the patient is injured or recently operated, and has to stay in bed. The detectors are put behind the patient’s back, and the picture is taken in an anterior-posterior view.

Lower X-ray quality

  • AP view

Supine position

Less inspiration is carried out

ABDOMINAL X-RAY

  • Distance: 110 cm

  • Normal kV

Mid-high radiation dose needed

Supine position (laying down)

This x-ray is more difficult to read as x-ray doesn’t

differentiate the different soft tissues.

However, they are very useful for assessing patients with abdominal pain.

Supine and orthostatic images

Air always goes up. Therefore, in supine position everything is equally distributed. But in the up-right position the upper region of cavities are seen as darker, as air floats

towards the top. For patients with abdominal pain we take x-rays in both positions.

[PDF p. 24]

BREAST X-RAYS

A mammography is an x-ray taken of the breast. Screening makes it able to detect early

tumors.

There are dedicated breast systems

CONTRAST

Contrast can be used to further study regions such as the digestive tract, nephro-urinary system, female genital tract or any conduit in general. We can either create positive contrast (by introducing barium or iodine) or negative contrast (air).

Urography: intravenous iodinated contrast is injected and excreted by the kidneys

  • Bowel study: Double-contrast barium
  • Gastric study: oral barium contrast

Interventional radiology

X-ray based technique in which we subtract the contrast from other structures to focus on

the structure of interest.

In interventional radiology, X-rays can be used to observe the trajectory of a catheter. It helps advance the catheter to the area of interest, where it will inflate and open a blocked vessel or insert a stent to prevent the vessel from collapsing. The catheter is inserted following the Seldinger technique (insert needle, insert guidewire, remove the needle, insert a catheter, remove guidewire and inject contrast)

Digital subtraction technique

[PDF p. 26]

KEY CONCEPTS. kV and mAs

- kV: Kilovolts = measures the amount of differential voltage present in the x-ray tube. Differential voltage is what will accelerate electrons going from the cathode to the anode. Therefore: ↑kV = ↑differential potential = ↑kinetic energy (velocity) = ↑ energy electron that are going to transmit their energy to the anode = ↑ energy photons In conclusion, increasing kV increases the energy of the x-ray beam photons. It also affects intensity, but the main effect we focus on for kV is the effect on energy. It’s the only variable which will affect the energy of the beam.

- mAs: milliampere second = measure of radiation produced (milliampere) over a set amount of time (seconds) via an x-ray tube, at a given kV. mA = tube current; mAs = radiation released per second. Increasing mAs means an increase in intensity but NOT in energy An increase in tube current (mA) results in a higher production of electrons that are inside the x-ray tube which will, therefore, increase the quantity of x-radiation. We release more photons, but they have the same energy as kV remains unchanged.

↑ kV = increase in x-ray photons energy ↑ mA = increase in current = increase in the number of e- released = higher intensity beam (more photons), but NOT higher energy

In relation to contrast and noise (image quality): ↑ kV = decrease in contrast (structures will appear darker), but more penetration ↓ kV = higher contrast (structures will appear whiter), more differential absorption ↑ mA = decrease in noise, better quality

Image 1: kV too low, too much contrast between tissues and little penetration Image 2: kV is increased to improve penetration. But now kV is too high (high photon energy, little absorption and much penetration. Photons with low energy are more absorbed) Image 3: modify mAs to correct image density. Reduces amount of radiation (milliamperes/second ↓) and corrects image quality.

[PDF p. 27]

Computed tomography (CT)

Radiology workshop II

Lesion in back of right lower lung: Issues with X-ray can be resolved by using CT scans. It’s the imaging modality of choice to detect cancer and neoplasms, to asses cancer development,… but also when you suspect patient with pneumonia, with abdominal infection, to asses the heart, … 80% of CTs are used for cancer patients but it can be used for many other purposes. It’s a very fast imaging technique, very available and not expensive . The big problem with CT is the amount of radiation.

HISTORY

CT scanners were first introduced in 1971 by Godfrey N. Hounsfield . The objective was to create a three dimensional image of an object, taking multiple measurements of it with X-rays from different angles and using a computer to reconstruct the image from hundreds of overlapping, intersecting planes. Initially, to obtain 1 slice it took 7 minutes. Nowadays, 1 slice can be acquired in 0.3 seconds, and reconstructed structures in 5 seconds.

PRINCIPLES OF CT

CT is a diagnostic imaging procedure that uses X-rays to acquire cross-sectional “slices” of the body. These cross-sections are reconstructed to recreate a 3D image of the studied object. As it’s mainly acquired in an axial plane, it’s also known

as CAT scan.

The CT X-ray tube emits a beam of X-rays/photons that goes through the patient and passes through detectors which record the intensity of attenuated radiation and convert it into an electrical signal. This is processed on a computer where the signal is transformed into a grayscale image in which brightness is proportional to the density (in UH).

As we are taking images from different angles, the tube is rotating around the patient where beam and detector move in synchrony.

The slice is made up of small volumetric elements that represent attenuation, called voxels (3D). When these elements are placed on a plane (2D), they become pixels.

[PDF p. 28]

The units used to measure the density within the pixel are the Hounsfield units, which range from values of -1000 to +3000. Each pixel is assigned a shade of grey based on its

attenuation value.

However, the human eye can distinguish only 30-40 gray values. Therefore, the target range of HU needs to be adapted to our eye by establishing specific windows. In conclusion, CT allows us to detect where things are, and characterize them according to density by using Hounsfield

units.

Grayscale:

Black structures (- dense) and white structures (+ dense). The densities are determined within the gray scale. The 0 value corresponds to water, and -1000 to air . Dense structures appear white (such as bone), whereas air and fat appear black or grey.

When we look at the picture, we can ask the machine to reconstruct the image by either using a soft tissue window or a lung window. We normally order 2 CT scans with two centered areas of interest: lung and soft tissue. Each window allows us to differentiate specific structures (same image acquisition, but different image reconstruction afterwards).

  • Soft tissue windows allow us to observe and assess soft tissue

(bone=white; muscle=gray; fat= darker gray). Soft tissues can be observed nicely, but lungs are seen as completely black. We cannot assess air or lung parenchyma. This reconstruction can be used to evaluate the chest and abdomen, but not the lungs.

  • Lung window can be used to assess the lung parenchyma. We can clearly observe the inside of the lungs, but we cannot

differentiate other structures. In CT we can also observe metal

density, by using iodine contrast injected through the vein, to look at blood vessels and lung parenchyma.

CT SYSTEMS

There are different CT acquisition modes/systems:

Sequential

Multislice/multidetector system (by placing multiple detectors) : rotates while the patient moves at the same time, with 4 detectors placed within the machine. The bid advantage is that it allows us to acquire images faster as it covers

more surface in less time.

Spiral, helical: rotation and movement of the patient

occur at the same time

Dual-source: 2 tubes are

found emitting radiation.

Fastest CT on the market.

Specially good for observing and assessing

the heart.

[PDF p. 29]

Dual source imaging, also known as spectral imaging, allows us to differentiate the structures depending on x-ray uptake. It can be done using contrast or not. It uses two separate x-ray photon energy spectra, allowing the interrogation of materials that have different attenuation properties at different energies .It allows to differentiate, for example, urinary stones depending on energy uptake. We can tell if the stone has to be removed, or if it can be dissolved with medication and no need for invasive surgery.

IMAGE QUALITY

CTs can be observed from the 3 different plains and perspectives (coronal, sagittal, axial) . Axial images are usually combined with other perspectives to assess diagnosis. Images are obtained in the axial plain but they can be processed to observe other perspectives.

Factors that affect image quality

  • Radiation

Spatial resolution

  • Noise

  • Artifacts

Concepts

  • Kilovoltaje (kV)

Represents photon energy. It reflects the energy in our image based on penetration capacity.

  • More kV = more radiation. By increasing kV, more energy is used to obtain high quality imaging

  • Less kV = less radiation. Structures absorb radiation but we get image noise (grainy)

  • Miliampere seconds (mAs)

Represents the amount of photons released per second (intensity).

- More mAs = more radiation, but implies there is less noise

The throwback of using high Kv and mAs is the high radiation dose.

Factors affecting image quality

1. Spatial resolution

The ability to obtain small details with high contrast. It’s influenced by system geometry, and determines image

definition.

[PDF p. 30]

Factors influencing spatial resolution:

➢ Slice thickness: the thinner the image, the

more anatomical imaging (high spatial resolution), but higher noise. Therefore, the thinner the slices, the better quality in terms of anatomical detail, but this is accompanied by

increased noise.

To evaluate something small and specific = thinner slices ( 0.6 mm) To evaluate more big and general structures = 1-2 mm slices

➢ Pitch: if a patient goes through the CT machine too fast, the detail can

decrease. Therefore, the choice of pitch affects both image quality and

radiation dose.

P = 1.0, x-ray beams are contiguous for adjacent

rotations

P>1.0, x-ray beams are not contiguous for adjacent rotations (gaps in the x-ray helix) P<1.0, x-ray beams overlap (a volume of tissue is irradiated more than once per scan)

If we want detailed acquisition, the patient must go through very slowly; If we want fast acquisition (emergency), we have to sacrifice some of the detail.

➢ Resolution : reconstruction filters / reconstruction algorithms to increase

resolution

2. Noise (ALARA principle)

Factors influencing noise

➢ Slice thickness and reconstruction filters

➢ Body size: the noise level doubles every 8cm of increase in the patient’s

diameter.

➢ kV and mAs: noise can be modified by increasing or decreasing radiation.

      - The **higher the radiation** dose, the **less noise** and better image

quality (when dose is doubled, the noise is reduced to a factor of ¼ ) - The lower the radiation dose, the higher the noise.

[PDF p. 31]

3. Artifacts: can interfere with X-ray absorption and affect image quality.

- Motion, breathing,… In this image, the data needed to reconstruct each image has changed position due to patient

movement.

- Metals: the presence of metals such as gold produces streak artifacts, as metals absorb radiation almost completely.

RADIATION DOSE

The radiation dose has to be carefully evaluated. The younger the patient, the more susceptible to amounts of radiation.

We need to use the least amount of radiation possible. This is done by following the ALARA principle, taking into account Kv

and mAs.

The effects of radiation the patient receives depend on:

  • Absorbed radiation (determined by kV, mAs and pitch)
  • The anatomical region that has been examined (effective dose): each organ has a characteristic factor, which indicates the probability of malignant radiation effects.

POST PROCESSING

● Multiplanar reconstruction (MPR)

  • MIP and MinIP (max and min intensity projection)

Only the pixels of maximum or minimum intensity in the reconstructed thickness are shown in the image. MIP is useful for evaluating vessels, and minIP to evaluate lung parenchyma and airway.

● Volume rendering technique

It can be applied to obtain images in colour and evaluate airways, organs in the abdomen, bones,…

  • Spectral imaging can also be used to quantify more accurately the different

structures.

● Cinematic rendering

[PDF p. 32]

[PDF p. 33]

Radiobiology and contrast media

INTRODUCTION

Radiological examinations present bones as white (as opposed to US, where they are black). They can only distinguish four basic densities:

  • Air

  • Fat

  • Water

  • Calcium

Organs of different attenuation can be easily distinguished. However, structures of similar density cannot be distinguished. Contrast media makes it possible to establish differences between neighboring structures and enhance and differentiate organs.

Indications for contrast media

● Lesion detection and characterization: characterize cancer and lesions (malignant or benign), depending on how much contrast is taken up. If it’s malignant, it will clearly light up as it’s very vascularized and metastasizing, and therefore takes up much of the injected contrast.

  • Tumor staging : It can serve to detect metastasis of the tumor all over the body, and determine the stage in which it’s found.

  • Response to treatment: we can also detect if chemotherapy has worked, if the tumor is dead and no longer takes up contrast.

  • Evaluation of vascularization: We can also use contrast to enhance specific structures, mainly vessels and vascular structures (detect pulmonary embolism, rupture,…)

● Special studies (urography, arthrography, perfusion,…). 90% of studies are done with contrast injection.

CONTRAST MEDIA FEATURES

  • They must be safe and completely eliminated from the body after injection
  • Contrast media take advantage of the absorption, transport and elimination pathways of physiological substances to be distributed throughout the body to produce the image
  • The duration of a contrast medium in the body should be proportional to the type of study and examination to be performed
  • Contrast media can be positive (they provide a hyperdense image with respect to the adjacent tissue) or negative (they provide a lower density image)

HOW is CONTRAST MEDIA ADMINISTERED?

Depending on what we need to study, we will need to travel faster or slower. Each product should be administered as approved.

  • Intravenous : For fast distribution throughout the body. It’s injected through a vein and travels throughout the body. Can also be introduced in other ways to observe specific structures.

[PDF p. 34]

- Intra-arterial

  • Oral: enhances stomach, bowel, rectum,…

- Intra-articular

CONTRAST MEDIA IN RADIOLOGY

Used in X-ray and CT, and in Ultrasound and MRIs to a lesser extent. Using radiographic techniques, we only have 4 densities. Therefore we use contrast.

RADIOGRAPHIC CONTRAST AGENTS (CT scans and X-ray)

Contrast can be either positive or negative:

  • Positive contrasts:

    - They are **metals**

- Classification:

    • iodinated (water-soluble or oil based)

    • Non iodinated. For ex, barium sulfate

    They are **opaque to X-rays,** thus causing increased

attenuation in radiological examinations: enhance the image

and are seen as white

  • Negative contrasts:

    - There are usually **inert gasses** _(such as air, CO2,...)_ which

don’t have x-ray absorption and are seen as black. - These neg contrasts (ex: air ) are used for digestive

examinations

    Why? Because they allow the walls of the digestive tract to

distend, and separate the folds of mucous membranes to

detect alterations.

Barium sulfate → conventional X-ray

Water-soluble iodine → CT

➢ Barium sulfate. X-rays

    - It is used in examinations of the **digestive tract**

    It is a salt that is not absorbed and does not alter the physiology of

intestinal transit

    - It is administered in the form of a **porridge or suspension**
    - Administered **orally or rectally,** depending on which segment of the

GI tract is to be studied

    - It is **mixed** with **water and flocculants** until it has an optimal

viscosity to be distributed through the mucosa, to be able to detect

wall lesions.

➢ Water-soluble iodinated agents. CT

In this image, we are using water-soluble iodinated radicals in CT, which allow us to see the aorta and the liver, along with structures within the liver: we can detect nodules (metastasis) within the liver and

assess liver size.

[PDF p. 35]

Iodine properties

  • The basis of the molecular structure is a benzene ring, to which up to 3

iodine atoms can be attached

  • They make the molecule opaque = it increases the structure’s attenuation = whiter (as a reminder: attenuation is the reduction of the intensity of an x-ray beam as it traverses matter. The reduction is caused by absorption)

The higher the iodine, higher opacity = higher the enhancement of the tissue.

  • Compounds attached to C1, C3 and C5 of the contrast molecule modify its properties such as solubility, osmolarity and viscosity
  • Its absorption occurs in seconds .

Then, it will be excreted. Depending on the radicals found in position R5, it will be excreted in one way or another:

- **Organic compound** = through the **kidneys**

- **H atom** = through **bile**
  • Iodine can be cleared through the kidneys. If they are not working properly, the contrast will remain within the body, and this can be detected on the CT. If we wait some time, we can assess how the

kidneys are excreting the contrast.

Pharmacokinetics of iodinated contrast media

  • Injected intravenously, they bind to plasma albumin and are distributed throughout the body following the circulatory system

They reach the peak of plasma concentration within seconds of their injection

Contrast media diffuse into the extravascular space, reaching the interstitial space through the pores of the endothelium

  • They do not enter cells (except hepatocytes) and have a 2-hour half life
  • Elimination : as we mentioned, will occur though one secretion or another depending

on radical 5

- Unchanged via the **kidney** (by glomerular filtration). At 24 h after

administration, 90% of the administered dose has been eliminated

- To a lesser extent **biliary excretion**, which increases if there is renal failure

Serves to:

  • Observe breast secretions

  • Directly assess the vasculature of an organ (angiography) : we can insert a catheter and directly inject contrast in the area of interest (in liver, brain,..)

  • It can be injected to see the distribution

ULTRASOUND CONTRAST AGENTS

  • The agents used are substances based on gas microbubbles, which interact with

US waves

  • Products:
- Sulfur hexafluoride - the most widely used

- Octafluoropropane
  • The differentiation between the source of echoes from the bubbles

and the source of echoes from the tissues is the basis of the contrast

image. This requires specific software

  • Mainly employed in cardiac assessment. Contrast is used to better see the cavity of the heart and the heart walls.

[PDF p. 36]

MRI CONTRAST AGENTS

Classification of contrast media in MRI

  • Effect on signal intensity

    - **Positive** (increase it)

Negative (decrease it)

  • Behavior in the magnetic field

    **Paramagnetic: Gadolinium (Gd),** Manganese (Mn)

Superparamagnetic: iron oxide

  • Mechanism and biodistribution

- Extracellular: Gd chelates

    Intracellular: mangafodipir trisodium (Mn-DPDP)

    Mixed distribution: gadoxetate disodium(Gd-EOB-DTPA), gadobenate

dimeglumine (Gd-BOPTA)

    Intravascular: gadofosveset trisodium

Contrast in MRI. Gadolinium (Gd) Similar to iodine in CT. We use gadolinium to see contrast uptake by structures in MRI . The MRI system is a big magnet, and when we introduce gadolinium, it alters the magnetic field. Gadolinium itself is a toxic metal, so it has to be bound to molecules to reduce its toxicity (chelators).

  • Free gadolinium in plasma is toxic . It is necessary to bind it to other molecules (chelators) to reduce its toxicity

It causes signal intensity increase in T1-weighted sequences and signal intensity loss in T2-weighted sequences

The difference between the molecules of different gadolinium chelates lies in:

- Its **structure** _(determines chemical stability and ability to release Gd ions)_

- **Charge** _(Ionic: improves the stability of the molecule; Non ionic)_

ADVERSE REACTIONS TO CONTRAST MEDIA

They are very safe drugs, but can have adverse reactions due to the person’s immune system. These reactions are usually mild, but in some cases may be severe. Prevalence of adverse reactions: up to 2.5 %

The risk to Gd contrast agents is significantly lower than for iodinated contrast agents

Mechanisms of adverse reactions

  • Primary - allergic or anaphylactoid reactions:

- Unpredictable - By the action of contrast on cells of the immune system that release mediators such as histamine and immunoglobulins

    **Not dependent on the dose.** Just a drop can cause an allergic reaction _(mild_

or severe consequences, such as cardiac and resp arrest and convulsions)

    **Not dependent on iodine concentration.** Management is complex

● Secondary - Chemotoxic

    - Can be **predictable**, and effects can be minimized
    - Due to direct cellular toxicity. By action of the contrast on **cells of the**

organism, for example, the kidney

- Dose dependent

[PDF p. 37]

- Nephrotoxicity: the contrast is cleared through the kidneys, so if we inject too much contrast, it can damage the kidneys (nephrotoxic).

If a person has poor kidney function, the patient could suffer from acute renal failure. Contrast agents should not be used in patients with a history of kidney dysfunction. Oral administration can be done as the contrast will not be absorbed and pass through the body.

RADIOBIOLOGY

Ionizing radiation is a type of radiation with high enough energy to ionize matter, extracting electrons. Can come from natural sources or be created artificially (X-rays). Radiobiology studies the interaction of this radiation with matter and its effects.

Radiological magnitudes and units

Traditional system: röntgen, rad, rem

  • International system units: Coulomb/kg, Gray (Gy), Sievert (Sv). International agreement, created with the purpose of structuring a system as universal and as suitable as possible for the different measurements

Absorbed dose: measured in in Gray (Gy) Equivalent dose: Sieverts (Sv)

Effective dose: measured in Sv or mSv.

Natural background radiation

It varies depending on the area we live in. We will receive more or less radiation. The average person in the US receives an effective dose of 3 mSv per year from natural

radiation.

Artificial radiation

20% of the total radiation we receive comes from artificial radiation

(radiological examinations, nuclear escapes,…). Nowadays, the most important source of artificial radiation (produced by humans) is medical diagnostic equipment. Each radiological imaging technique requires more or less radiation

dose.

[PDF p. 38]

Since being discovered, it has been observed that X-rays (ionizing radiation) can produce harmful effects on health. Ionizing radiation has many beneficial applications, but can have harmful effects.

Biological e�ects of radiation

Biological effects derive from radiation damage to DNA. DNA contains all the information for growth and control, and is transmitted from cell to cell during replication. Damage to it will

therefore also be transmitted.

Radiation can directly affect DNA, inciding on it and breaking it, or indirectly.

- Direct: effect on DNA molecules (target molecule) - Indirect: effect on other molecules that form free radicals (ionization of H 2 O=HO+H) The main action of radiation on the human body (80% composed of water) is considered

to be indirect .

Effects according to the affected cells:

  • Somatic

Hereditary (coming from damage to reproductive cells)

Effects according to the cause-effect relationship:

  • Stochastic (probabilistic)

  • Deterministic

Deterministic e�ects

They are produced by the death of many cells in a tissue or organ.

  • The severity of the effect

increases with radiation dose.

  • There is a threshold dose for the

effect to occur

  • Deterministic effects occur after

exposure to relatively high doses

of radiation.

Acute radiation syndrome Periods of the syndrome: prodromal (nausea, vomiting, diarrhea) → Latency (days-weeks)

→ Overt disease

Stochastic e�ects

  • Consequence of sublethal damage (chromosomal mutation) in one / few cells:

    • Germ cells: inherited genetic disorders
    • Somatic cells: development of a cancer

  • The probability of their occurrence, but not the severity, increases with dose

Severity doesn’t depend on the dose. Just one disruption of DNA is inherited by dividing cells and the damage propagates. Therefore, children are more prone to stochastic effects, as their cells are continuously proliferating and growing. Tumor arises. Theoretically, a child with cancer who has to be diagnosed through CT and radiological systems, after the initial cancer disappears, can then manifest cancer in adulthood due to this stochastic effect (mutations produced and accumulated during rutinary cancer check ups during the childhood cancer).

[PDF p. 39]

  • There is no threshold for stochastic effects. Can occur by just one disruption
  • Stochastic effects occur after exposure to moderate-low doses.
  • The main somatic stochastic effect induced by ionizing radiation is the increase in

the incidence of cancer

Special caution should be taken in children and women at child-bearing age.

[PDF p. 40]

Nuclear medicine. PET I

“Nuclear medicine is a medical imaging specialty that uses small amounts of radioactive material ( radiopharmaceutical ) to diagnose or to treat many diseases”

Radiology is destined to detect changes in size and anatomy; Nuclear medicine focuses on metabolic changes and receptors,

etc.

It allows detection but has the disadvantage of being less accurate. Therefore, it has to be complemented with other tests, for example a CT scan, to observe the anatomical image. PET and CT are fused to clearly define and precisely localize

the abnormal structure.

RADIOPHARMACEUTICAL

The main difference with other techniques is that, in nuclear medicine, we are using a drug and an isotope to create an image. The drug determines the biological behaviour (targets the site), and the radioisotope determines the physical characteristics and allows the detection of the drug (acts as a flashlight) . We detect the emission of radioactivity.

  • Radioisotopes: Isotopes can be stable, unstable or radioisotopes: their nuclei emit energy in the form of ionizing radiation while searching for a more stable configuration.

SOURCE OF RADIATION

In radiology (X-rays), radiation has its origin in the atomic shell due to the deceleration of high velocity electrons when they collide and interact with target material.

In nuclear medicine, the radiation comes from radioactivity, from the disintegration of the nucleus accompanied by the emission of radiation, as opposed to X-rays.

KINDS OF IONIZING RADIATION

Alpha radiation

  • Beta radiation

  • Gamma radiation

[PDF p. 41]

If it were necessary to detect a spinal lesion, we would select a radiopharmaceutical which emits gamma, due to its greater penetration. Any high amount of these radiations can be potentially dangerous for the patient, causing irreversible DNA damage. Alpha-radiation can be stopped by paper sheets, beta by aluminium foil and gamma by lead. We need to use an emission strong enough to penetrate the structures of interest.

Radiopharmaceuticals are used in 2 situations: treatment and diagnosis.

Therapy: alpha and beta

Diagnosis: gamma or positron-emission (beta +). Gammacamera or SPECT are both gamma emitters.

We can find 3 different types of equipment in nuclear medicine:

GammaCamera (scintigraphy)

- SPECT or SPECT/CT

- PET or PET/CT

GammaCamera and SPECT/CT are both based on

gamma emission; PET is based on positron emission.

GAMMACAMERA (SCINTIGRAPHY)

We perform a planar study, which is enough to observe any pathological uptake. To localize it better, we have to use SPECT study, which observes the patient’s 3 axes.

Example: we see a spot on the vertebrae. But depending on the location within the vertebrae, it will have one interpretation or another. Metastatic or benign. To locate it, we use SPECT or SPECT/CT (which allows the fusion of both images) to define and localize the uptake.

Usually, first we do a planar study, and if an abnormal uptake is observed, we perform a

SPECT/CT to further and better localize the

spot.

[PDF p. 42]

  • BONE SCINTIGRAPHY

Studies are usually performed to detect any osteo malignancies. The radioisotopes used are Diphosphonates (MDP)+Technetium (99m) .

Diphosphonates are absorbed by the surface of the bone in proportion to local bone vascularization and osteoblastic activity. We know there is an association between uptake and bone vascularization and remodeling; most pathological bone conditions (infectious, traumatic, neoplastic…) are associated with an increase in vascularization and bone remodeling . In this way, we will be able to detect benign or metastatic tumors.

Normal distribution

There is physiological increased uptake in joints, sternum and spine (ex: in the sacro-ileac joints, due to the continuous remodeling of this area), but also in the bladder and kidneys.

Clinical context examples:

  • THYROID SCINTIGRAPHY

For thyroid scintigraphy there is no need for a drug; we only use the radioisotope, as isotopes such as iodine incorporate naturally into the thyroid gland. This is because iodine is involved in thyroid hormonogenesis.

Radioiodine follows normal iodine metabolism in the

thyroid gland.

It is introduced in the patient and used to detect:

  • size of the gland

- localization

  • function (example: a cold node means there is reduced uptake of iodine = decreased function)

[PDF p. 43]

In addition, iodine has an interesting feature: it emits both gamma and beta particles. Therefore, it can be used for diagnosis, but also as treatment. It can be used as Thyroid cancer Therapy. The beta emission can destroy tumor cells in the metastatic regions.

  • RENAL SCINTIGRAPHY

For renal scintigraphy, we use radiopharmaceuticals such as Technetium-MAG3 and Technetium-DTPA. We can perform dynamic studies.

In dynamic studies, we acquire a series of images during a time of varying duration (from seconds to mins). Acquired data are processed and give out activity-time curves, which describe organ uptake/excretion rates.

Example of left kidney (PA view) dysfunction observed on renal scintigraphy:

We obtain images during 20-30 minutes and perform an image every 2 minutes. In the end we are left with images which reflect the dynamic distribution of the radioisotope, depending on its uptake/elimination rates.

  • MYOCARDIAL PERFUSION SCINTIGRAPHY

For this type of scintigraphy, we use radioisotope Technetium-MIBI.

With a myocardial perfusion, we can detect blocked coronary arteries which supply blood to the heart: in normal/resting conditions the heart works fine; but while performing high activity, the heart has to pump more blood, as well as increase its supply. An occlusion in coronary arteries will mean an area of the heart stops receiving irrigation during high activity.

[PDF p. 44]

To perform the test, we make the patient exercise, obtain an image, and do the same in resting position.

  • In a normal heart, the distribution is homogeneous.

  • If we see decreased uptake of the radiopharmaceutical in the scan corresponding to high activity, it means there is a block of the coronary artery in a specific area. This situation is typical of ischemia: an area of the heart doesn’t receive blood supply during exercise but is seen as normal in resting position.

  • If there is total necrosis, there is neither normal uptake in the resting images nor in the active ones. This situation is typical of an infarction.

SENTINEL NODE BIOPSY

Sentinel node is used in those oncological processes in which lymph node involvement has consequences on the prognosis of the patient (melanoma,…).

Sentinel node is the first lymph node or group of lymph nodes draining cancer cells. Sentinel node biopsy (SNB) is the identification, removal and analysis of the sentinel lymph nodes of a particular tumor.

To see if the tumor has reached the sentinel node, we inject a radiopharmaceutical and observe if it reaches axillary areas where the sentinel node is located. The radiopharmaceutical in question is

Technetium-Nanocolloidal albumin.

If the lymph node is free of tumor:

Good prognosis

No lymph node involved with tumor in the rest of the body

If the lymph node is found to be involved with the

tumor:

Bad prognosis

Is it extended enough to need a lymphadenectomy?

[PDF p. 45]

Nuclear medicine. PET II

In nuclear medicine, there are different types of radiation emitted by radioisotopes. Different applications for each:

Therapy: alpha or beta - emitters

Diagnostic: image created through beta + (PET) or gamma emitters (Gammacamera or SPECT)

RADIOPHARMACEUTICALS

Therapy: alpha or beta emitters such as iodine

Diagnosis:

  • PET (positron emission tomography): Beta + radiation emitters (release positrons). Mainly fluoride isotopes (F 18 )

Gamma camera/SPECT: gamma radiation emitters: Technetium, iodine,.. Main difference between PET and Gamma Camera /SPECT: type of radiation they base themselves on to produce a diagnostic image. Use different radioisotopes.

PET. POSITRON EMISSION TOMOGRAPHY

PET scans are based on beta + radiation:

  1. We have isotopes with an unstable nucleus
  2. They’re stabilized through disintegration and emission of positrons (electrons with a positive charge (e+)).
  3. When These positrons come across an electron, they undergo aniquilation: the mass of both is destroyed and transformed into 2 photons of

511 keV.

Tomographs: system dedicated to the detection of these high energy photons:

  • We obtain a sequential acquisition of images as the patient advances through the

device.

  • We need to do attenuation correction of the photons and quantification (SUV). Attenuation = loss of detection of true coincidence events, leading to severe artifacts in PET image. “Attenuation artifacts” are due to the reduction in the intensity / strength of the signal when it travels through body tissues of different densities (specially bones and soft tissue). Has to be corrected during image reconstruction.

More precise localization of the focal uptake can be done by combining PET and CT, for higher diagnostic precision (anatomical location) and contribution to therapy planning.

Attenuation vs Non-attenuation

images in PET.

[PDF p. 46]

PET/CT scan protocol

We can compare PET images with CT,

and fuse them.

The patient enters a CT scanner and we obtain the digital scout image and helical

CT.

Consequently, the PET image is taken. After attenuation and scatter correction, the fused image is revealed.

RADIOPHARMACEUTICAL. 18 F - FDG

Radiopharmaceutical 18F-FDG is mainly uptaken along with glucose. Its concentration measures glucose metabolism, and thus, is an indirect measurement of cell proliferation by observing glucose uptake (more proliferation = more glucose uptake)

Neoplastic tissues (tumors) uptake more glucose as they present increased glycolysis

thanks to:

Higher GLUT-1 transporter expression

  • Increased enzymes of glycolysis such as hexokinase All induced by anaerobic conditions (HIF-1) in which they are in during their early stages. The enzymes for glycolysis incorporate FDG, and the molecule of glucose with fluoride is retained by the tumors. This can be detected through imaging due to radiation emission.

Physiological distribution

We introduce the radioisotope and wait 1h. We indicate the patient to fast prior to detection

so food doesn’t interfere.

18F-FDG physiological distribution after 1h:

  • Brain (high uptake, mainly runs on glucose)
  • Heart (more or less depending on the metabolic rate)
  • Liver (glucose metabolism → glycogen)

Kidneys and bladder (through which fluoride is excreted),..

Patient preparation

  • Non-diabetic: 6h fasting
  • Type II diabetic: 6h fasting + metformin (lowers sugar blood levels by improving the way the body handles insulin)
  • Type I diabetic: breakfast (6h) + insulin to allow uptake

Hydration: the day before and on the day of the PET scan. This increases micturition, ensuring 18F-FDG won’t concentrate in the urine.

Room temperature

  • Immobilization and correct positioning, avoids fusion errors

[PDF p. 47]

Importance of blood glucose levels: If the glucose levels are elevated, the level of insulin will increase. When insulin levels increase, our muscles tend to “suck up” glucose, and the radioactive tracer won’t go to the areas of interest. Measure glucose levels before test.

High glucose availability, high insulin → muscle uptake

Most of the 18F-FDG is trapped at the muscles, and there is less availability for the rest of the tissues. We can underestimate the real distribution and diagnosis is

altered.

Importance of room temperature: if it’s too cold, brown fat will enter thermogenesis to increase body heat. Brown fat will increase glucose and therefore FDG uptake = abnormal distribution of the radioisotope

Physiological and NOT pathological uptake

  • Insulin : high insulin will cause the muscles to uptake and retain the radiopharmaceutical. Insulin must be measured as to not diagnose a false positive

  • Cough: respiratory tract presents more uptake due to possible inflammation of this area. In addition, there is high uptake from the muscles in the abdomen, as they contract when we cough. The uptake is not pathological, but physiological .

  • Inflammation : inflammatory sites, same as cancer, are sites with high activity and cell proliferation (lymphocytes, immune system, etc). The abnormal glucose uptake must not be mistaken with

cancer.

Example: FDG bowel uptake during infections:

    Colonoscopy colitis: inflammation = increased uptake

(acquired during colonoscopy) - Infectious colitis: increased uptake by the bowel

  • Previous surgery (laparotomy): cells in the scar have a very high uptake of FDG.

● Previous surgery (laparoscopy): same situation. High uptake by the incision site.

[PDF p. 48]

● Anatomical variations:

    Pelvic kidney

    Horseshoe kidney (it’s not the bowel, but the kidney). _In this particular image,_

we can also observe a lung nodule, which, if it uptakes more than a threshold value, has more risk of being a pathological tumor. These are not pathological situations, but normal anatomical variations.

● Breastfeeding: increased breast uptake due to breastfeeding

Acquisition protocols

We also have many different adquisition protocols:

Head and neck protocol: we perform head and neck PET

- ARMS down

Body protocol: we perform an abdominal PET

- ARMS up. In this case the patient has adenopathies. The PET was performed to identify the primary tumor, which was found to be in the abdomen and with

metastasis in head and neck

Whole body PET (multiple myeloma. Mass in the scalp. We can see the uptake in the bone marrow).

    Base of the skull to mid-thigh

    Head and neck protocol

When we see many focal uptakes in the patient, they can be physiological, pathological,… so we have to perform further tests to confirm its pathological.

Anterior-posterior projection (bone metastasis)

  • Posterior-anterior (masses in the breasts)

Example: In the patient on the left, we see an inflammatory pathology: increased uptake in the lymph nodes. However, in the patient on the right, there has been lymph node metastasis from a mass in the lung. It has metastasized to lymph nodes in the mediastinum.

[PDF p. 49]

LUNG CANCER

Lymph node: size and morphology are normal, but PET can detect tumoral cells due to the abnormal glucose uptake. We are able to detect the disease earlier than other techniques, before there are morphological manifestations.

LUNG INFECTION

Tuberculosis patient

NECK CANCER

Lymph node in the neck presents an increased uptake, even with normal size. It’s possible to detect the tumor and adapt the therapy to the extension of the disease.

ESOPHAGEAL CANCER

Tumor in the esophagus and lymph node metastasis. It’s also visible on the

CT. Bilateral disease in this case.

IMAGE ANALYSIS

It’s also possible to quantify the amount of disease within the lesion. Semiquantitative analysis. SUV: In this patient with multiple myeloma, we can calculate the SUV (standardized uptake value) through this formula in order to perform a semi quantitative analysis. There are values to diagnose malignancy.

There are different types of tumors, and each is different.

There are malignant tumors with very high uptake, which proliferate very fast and

are less differentiated

We can also find malignant tumors with medium to low uptake.

  • There are also tumors with very very low uptake (these are tumors which are highly differentiated, meaning they are vascularized and not under anaerobic conditions = less glucose uptake, not so dependent on glycolysis). Could give a false negative depending on interpretation.

SUMMARY

  • PET isotopes used to label radiopharmaceuticals are produced in the cyclotron . PET scans give complementary information to usual morpho anatomical techniques
  • PET scans are more sensitive than other medical techniques, BUT:
