Results have to be further confirmed, as they can detect non-pathological lesions (anatomical variations or benign lesions) that could be interpreted as pathological

Very good in early cancer diagnosis, prior to symptoms and morphological changes

  • It can be used for staging cancers (SUV analysis)
  • Can be used for monitoring response to treatment. Metabolic changes can occur in the tumor even when there haven’t been morpho anatomical changes (changes in size), or we can also detect new metastasis due to increased absorption in other areas, which would lead the physician to change the treatment.

[PDF p. 50]

[PDF p. 51]

Magnetic resonance imaging (MRI)

MRI relies on the existence of a magnetic field, which is aligned with the orientation of the patient (depending on the company, aligned from head to feet or feet to head). We are able to recover 3D images of specific organs of the body, or even localize active regions of the brain of the patient. (TED TALK: human brain mapping and brain decoding)

WHAT IS A MAGNETIC FIELD?

We have a magnetic field (B), which consists in a manifestation of the presence of electrical particles in movement.

1. What is its e�ect on electric particles?

The only particles that can interact with a magnetic field are moving charged particles (+ or -), which have a velocity different than 0.

These particles will suffer from a force whose origin is the magnetic field, which will have an effect on the particle’s trajectory. This force, known as Lorentz force, appears ONLY for moving particles.

The behaviour of this force is quite peculiar: if we have an electric field, the force equals the electric field times the charge. However with magnetic fields, the Lorentz force is proportional to the charge, but also to the velocity and to the magnetic field. The velocity plays a role in the generation of the field. If v= 0, it won’t be generated. Magnetic behaviour is more complex than electric behaviour.

In the case of the magnetic field, the force will be perpendicular to the velocity and magnetic field, and its orientation will depend on the orientation of the magnetic field, the direction in which the particle travels (v) and the charge of the particle.

As the force is perpendicular to velocity, it will not provide energy to the particle. Therefore it won’t modify its kinetic energy. Its only effect is to deviate the trajectory of the particle. This is opposed to the electric field, where the force can modify Ek.

Lorentz force:

From this equation we can obtain the unit for the magnetic field, which is the Tesla (T). 1 Tesla = 10^4 Gauss (G).

[PDF p. 52]

The Earth’s magnetic field is 0.5 Gauss, quite small. There are magnetic resonance systems of over 1-1.5 Teslas. The limit value used in medical application in the EU is 3 Teslas (for clinical use). For research, they can be over these values, but not in clinical practice. 0.1 T is the typical magnetic force we will find on the surface of a potent permanent magnet.

2. How can we generate this magnetic field?

The magnetic field appears surrounding an electric charge in movement. The appearance of any magnetic field is based on the displacement of these charged moving particles.

In this example, a positive particle is moving along the Y axis with a velocity “v”. As soon as it starts moving, a magnetic field appears around the moving particle. The field has the shape of concentric circles which are found on a plane perpendicular to the direction of the particle’s velocity.

But this is in the case of a single charge.

If we take a large amount of charges and place them in a ring, each of these single particles will generate a small magnetic field. When we combine all of them, we obtain the second diagram (cross section): the final magnetic field we get for a circular ring will be pointing upwards within the surface, crossing the surface of the ring and moving outwards.

The final result is there is a region in which the magnetic field lines leave the circle (north pole) and another in which they are entering (south pole). This is a magnetic dipole (N and S), the most simple configuration of a magnetic field. North and south poles cannot be created in an isolated way. This configuration is important as at the subatomic level, we observe this behaviour.

At a subatomic level:

  • in each electron orbital, the oscillating electrons are rotating, creating a magnetic dipole.
  • the protons inside the nucleus aren’t stationary, but also rotating.

Both systems will generate their own magnetic field. Because of the displacement of these charges, there will be an interaction with an external magnetic field. Can we suppose the magnetic field has no effect on a person? NO. There is interaction when we apply a magnetic field on a patient, due to the spin of these charged particles that make up the molecules we are created by.

[PDF p. 53]

3. How does the magnetic field interact with matter?

Response of the dipole to an external magnetic field: all the dipoles will be aligned with the external magnetic field.

Green arrow: external magnetic field Two dipoles placed within this external magnetic field. Charges are leaving and penetrating the screen.

What is the effect of this external

magnetic field? In both 1st and 2nd dipole, the Lorentz forces acting on the charges are different directions, but same value. Therefore, there is no displacement. The only effect the magnetic field can have is to reorient the dipole through rotation.

There are two possibilities of reorientation:

  • Parallele orientaion, N pointing upwards, S pointing downwards, in a parallel orientation in relation to the external field (more stable configuration)
  • Antiparallel orientation in relation to the field, which is more unstable and requires additional energy.

Naturally, the magnetic field tries to align the north and south poles.This reorientation is known as magnetization.

TYPES OF MAGNETIZATION

Depending on the material, there are different types of magnetization and orientations.

  • Ferromagnetic: if we apply an external magnetic field, we induce a global magnetization. There is a significant degree of magnetization, which remains when the external field is removed: the dipoles will retain magnetization and remain oriented. Seen in materials such as iron and niquel.

  • Paramagnetic: in this case, magnetization is very weak. The dipoles in the material will align, but when the external magnetic field is removed, the magnetization disappears. The dipoles disorganize and do not remain oriented.

Seen in the case of O2 and aluminium.

  • Diamagnetic: When we apply a magnetic field, the system responds in a way that acts in the opposite way. There is a resistance to being oriented. The dipoles are found pointing in the opposite direction to the magnetic field. Observed on living

materials.

Any material interacts with magnetic fields, in one way or another. The normal interaction seen in the human body is the diamagnetic magnetization/behaviour.

[PDF p. 54]

REAL MRI (magnetic resonance imaging)

In real life, precession moment appears due to rotation: the system doesn’t follow its original trajectory. In the previous diagram: if the rotation of the particles is relevant, as is the case light particles, this rotation (omega) is not “correct”, meaning that the trajectory indicated

isn’t correct.

What would be the real effect of the magnetic force when this system is rotating?

Image shows the classical point of view of the behaviour of a proton (without spin) . Protons have a property known as spin, which is originally placed in an antiparallel way. When submitted to a magnetic field, the proton creates a magnetic field in the sense of the spin, found in an antiparallel fashion, which will try to obtain the more stable orientation by transitioning to the parallel conformation.

  • If there were no spin, the expected result would be to follow the classical trajectory

shown.

  • However, due to rotation, it will orient into the parallel direction following a helicoidal/spiral transition towards said orientation. This spiral movement is accompanied by the emission of an EM wave, generated because of the displacement of the protons when transitioning from antiparallel to parallel orientation.

[PDF p. 55]

If this were the only action of the MRI device, after being introduced into the field, all the dipoles would be oriented and nothing would happen. There is a sequence of different processes generated by the system, whih will allow us to retrive an image.

  1. Patient is introduced into the system, into the magnetic field. All the dipoles will be pointing in a parallel way to the magnetic field.

  2. Excitation . We will start applying a sequence of stimulus: first, we will send energy in the form of an EM wave, which has an energy of the magnitude of a radiowave/microwave (low frequency). The protons will absorb the energy and reorient in the opposite, unstable configuration due to excitation.

  3. We then let the system relax. When retrieving the stimulus, the system will tend to return to its stable configuration. This is done through that spiral/helicoidal transition mentioned earlier, which is accompanied by the emission of EM waves that are detected by the system. This sequence is carried out repeatedly, which is what gives out the MRI image

based on the emision.

The spiral movement generates an EM wave with a frequency that depends on the electric dipole. This frequency is different for the different protons found in specific substances in the body.

  • It depends on the type of particle. Each particle will have its own specific magnetic dipole.
  • Also depends on the local value of the magnetic field. Depending on what the surrounding molecules are, the local magnetic field will be different.

MRI mainly measures protons (hydrogen, H+) and their dipole’s emission. Therefore, the most important echo will come from water, a very abundant molecule inside the human body. Other techniques can measure other nuclei, but only those with an odd number of nucleons (for example, O16 will not be detected by MRI as it won’t interact with the EM field, due to lack of spin).

[PDF p. 56]

COMPONENTS OF DIPOLE BEHAVIOUR

We can split the behaviour of the dipole into 2 parts:

  • Longitudial magnetization : the part that is parallel to the external magnetic field is known as the longitudinal magnetization. Longitudinal magnetization increases in time (asintotical increase). T1 corresponds to the time by which 0.67 of the spin has returned to the longitudinal magnetization value.

  • Transverse magnetization: the behaviour of the component perpendicular to the external field is known as the transverse magnetization. For the transverse movement, there is a decrease of magnetization in time. T2 corresponds to the time you have to wait for the 0.37 transverse component to have transitioned nto the parallel orientation.

On an MRI device, we measure T1 and T2. Depending on the material, T1 and T2 will be different.

  • They can be very large, as is the case of water (meaning it takes a long time to rearrange the dipoles).
  • However, in solids this reorientation is done really fast, meaning T1 and T2 are very low. Due to many studies, we can determine which tissue is being detected depending on the obtained T1 and T2 for each region, and therefore, this will allow us to classify the structures in the image.

SAFETY CONCERNS MRI

  • There is a maximum magnetic field a person can be subjected to. The max is 40 mT. Only in specific situations can this value be increased.

  • We have to be aware of metallic components, as this can imply mechanical and

thermal issues.

  • The key point for the device to work is that we absorb EM waves. This means that the temperature can increase. There is a magnitude defined, known as SAR (P/M) (represents the energy absorbed by the body of the patient. Measures the ability to transform this energy received into thermal energy )

[PDF p. 57]

MRI basics

WHAT IS MRI?

MRI is an imaging method based on the nuclear resonance phenomenon. It obtains sectional images, in any plane, using a very strong magnetic field, gradients and radiofrequency pulses. The machine detector is similar to CT, but longer and more tube-like.

Advantages:

  • It doesn’t use ionizing radiation. Therefore, there is no cancer risk.

It can obtain anatomical, functional and other information.

  • It achieves very high contrast between different tissues, while in CT, as it is based on density, when tissues with similar density are close together they’re difficult to differentiate.

ELEMENTS

  • Magnet

The magnet creates a magnetic field. Its power is measured in Teslas (T, in IS) or Gauss. 1 T = 10000 G. Nowadays, the vast majority of equipment is 1.5 - 3 T. The magnet never turns off : whenever we enter the room, we must chek we’re not wearing ferromagnetic material.

According to quantum mechanics, each element of an atom has an angular momentum called “spin.” For us to understand it, it is usually compared to a sphere rotating on an axis. Every electric charge in motion (in this case spinning ) causes a magnetic field.

MRI is based on hydrogen atoms, as they interact with the magnetic field (uneven number of protons) and there are many H atoms in our body (found in water).

Function of the magnet:

  • Under normal conditions, each atom is oriented randomly in parallel or antiparallel ways and the total magnetic moment is 0.

  • Under a very strong magnetic field (referred as B 0 ), the hydrogen atoms will align themselves in a parallel or antiparallel direction to the field (this will only affect atoms with an uneven number of protons, as they will have an electrical charge).

There are always slightly more atoms in parallel, making the neat magnetic moment (M) is different from 0. This magnetic moment is what we will modify in order to obtain a signal and create an image

[PDF p. 58]

We send a low frequency wave to induce the antiparallel state in all of the dipoles. Once the excitatory stimulus is removed, the particle will tend to return to its stable state. This is done through the process of precession (spiral/helicoidal transition). It precesses at a constant angular velocity (omega) which is known as Larmor frequency.

This Larmor frequency depends on the constant γ, which is specific for each atom; and depends on B, the static magnetic field. Each material = specific Larmor frequency

Radiofrequency (B1)

When additional energy in the form of a radio wav e is added to the magnetic field, the magnetic vector (M) is deflected. The radio wave frequency/ radiofrequency (RF) that causes the hydrogen nuclei to resonate is dependent on the element sought (hydrogen the case of MRI) and the strength of the magnetic field (B = Larmor’s frequency).

Due to the phenomenon of magnetic resonance, RF is “absorbed” by the hydrogen atom, altering its state to another of higher energy, flipping the M angle. When the RF stops, the atoms return to their initial state, emitting that extra energy as RF that provides us the signal for the image.

Initially, there is no magnetic moment in the transverse (Y) plane (magnetization is purely longitudinal). After radiofrequency is applied, we modify the spin, finding components in the Z axis and in the transverse plane. After a certain time, the spins end up being all oriented in the transverse plane (magnetization is purely transverse). But when we retrieve the radiofrequency, the atoms return their spin to the initial orientation following a spiral transition (precession), through the emission of a RF wave.

When we stop radiofrequency (precession), T1 relaxation and T2 relaxation both occur at

the same time

[PDF p. 59]

During precession:

  • T1 or longitudinal relaxation: during precession, we are gaining momentum in the Z axis, which is measured by T1. T1 relaxation is the process by which the net magnetization (M) grows/returns to its initial maximum value (M0), parallel to B0. T1 = can be viewed as the time required for the z component of M to reach 63% of its

maximum value.

During this process, there is transfer of energy from the spins to nearby molecules, while they go back to the Z axis.

  • T2 or transverse relaxation: during precession, components in the transverse plane lose momentum and decrease until reaching 0, measured by T2. Therefore, T2 relaxation is the process by which the transverse components of magnetization (Mxy) decay or dephase. T2= i s defined as the time required for the transverse magnetization to fall to approximately 37% (1/e) of its initial value. Spins dephase and begin to desynchronize, losing overall signal.

Each tissue, depending on its composition, has different T1 and T2 relaxation times, which have been previously studied and identified. This allows us to classify tissues and create an image. Depending on the sequence used, a signal from the same tissue changes radically. We can attenuate the signal of a tissue to obtain the image of interest.

  • Gradients

Gradients generate slight alterations in the homogeneous magnetic field, making the magnetic field intensity at each point slightly different. In other words, it will make the waves/energy coming back from each point slightly different. This will allow the machine to locate in the space where the signal comes from, and therefore identify and recreate the structure in the 3D space into an image.

[PDF p. 60]

  • Antenas or coils

For optimal image quality, the antenna/coil that receives the signal should be as close as possible to the area to be studied. For this reason, there are normally multiple antennas, each designed to study a specific area. The correct use of the antennas and the positioning of the patient in them is a determining part in the final quality of the image.

THE SEQUENCES (“SOFTWARE”)

Each tissue behaves differently according to its composition and according to its excitation and relaxation parameters. We can program “sequences” where we adjust different parameters to “enhance” specific aspects of the image/tissues. Main sequence parameters (TR, TE, TI, slice thickness, flip angle,…; no need to know them) can be modified to obtain more signal from one tissue or another.

Each pixel is assigned a shade of gray based on the intensity of the received signal. However, the signal intensity depends on the tissue, the equipment and the sequence used. Therefore, ulike the HU, its value is variable : there is no absolute value for each tissue, as the signal intensity can be modified depending on the tissue, the energy of the incident wave and the selected sequence.

● Tissue factors:

    - H1 concentration

    - Relaxation time T1

    - Relaxation time T2

    Dynamic state

● Operational factors:

    - SEQUENCES: TR, TE,

matrix, FoV, flip angle,…

    - Number of excitations

[PDF p. 61]

ACQUISITION

  • It usually takes about 20 mins-half an hour to acquire the different sequences. But you adapt to the time you have.
  • Antennas are very important in acquisition to study the area of interest. IV contrast.
  • Movement artifact! Claustrophobia . We can sometimes opt to sedate the patient (claustrophobic, patients with dementia,…) to obtain the image.

SAFETY

Very safe technique. It does not use ionizing radiation.

Can be performed during pregnancy

  • It has no known effect on the tissues of the body. No cases have been described that show that static magnetic fields produce alterations on the structure of DNA, reproduction and/or cell growth.
  • The main risk is related to the presence of external metallic/electronic materials, which can produce the projectile effect, interaction with metal components present.

MRI SAFETY LABELLING

The main risk derived from the static magnetic field is due to its interaction with ferromagnetic materials (iron, nickel and cobalt) and the consequent “projectile effect”. This also applies to patients with ferromagnetic foreign bodies or transplants.

The production of heat due to RF absorption is also a potential risk. It can cause burns on the patient. Levels are controlled by SAR limits. SAR levels are estimates of the amount of energy absorbed by each region of the body, and vary significantly depending on the patient and the equipment.

[PDF p. 62]

[PDF p. 63]

Ultrasound waves

MECHANICAL WAVES

A mechanical wave is any wave that can produce a deformation of a material through

contact forces.

The result will be a perturbation on/in the material: some of the atoms will be moved from the previous position to a new position. In the case of material deformation, the concept of

stress is introduced.

Stress is the force that acts per unit of area on the material. It indicates how intense the force acting on each atom will be.

  • When the force is applied perpendicularly, it is said to be normal stress.
  • When the force acts in parallel to the surface, it is said to be shear stress.
  • When the stress acts to shorten bonds, it’s a compressive stress.
  • When it acts to lengthen bonds, it’s a tensile stress.

If stresses are small, the deformation produced is proportional to the stress and is also reversible. In this case, the process is said to be an elastic process. In contrast, if the stresses are very large, they can end up producing permanent deformation or fracture.

TYPES OF MECHANICAL WAVES

  • Transverse waves: as a result of perturbations in the X or Y axis. Chemical bonds between all the atoms are displaced and the oscillation occurs in a perpendicular direction to the direction in which the wave propagates/advances. Therefore, transverse mechanical waves are shear waves (transversal): the atoms involved move in a direction that is perpendicular to wave propagation.

  • Longitudinal waves: as a result of a perturbation in the Z axis. The oscillation occurs in the direction of the atom chain, one after the other. In other words, in the

same direction in which the wave advances.

Longitudinal mechanical waves are therefore waves of compression and tension ( normal): the atoms involved move in the same direction as the propagation of the

wave.

[PDF p. 64]

The state of matter affects the type of wave that can exist. In fluids it’s impossible to have shear stress, only pressure. In solids, all range of possibilities:

  • In the solid state there can be longitudinal and transverse mechanical waves.

  • In the fluid state (liquid or gas), ONLY longitudinal waves are possible. A fluid cannot produce a deformation to counteract the action of shear stress: the elements of a fluid are simply displaced when subjected to shear stresses. As they are displaced, they rub against other elements in the fluid, and thus energy that is injected in a transverse manner is rapidly dissipated in heat. In this case, in which only normal stresses are possible, waves are referred to as pressure waves (act by pushing and pulling). The force that produces a pressure is always perpendicular to the area of the system, as it’s a normal stress.

Equation which characterizes a wave

  • Ambient/Atmos pressure usually corresponds to 1 atm (know the conversion to cm H2o and mmHg)

p0 corresponds to the altitude of the wave (Amplitude)

The absolute pressure will always be larger than 0 (p>0)

  • ω = angular frequency

From here, the velocity of the wave is defined as:

What is negative pressure? It makes reference to the behaviour compared to ambient pressure. Negative pressures are those pressures below ambient pressure. It has an absolute value, not negative.

[PDF p. 65]

WAVE CATEGORIES ACCORDING TO FREQUENCIES

Mechanical waves are categorized into three groups according to their frequency:

  • Infrasound: mechanical waves where the frequency < 20 Hz

  • Sound: waves with frequencies between 20 Hz< f < 20 KHz (max a human can hear)

  • Ultrasounds: mechanical waves with frequency > 20 KHz

Because propagation velocity depends on the elastic properties of the material involved, it will have different values in different types of material.

  • Inside the human body (water), they travel at 1500 m/s
  • In air, they travel at 340 m/s

MEDICAL ULTRASOUND

Ultrasound is used in medicine in diagnosis and treatment. Equipment that uses ultrasound for diagnosis is based on very different principles to other wave-based diagnostic equipment, such as X-ray machines. In the case of ultrasound, it is the wave reflected by the interior of the body that is analyzed; in contrast, with ionizing radiation, it is the transmitted wave that is analyzed. Medical devices work with waves travelling at 1-20 MHz (landa = 1.55 mm)

An example of treatment by ultrasound is lithotripsy, in which ultrasound waves generate pressure waves and shear waves in kidney stones, breaking them into smaller parts that can be eliminated naturally. Another example is high-intensity focused ultrasound (HIFU), which makes it possible to destroy certain brain cells responsible for trembling in

Parkinson’s disease.

[PDF p. 66]

EFFECT OF ACOUSTIC IMPEDANCE

Let’s suppose that we have a flat ultrasound wave propagating through medium A, and it arrives at a different medium B. These two mediums are separated by an interface. Given the density and elastic properties of the medium (Z), we might find that it is very difficult to generate a displacement of atoms, even if the incident pressure is very high. Such reasoning suggests that if medium A is air and medium B is water, which has a density about 1000 times greater than air, it should be difficult to transmit a sound wave from the air into the water (This can be experienced in a swimming pool: when we put our heads below the water, external sounds are greatly attenuated).

More rigorously: there is a series of magnitudes that are conserved on either side of the interface. There must be continuity in velocity and pressure, and intensity must be conserved. From these relationships, it is found that, if a pressure wave pi is incident from medium A to medium B, then the reflected wave p R and the transmitted wave p T are obtained by:

Where zA and zB are the impedances of each medium.

Z is constant and specific to each material, known as Acoustic impedance . It reflects the resistance of a material to being displaced. The acoustic impedance depends on the number of atoms in a region (density) and the elastic behaviour of a material (which is reflected by the velocity of sound within it).

z = density · velocity of sound in the material

From these equations, we find that the energy (the intensity) is conserved. That is, the incident intensity is divided between the reflected wave IR and transmitted wave IT.

To sum up, we can conclude that at any interface between two materials, there will be a reflected wave and a transmitted wave. The relation between incoming pressure wave and reflected wave will depend on the impedances of both materials. There are two limiting situation s:

  • If the impedances of the two mediums are similar (zA and zB), there is practically no reflected wave, and 100% is transmitted.

  • If there are very different impedances, practically 100% of the energy is reflected, and almost nothing is transmitted

[PDF p. 67]

When a pressure wave (constant) arrives at a material:

  • If the impedance is high, the velocity will be quite low.
  • If the impedance is low, the resulting velocity will be high.

* Remember: all these waves propagate energy. The more atoms we have to displace, the smaller the displacement.

ULTRASOUND IMAGING

How and why is an ultrasound wave reflected? How are the different shades of grey generated in the image?

The ultrasound is generated by a device that contains a piezoelectric crystal. This crystal, when subjected to a voltage, oscillates and transmits its vibration to the medium around it.

This device is called a transducer and it acts both as a source and a receiver for waves.

The transducer generates and emits the US wave. This wave is generated in air (very small impedance) and impacts on the human body (high impedance). To avoid there being no transmission ( which would mean the wave wouldn’t reach the rest of the tissues inside the body), the wave is generated inside a material whose impedance is very close to the impedance of the human body. For example, a gel. In this way almost all of the energy is transmitted and nothing is reflected, so the ultrasound gets into the body.

The wave propagates through the body and it will encounter different surfaces on different organs. At each surface, part of the wave will be reflected and detected by the machine. The intensity of the reflected part of the wave will depend on the impedance values. At the extremes of the range of possibilities are bones and large cavities full of air. where the impedance is very different from that of the rest of the body, and so a mechanical wave will find it difficult to penetrate them.

The reflected wave will go back towards the transducer, where the signal is registered. By measuring the journey time ∆trip (from when the wave was generated until the echo – the reflected wave - is received), it is possible

to determine the distance at which the

surface is located.

This is the operating principle behind echography by ultrasound: by sweeping through different directions and collecting the various echoes from each surface, it is possible to construct an image that represents, in gray-scale, the intensity of the reflected wave .

  • White/bright lines are observed when there is big echo; maximum intensity

  • Dark/black lines are observed when there is no or almost no echo

[PDF p. 68]

Echoes and gray-scale

A material that does not generate an echo is said to be an-echoic or non-echogenic. In contrast, materials that do generate an echo are said to be echogenic.

Inhomogeneous materials generate more echo. For this reason, bones result in brighter images.

  • Fluids and air-filled cavities don’t generate any acho, and appear black in the echography image.

Ultrasound studies and frequency

- Low frequency: used in studies at depth, in which great detail is not necessary. - High frequency: used for surface or near surface studies, in which greater image detail is required.

DOPPLER EFFECT

Ultrasound can be used to measure the velocity of blood. This is based on the modification of the frequency of a wave by a body in movement: the Doppler effect. It’s used to study the reflected wave coming from a moving body.

Doppler effect :

When a stopped ambulance generates a sound, it propagates in the same way in all directions (same wavelengths)

However, when velocity is different from 0, the result is that the wavelengths are clustered in front of the ambulance and separated in the back part. The wavelength length in front is lower than that at the back. Therefore, people hearing the ambulance when it approaches them hear it with a higher pitch than those watching it while it moves away.

Applied to medicine: RBC reflect waves, producing this doppler effect. This higher frequency can be detected by the machine. We can calculate the velocity of a RBC by paying attention to the frequency of the reflected wave.

Inside the human body, the frequency f R of a wave reflected by an erythrocyte will be:

Where f0 is the original frequency of the ultrasound wave, v ult is the component of velocity deriving from the erythrocyte along the ray followed by the ultrasound waves, and c is the velocity of sound in the body.

[PDF p. 69]

Doppler displacement

Doppler displacement/shift can be observed due to the effect of the angle

formed between the transducer and the vessel.

Within the same vessel, when using ultrasound, we can measure the velocity of erythrocytes in different ways. According to the angle formed between the transducer and the vessel, we will find a Doppler shift that is either positive (bottom) or negative (top), depending on v ult : whether the erythrocytes are approaching or moving away from the transducer.

Approaching: f > fo (+ shift, increased value)

Moving away: f < fo ( - shift, decreased value)

ULTRASOUND - SAFETY CONCERNS

The energy which is being transported by mechanical waves is absorbed by the human body.

  • Thermal index (TI): reflects that there is an energy being absorbed by the system, and thus, increasing its temperature. If it’s being absorbed by a specific tissue, the temperature will increase in said tissue. There is a limit temperature. The TI reflects the power absorbed in a specific tissue compared to the power needed to increase 1ºC the tissue of that specific organ. This ratio has to be smaller than one. By using this limit, we can be sure no dangerous increase in temperature will be produced.

  • Mechanical index (MI): The pressure of the US will oscillate around an ambient pressure. Is there any limit to pressure values? In the case of the maximum, we have already seen that we can cause “damage” or heat material if the pressure is very high. It turns out that there is also a risk of damage if the minimum pressure goes too

low.

If the oscillation minimum falls below the vapour pressure, we are going to produce something that will locally increase the pressure (bubbles). There will be a spontaneous transition from the vapour to the liquid phase when the pressure falls below P vapour. This process is called cavitation, the generation of a vapour phase in a liquid due to the fall in pressure.

The mechanical index is defined as:

It’s proportional to the peak negative pressure (p0) and therefore, to local pressure p. MI has to be lower than 1.9. If this MI is too large, there is a higher possibility of producing cavitation inside the patient (creation of vapour phase bubbles when you try to induce a pressure on a liquid which is below the vapour pressure of said liquid).

[PDF p. 70]

* Concept of vapour pressure - we introduce a small amount of a liquid in a closed cavity. If there is no gas, the P = 0 as there is nothing exerting a force on the material. Therefore, in principle the pressure in the liquid is equivalent to the vapour pressure, which is 0. However, the liquid has a temperature which is different than 0. Therefore the molecules are vibrating. This vibration causes some of the liquid particles to switch (jump) from the liquid to the vapour phase. Therefore there is a region of molecules in liquid phase and a region containing more and more molecules in vapour phase. The pressure in the upper part will increase until there is an equilibrium between the molecules evaporating and those recondensing. This behaviour occurs even for temperatures below the boiling point of the liquid. There is a point where there will be an equilibrium, so the vapour pressure will be equilibrated with the liquid pressure. The pressure in the equilibrium depends on the temperature of the system.

[PDF p. 71]

Ultrasound

Ultrasound workshop I

Ultrasound waves are mechanical waves with frequencies > 20 kHz (audible range: 20 Hz 20kHz) Its main application in medicine is the generation of images of the body.

HOW IS IT GENERATED?

The generation of US waves is based on the piezoelectric effect.

The thickness of some materials can be modified by an electric current In this case, Quartz crystals will modify their thickness when struck with an electrical signal. These changes in thickness will generate a mechanical vibration and produce high frequency wave s (US waves). In conclusion, an electrical signal will be transformed into a mechanical ultrasound.

The ultrasound waves will be transmitted to the body, where some will penetrate more than others. Some waves will be transmitted (penetrate) while others will be reflected. These reflected waves are received and transformed into an electrical signal by the transducer, which will process the data and give us an image.

ULTRASOUND EQUIPMENT

The waves are generated by a transducer/probe. We use different probes depending on the part of the body we want to scan.

  • Patient contact

Connector (cable) to the US machine

Images can be seen on a monitor in real time, and in any plane

↳ Transducers/probes

There are different types: (vary in shape, size,…). Some are used for tendons, for neonates (brain), transvaginal transducers (gynecology, to scan uterus and ovaries), in the surgery room,…

    - Curvilinear transducers are used to check the **abdomen** .

Low frequencies are used, to obtain more penetrance although with less resolution (DEEP in the abdomen. Used for in depth studies).

    - Linear transducers: are used on superficial structures such as the skin.

High frequencies are used, which give lower penetrance but higher resolution (SUPERFICIAL structures, for superficial, detailed studies).

“the higher the frequency, the more the wave is absorbed and the shallower the penetration”

[PDF p. 72]

ATTENUATION IN ULTRASOUND

The amplitude and intensity of ultrasound waves decrease as they travel through tissue, a phenomenon known as attenuation. Given a fixed propagation distance, attenuation affects high frequency ultrasound waves to a greater degree than lower frequency waves, as the higher the frequency, the more the wave is absorbed and the shallower the penetration.

Amplitude and intensity of the wave therefore depend on the attenuation.

    - **Materials with low attenuation/low absorption** = **water and fluids.** They appear

black on the image, and are said to be anechoic (don’t produce an echo) . When present as artifacts, these structures will present posterior acoustic enhancement.

    - **Materials with intermediate attenuation** = **solid tissues.** They present with

different scales of gray, depending on if they’re hypo, iso or even hyperechoic.

    - **Materials with high attenuation/high absorption** = **bones, calcium, metal,..** They

appear white on the image, and are said to be hyperechoic (produce the highest echo). When present as artifacts, they produce posterior acoustic shadowing.

The calculi (calcium) in the kidney and gallbladder cause posterior acoustic shadowing The hepatic cyst ( *cystic structures: closed capsule or sac-like structures, typically filled with a liquid) demonstrates the opposite phenomenon of posterior acoustic enhancement.

[PDF p. 73]

Shadowing occurs when there is a region that does not allow ultrasound to pass through. This is the case with bone, a gallstone,… In each of these cases, there is a region in which there is a big difference in impedance (bone, stone, air) that makes the wave reflect, and consequently we cannot see what happens behind such a region. These regions appear darkened, and are like a shadow of the zone that has reflected the ultrasound; the zone itself

being bright.

DOPPLER COLOUR ULTRASOUND

Another property of the US is that it gives info about the flow direction of the structures that are moving (arteries, veins,..). They present the known Doppler Effect: the frequency varies depending on the direction of the moving particle. Therefore by using this colour technique, depending on the direction and velocity, we will see red or blue colours.

It’s used to codify flow direction.

  • Flow towards the transducer: RED

  • Flow away from the transducer: BLUE

Example 1: liver US. We can observe the portal vein, with blood flowing towards the liver. In cirrhotic patients, the liver is very rigid, with no elasticity. A sign of this in the US is that the portal vein receives a reverse flow, as the flow cannot go towards the liver.

Example 2: carotid artery and jugular vein

COLOR AND POWER DOPPLER

Example: right kidney ultrasound. Patient comes in with renal failure, so we want to

check the vasculature.

Colour doppler identifies moving structures, such as those present in vasculature. But we can also use Power doppler to check vasculature, which is better as it’s very sensible for movement. However, it is not specific for the direction of the flow and therefore doesn’t codify the flow direction. Only serves to see if there’s movement or not.

[PDF p. 74]

COLOR AND PULSED DOPPLER

Pulsed doppler allows us to quantify velocity and determine flow direction.

When US waves contact with blood:

  • if it flows towards the transducer, we register a POSITIVE VELOCITY
  • if it flows away from the transducer: NEGATIVE

VELOCITY

Example 1: we can see the morphology of the arteries in the right kidney.

Example 2: carotid-jugular

blood flow . Arterial flow

(pulses) and Venous flow (it’s a continuous flow, barely any pulses)

LOCALIZATION OF STRUCTURES

LIVER US ABDOMINAL US THYROID-GLAND US

CARPAL TUNNEL US EYE US TESTICLES US

[PDF p. 75]

DETECTION AND LESION CHARACTERIZATION

We can classify tumors, detect metastasis, detect the presence of fluid,…

DIAGNOSIS-CLINICAL CORRELATION

US can be used to detect acute appendicitis. A patient refers having pain in the right iliac fossa. While checking this area, the patient refers increased pain once we stop compression. This is indicative of acute appendicitis. It’s important to know, in the moment, how the pain evolves in real time.

COMPARATIVE IMAGES

In this ultrasound image we can see the left and right thigh. Each image shows normal and abnormal muscle in the thigh, after running. By comparing the images, we are able to detect an abnormality in the right thigh, caused by a Hematoma within the

muscle.

VASCULARIZATION AND INFLAMMATION

GUIDED BIOPSY

To know where the lesion is while introducing and guiding the biopsy device, as it allows us to observe the image in real time. Example: needle entering liver and going towards the lesion.

[PDF p. 76]

Ultrasound advantages

- Doesn’t use ionizing radiation

Availability, portable and fast

  • We can scan in real time. Allows immediate visualization

  • Guidance for invasive procedures

  • We can obtain many perspectives and angles (good for lesions and localization)

Cheaper than other imaging modalities. Therefore, nowadays, they are readily available in primary care.

Ultrasound limitations

- Operator experience dependent

  • Unable to penetrate bone or air-filled structures (as they have very high attenuation )

Limited in patients with obesity, as there is very low penetrance and we can’t reach

to observe all the structures

[PDF p. 77]

Ultrasound workshop II

1st scenario

Parkinson’s patient is treated using HIFU (high intensity focused ultrasound). It’s an example of the applications of ultrasound to treatment. HIFU makes it possible to destroy certain brain cells responsible for trembling in Parkinson’s disease.

Effect of US = to increase temperature locally, in order to destroy said part of the brain. Patient’s head is surrounded by a hemisphere where many many transducers are located. The US waves join at the centre part of the brain.

What are the expected results when the US waves transduce?

Using a single transducer we would need a huge amount of intensity, which could potentially damage all the crossed tissues; in addition, the intensity will decrease when it goes inside of the patient, all along it’s path prior to reaching the centre of the brain. We would hit a whole trajectory of tissue on the brain of the patient. When we combine many transducers we reduce the intensity that needs to be generated by each and can be sure that only the central part will be affected by the combination of

intensities.

Another problem: before reaching the brain, the US wave is directed onto the skull which will absorb a large part of the energy and reflect it. Therefore, it’s temperature will increase. This is why patients refer pain when inside the machine. To solve this problem, the skull is recovered by a layer of water. The presence of water serves to match the impedances, but also to refrigerate the head of the patient.

2nd scenario

We are presented with a carotid artery stenosis. When blood vessels narrow, the blood has to circulate faster to make the same amount of blood reach the tissues in the same amount of time as it used to (smaller diameter = increase the velocity of erythrocytes).

In order to diagnose carotid artery stenosis, velocity of blood cells needs to be measured.

We take 2 measurements:

- Peak systolic velocity (PSV)

- Diastolic velocity (EDV)

If we modify the angle between the transducer and the vessel, the effect on PSV and EDV is that their values will change. If we place the transducer in such a way that the erythrocytes are travelling towards the transducer, we will observe

[PDF p. 78]

the Doppler shift phenomenon: it’ll be either positive (bottom) or negative (top), depending on v ult : whether the erythrocytes are approaching or moving away from the transducer.

If the measured velocity overgoes a threshold, the patient has stenosis, which will be a problem. Therefore, we can’t be playing with the angles of the transducer, as we will recover different velocities which don’t correspond to the real velocity. The velocity that we measure taking into account the Doppler effect is always smaller than the real erythrocytr’s velocity. Therefore, if the measured value is higher than the threshold, we can confirm that the real velocity is higher.

3rd scenario

A doctor wants to examine the heart of a patient using US (echocardiogram). But the heart is surrounded by bone, air in the lungs, soft tissue,… We need to cross the different tissues to reach the heart. Therefore, we’ll have to decide where the best location to place the transducer is, in order to obtain an image of the heart. The best: in between the ribs, pushing against the skin of the patient, to reach the heart and be as close as possible.

[PDF p. 79]

Dosimetry workshop

1st scenario

Man suddenly fell ill due to a polonium intoxication, which decays through alpha decay. It’s very toxic due to its ionizing capacity, and as it’s soluble in water, can cross through biological membranes.

How much of the energy released by the material was absorbed by this man?

Because of the size of the alpha particles, specific properties appear in the case of alpha particles, as well as for protons. The whole amount of energy released inside the body is absorbed, as it is found inside the body.

An external alpha source producing 5 MeV can seem to be much more dangerous as the energy per decay is higher. However, that is not correct. An external alpha source isn’t dangerous as this radiation has to cross a given amount of air + the outer skin. So all the energy is absorbed before the radiation reaches the tissues, on the surface. Nothing will penetrate. Alpha sources are dangerous when ingested and introduced inside the body. From here, the whole of the energy released will be absorbed.

2nd scenario

  • Increasing the intensity (electric current, mAmp) increases the number of electrons released, and therefore, the number of photons.
  • The average energy per photon only depends on the voltage value. This increases the probability of radiation, not the effect per photon.

What will be the effect on the absorbed dose (dose=energy per mass)? The absorbed dose will increase, as we have more photons and the energy absorbed is proportional to the number of photons.

3rd scenario

Lead apron = 0.25 mm of lead which attenuates approx 90% of the incoming radiation. Mainly designed for x-ray beams of 100KeV. For this case, the lead apron is effective. Even if it doubles the time of exposure (dose increases with time of exposure)

However, in the case of PET, 511keV photons are released. Therefore the lead apron has a thickness of 4mm in PET scanning. This means it is not truly effective (time-thickness wise)

Therefore, we will have to use other alternatives to reduce the absorbed energy.

[PDF p. 80]

Factors that affect the absorbed dose

  • Time (try to optimize the time spent in the room. Effective personnel training)

Amount of energy

  • Distance to the source

Increase thickness of shielding (we can increase the shielding, in case of PET not of the apron, but of the walls in the room)

4th scenario

PET scans release 511 keV photons; therefore, they are found in the Compton region.

In X-ray CT we try to prevent Compton scattering (as we are interested in retrieving quality images) and therefore use energies below the 30 Kev limits.

For radiotherapy, we are interested in the radiation reaching all of the tissues with tumors. We are interested in penetrating all the structures, therefore we will use energies in ranges

between 30 keV and 20 MeV.

COMPTON SCATTERING

Occurs at all energies ( = Led grid) but is more predominant at high energies

It increases patient dose (as it causes more ionization)

Decreases image contrast, thus decreasing the quality

[PDF p. 81]

FIRST WEEKS OF DEVELOPMENT

[PDF p. 82]

Gametogenesis

What are gametes?

Gametes are highly specialized sex cells, born only for reproductive purposes . If not fertilized, they degenerate in 24h (oocytes) or 2-3 days (sperm) after being produced. They are haploid cells.

Where do they come from?

Gametes originate from primordial germ cells . PGCs are diploid cells destined to perpetuate the species, giving rise to gametes. These are the only cells that undergo meiosis.

Gametogenesis consists in the conversion of the PGCs into male or female gametes.

Where does gametogenesis take place?

Gametogenesis occurs in the sex glands or gonads. During the end of the 3rd week of gestation, PGCs originate in the wall of the yolk sac and migrate to the gonads in the second month of gestation. This occurs both in males and females. In males, these PGCs remain inactive until puberty

SPERMATOGENESIS

Spermatogenesis begins at puberty in the seminiferous tubules of the testis.

- If we did a cross-section of a sex cord in a

neonate male, we would observe inactive primordial germ cells (spermatogonia) surrounded by sertoli cells and a basement

membrane.

- If we did a cross-section of the testis during puberty, we would observe that these tubules acquire a lumen. Spermatogonia begin dividing and sperm are released and transported through

the tubules.

[PDF p. 83]

Spermatogenesis entails two events:

  • Genetic reduction of PGCs: from the primary spermatocyte, there are 2 meiotic divisions which give place to 4 spermatids.
  • Spermiogenesis: process by which spermatids mature into 4 spermatozoa.

How does spermatogenesis occur?

Spermatogenesis is characterized by 3 specific functional phases:

1. Mitotic proliferation: from 1 first spermatogonium, there are successive mitotic divisions. But these cells remain connected, and we obtain a synchronic progeny (they all began dividing at the same time and will continue to do so, synchronously). 2. Meiosis I and II, through which primary and secondary spermatocytes give early spermatids (still joined together). 3. Differentiation / Spermiogenesis, phase in which spermatids mature into spermatozoa . In the process of spermiogenesis:

           - Nuclear condensation occurs

           - Acrosome formation

            - Formation of neck, middle piece and tail

            - Release of cytoplasmic material

10% of ejaculated cells are grossly abnormal. There are problems when this percentage goes over 20%

              - This whole process takes around **64 days.**

                        - It **begins at puberty** and it’s a **continuous** process,

which continues until the death of the individual.

              - Spermatogenesis is **coordinated radially** within the

tubule cross-section. We can find spermatogonia at different stages of development.

[PDF p. 84]

Blood-testis barrier

An important function of sertoli cells is the formation of the

blood-testis barrier.

They form a physical barrier that isolates meiotic and post-meiotic germ cells from immune and lymphatic systems, protecting them. This is done because, as they are different from the rest of the cells of the body, they could be recognized as foreign cells and be attacked.

  • Sertoli cells: create the blood-testis barrier and favour

spermatogenesis (FSH)

Leydig or Interstitial cells: located in the space between the tubules. Cells responsible for the production of testosterone (LH)

How is spermatogenesis controlled?

It’s controlled by the anterior pituitary gland, through FSH (follicle-stimulating hormone) and LH (luteinizing hormone) . This gland is stimulated by the Hypothalamus.

  • FSH is going to act on sertoli cells, making them promote spermatogenesis by activating spermatogonia.

  • LH acts on leydig cells, which will start producing testosterone. Testosterone will also reinforce spermatogenesis.

[PDF p. 85]

OOGENESIS

Oogenesis takes place in the ovaries (which are the female gonads/sex glands).

Oogenesis vs spermatogenesis

Same as in spermatogenesis, oocytes are derived from Primordial Germ Cells (oogonia). Oogonia, same as sperm, originates in the wall of the yolk sac. They migrate towards the gonads in the 2nd month of gestation. But in contrast with males, oogonia become very active and start dividing by mitosis during the 5th month of gestation.

Many PGCs are lost during development. If PGCs don’t reach the undifferentiated gonad,

and instead reach and accumulate in other

tissues, they give rise to bizarre tumors known as teratomas (as they are rapidly dividing cells).

Contrarily to spermatogenesis, from each primary oocyte (coming from oogonium), only one secondary oocyte is obtained, and from there, only 1 mature oocyte is formed if oogenesis is completed (formation of polar bodies).

How does oogenesis occur?

1. Before birth, oogonia proliferate and peak by mid-gestation. Some of them will enter oogenesis and differentiate into primary oocytes which

enter meiosis I.

But they will remain arrested in diplotene of prophase I until puberty.

The rest of oogonia which don’t differentiate, degenerate before birth.

[PDF p. 86]

Primary oocytes arrested at prophase I are surrounded by follicular cells (sustentacular cells), found forming primordial

follicles.

A primordial follicle contains the nucleus of the primary oocyte + flat

follicular cells.

Ovaries at birth only contain a given number of oocytes, arrested in meiosis I. Only a few of them will undergo ovulation, and the rest will

die.

2. From infancy to puberty, primary oocytes remain arrested in diplotene of prophase I.

3. Oogenesis is resumed at puberty. But it doesn’t continue as a continuous process, but a cyclic one:

A few follicles start maturating, and only 1 primary follicle will obtain maturity. From puberty to menopause, every 28 days , one follicle fully matures and resumes meiosis I, entering meiosis II where it once again becomes arrested until

fertilization.

4. If fertilization occurs, the

secondary oocyte continues meiosis II until it gives place to the mature oocyte.

THE OVARIAN CYCLE

The ovarian cycle makes reference to changes taking place in the ovary.

  • Maturation of follicle

Ovulation and oocyte release

Corpus luteum

Degeneration of corpus luteum

[PDF p. 87]

1. Follicular phase

This development and resumption of oogenesis begins at puberty. It involves the formation of the primary follicle, granulosa cells, the zona pellucida, the

formation of an antrum and theca cells.

● Formation of the primary follicle

Flat follicular cells of the primordial follicle become

cuboidal follicular cells. These stratified cuboidal

follicular cells, known as granulosa cells, form a several layer membrane . The zona pellucida also arises (a membrane of glycosaminoglycans and glycoproteins which separates the oocyte from the granulosa cell layer) . This gives place to the primary follicle.

● Formation of secondary follicle

Under the action of FSH (pituitary g.), several changes take place in the follicle: a cavity (Antrum) forms around granulosa cells and fills with liquid, pushing the granulosa cells. The primary oocyte becomes displaced and pushed onto the wall of the zona pellucida. As the antrum evolves, it gives place to the structure of the secondary follicle. Theca cells are found surrounding granulosa.

● Formation of tertiary follicle or Graff follicle

The secondary follicle becomes mature at midcycle, forming the tertiary follicle. Theca cells form a bilayer (theca-interna and theca-externa) around the granulosa cells. Theca-interna cells produce precursors of estrogens, which are transported to the granulosa cells and are transformed into estrogens.

* During this whole maturation, the oocyte is still arrested at diplotene of prophase I (primary oocyte). It enters meiosis II when released from the follicle during ovulation.

Functions of the follicle

  • Production of hormones (estrogens and progesterone (during luteal phase))

Enabling ovulation, as it expels the oocyte out of the ovary when the time comes

[PDF p. 88]

2. Ovulation

Ovulation takes place at mid-cycle (day 14).

The secondary oocyte which is arrested at metaphase II, is expelled at day 14 during ovulation.

Granulosa cells and theca cells, which have been left behind, undergo morphological changes under the influence of LH : they are converted into secretory cells known as luteal cells, which will now mostly secrete progesterone. (In addition to still secreting small amounts of estrogen, they secrete lots of progesterone). Together, these luteal cells from the corpus luteum.

  • In the 1st half of the ovarian cycle: primarily estrogen
  • In the 2nd half of the ovarian cycle: mainly progesterone

3. Luteal phase

These luteal cells form a body known as the corpus luteum, which is released at the same time as the secondary oocyte. It’s made up of secretory cells whose function is to secrete progesterone. It finally degenerates if not fertilized, and the process is restarted.

Only if the secondary oocyte is fertilized, it will complete meiosis II.

Characteristics of ovulation

The expulsion of the oocyte is the result of intrafollicular pressure and possibly, the contraction of smooth muscle in the theca externa .

It’s a hormone-mediated process (this stage is regulated mainly by LH)

The LH surge resumes meiosis I and the secondary oocyte enters meiosis II, where it is arrested at metaphase

Ovulation consists in the release of this 2ary oocyte

[PDF p. 89]

How is oogenesis controlled?

The hypothalamus acts on the anterior pituitary gland, promoting the release of FSH and

LH.

  • The amount of FSH is larger in the 1st half of the cycle. FSH stimulates granulosa cells in the ovarian follicles to synthesize aromatase, which converts androgens produced by the theca cells to estradiol, to produce estrogen. As the levels of estrogen increase in the blood, it has negative feedback on the pituitary glands. This is so until estrogens peak and acquire a certain concentration in plasma (200 pg/mL) around days 12-14. (This high amount of estrogens informs the pituitary that the oocyte is ready to be released). From this threshold, estrogen creates a positive feedback and induces the pituitary to increase the amount of FSH, but mainly LH.

The increased amounts of LH favour the resumption of meiosis and therefore

induce ovulation.

  • The amount of LH increases mainly in the 2nd part of the cycle . It acts on luteinized theca and granulosa cells which will begin to secrete progesterone. The high amounts of LH produced (LH surge) after the estrogen threshold has been surpassed, trigger ovulation. Ovulation normally occurs 24 to 36 hours after the LH

surge.

Estrogens and progesterone both act outside of the ovary, but not on the ovary. They act via the vascular system. FSH and LH act on the oocyte and structures which contain it.

[PDF p. 90]

THE MENSTRUAL CYCLE

It makes reference to the cyclic changes that take place in the endometrium, produced by estrogens and progesterone . Cyclic changes in the endometrium are accompanied by changes in the uterine tubes, vagina and cervical mucus. This is controlled by estrogens and progesterone:

Estrogen promotes proliferation and cell growth (proliferative phase)

Progesterone increases secretory activity (secretory phase)

➢ Days 4-14. Proliferative phase

After the previous ovulation, from day 4 to day 14, the endometrium has to be regenerated . This is stimulated by estrogen, which induces proliferation and renewal.

This is known as the proliferative phase:

Epithelial cells grow and proliferate

Blood vessels extend and accumulate blood in case there is implantation

Glands are formed which secrete glycogen to nurture the future embryo

Watery cervical mucus (penetrable)

➢ Days 14-21. Secretory phase

Progesterone is released, glycogen accumulates and blood vessels continue to grow (secretory phase) :

Increases secretory activity

There is less contractility of the uterine tubes

  • Cervical mucus becomes thick and non elastic (non-penetrable, to protect the oocyte)

➢ Day 25. Menstruation

If there has been no fertilization, the corpus luteum degenerates and stops secreting progesterone. Therefore blood vessels strat becoming necrotic and constrict due to the lack of progesterone, which is the hormone which

maintains the endometrium.

[PDF p. 91]

Fertilization

Fertilization marks day 1 of the life of a human being.

FERTILIZATION PROCESS

Fertilization consists in the fusion of gametes in the ampulla of the uterine tube.

PHASE 1. PENETRATION OF THE CORONA RADIATA

  • The secondary oocyte arrested at metaphase II is released on day 14.

  • There is activity from the fimbriae in the tube, finger-like projections which capture the released oocyte through.

Meanwhile, after sexual intercourse,

sperm enter through the vagina and into

the uterus.

  • The vagina provides a very acidic environment, which is incompatible with sperm. Therefore, sperm are released along with semen, which alkalizes the pH so sperm

can survive.

Sperm make their way to the uterine tubes. During this journey they undergo a process known as capacitation : it consists in the removal of some glycoproteins which, if present, don’t permit fertilization. Only capacitated sperm will be able to fertilize the oocyte.

  • The few sperm which reach the oocyte penetrate the corona radiata and reach the zona pellucida.

PHASE 2. PENETRATION OF THE ZONA PELLUCIDA

Sperms need to penetrate the strong barrier which is the zona pellucida. This is achieved through binding in a ligand-receptor fashion

  • Molecules on the surface of the sperm are specific binding sites for the ZP3 ( zona pellucida-3) receptors found on the zona pellucida.

[PDF p. 92]

This ligand-receptor reaction (acrosome reaction) triggers the breakage of the membrane which contains the acrosome and the release of enzymes such as acrosin, which carry out extracellular digestion of the zona pellucida.

PHASE 3. FUSION OF THE SPERM AND OOCYTE MEMBRANES

  • After digestion of the zona pellucida, the two membranes contact one another and

fuse.

  • This fusion causes a weak membrane depolarization and leads to a calcium wave that triggers resumption of meiosis II.

In the oocyte there are enzymes contained in vesicles, known as cortical granules. The induced calcium wave also triggers a cortical reaction, the fusion of these cortical granules with the oolemma (oocyte membrane)

  • The cortical granules release their content, and degrade and inhabilitate all receptors to prevent other sperms from entering. This is known as the zona

reaction.

  • The zona reaction hydrolyzes ZP3 receptors, blocking the possibility of polyspermy.

RESUMPTION OF MEIOSIS II

  • The entry of sperm resumes meiosis II, which is quickly completed (giving place to Polar body II)

  • Only the sperm’s nucleus enters and fuses with the oocyte’s nucleus. This gives place to a unicellular embryo, known as the pronuclei stage zygote.

  • Female and male pronuclei duplicate their nucleus and carry out the first mitotic division, giving place to the two-cell stage zygote . These are the first two cells of the future organism, known as blastomeres.

[PDF p. 93]

This whole process, from fertilization till the formation of the two-cell stage zygote, takes around 30 hours. If there’s no fertilization, the oocyte usually degenerates after 24h .

The main outcomes of fertilization are:

  • The resumption of meiosis II in the oocyte

The formation of a 2 cell-stage embryo, known as zygote

  • Restoration of diploid number of chromosomes
  • Formation of a new individual with unique genetic content

Determination of the sex of the embryo (XX o XY, at the 2 cell stage)

FIRST WEEK OF DEVELOPMENT

During the first week of development, the zygote goes from the two-cell stage to the blastocyst stage through cleavage, and begins the process of implantation .

Cleavage consists in a series of mitotic divisions which gradually reduce the size of embryonic cells (blastomeres) until the normal size seen in somatic cells is recovered . The zona pellucida compresses the dividing cells into a reduced space, forcing them to regain

their normal size.

FORMATION OF THE BLASTOCYST

First 30h: 2-cell stage. The zygote travels along the tubes to the uterus for its implantation. During this process, it undergoes cleavage and segmentation.

  • The zygote divides (4 cell stage, 8 cell stage,…) until the Morula is formed on day 4.

  • Early blastocyst: around days 4-5, fluid enters the morula and forms a cavity (blastocystic cavity), which compresses the cells and forms the early blastocyst. At this point, we find an external layer of cells surrounding the cavity, and a compressed inner mass.

[PDF p. 94]

  • Late blastocyst: the early blastocyst releases the zona pellucida, in order to implant into the endometrium. In this way, the late blastocyst is formed. Only a blastocyst released from the zona pellucida can achieve implantation.

At this point, the inner cell mass is known as the embryoblast (which will give place to the embryo) and the outer layer of cells is known as the trophoblast (will be in charge of forming structures which nurture the embryo).

BEGINNING OF IMPLANTATION

Implantation begins during day 5-6 after fertilization. In other words, between days 20-23 of the menstrual cycle. The monolayer of flat epithelial cells in the outer cell mass (trophoblast) will differentiate upon implantation, into:

Cytotrophoblast (epithelium, seen in light green)

Syncytiotrophoblast (syncytium, seen in dark green. Formed by many dividing cells which do not achieve complete cytokinesis; therefore, they form a mass with many nuclei floating in one big cytoplasm) . This structure has a highly invasive power and starts eroding the endometrium, enabling implantation.

[PDF p. 95]

Ectopic pregnancies

An ectopic pregnancy occurs when a fertilised egg implants itself outside of the womb . For example, in the fallopian tubes, abdominal cavity, cervix,…

● Tubal pregnancy: the embryo begins to develop in the ampullary region (uterine tube). It normally ruptures during the 2nd month of development and

has to be removed.

  • Rectouterine pouch pregnancy: rarely, the oocyte can escape fimbriae in the tube during its release and the fertilized embryo implants and develops in the rectouterine pouch, the lowest point of the abdominal cavity (Douglas pouch). It’s a pregnancy of risk, but it can come to term.

[PDF p. 96]

2nd week of development

The main event of the 2nd week is completion of implantation.

The 2nd week of life corresponds to days 21-28 of the endometrial cycle. This is why each cycle has a duration of around 28 days: it allows time for implantation to

occur.

If there were no sign of implantation, menstruation would happen. Implantation takes place by attaching to the endometrium at the embryonic pole: the blastocyst leaves the uterine cavity and penetrates into the endometrium.

8th DAY OF DEVELOPMENT. BILAMINAR DISC STAGE

In the late blastocyst which begins implantation, we can differentiate an inner cell mass (embryoblast) and an outer cell mass (trophoblast, which is divided into cytotrophoblast and syncytiotrophoblast). On the 8th day of embryonic development, the embryo enters the bilaminar disk stage. This bilaminar structure is formed in the embryoblast, and is composed of the Hypoblast layer and the Epiblast . The amniotic cavity also arises between the epiblast and the cytotrophoblast along with amnioblasts, flat cells which are found surrounding the amniotic cavity . Many more changes continue to take place in the trophoblastic tissue.

The corpus luteum knows implantation has begun because the embryo releases a hormone by day 9-10, called human chorionic gonadotropin (HCG), the same hormone tested for the ascertainment of pregnancy ( by the end of the 2nd week, this hormone is found in high enough quantities as to be detected) . This signal reaches the corpus luteum and makes it remain active, releasing progesterone. Luteolysis does not occur and gestation may proceed. The corpus luteum works until the 4th month of gestation. From there, the placenta takes over.

[PDF p. 97]

9th DAY OF DEVELOPMENT

On the 9th day, a fibrin coagulum forms to prevent the embryo from leaving the

endometrium.

Within the Syncytiotrophoblast, spaces form and fuse together forming larger cavities, known as trophoblastic lacunae. In the embryoblast, cells coming from the hypoblast begin to migrate, forming a thin

membrane which covers the inner surface of

the cytotrophoblast. This membrane is known as the exocoelomic membrane, which lines the blastocyst cavity. This lined cavity becomes known as the exocoelomic cavity.

The exocoelomic membrane, together with cells of the hypoblast, form the primitive yolk sac (which is the site of PGC formation). By day 9, the blastocyst is completely embedded in the uterus wall.

12th DAY OF DEVELOPMENT

In relation to the trophoblast: lacunae in the syncytiotrophoblast continue to expand and fuse forming lacunar networks. At that time, capillaries in the endometrium dilate, forming maternal sinusoids. The syncytiotrophoblast finally erodes the wall of the endometrium, reaching maternal sinusoids and uterine glands and allowing maternal blood and uterine secretions (glycogen and proteins) to flow into the lacunar network. Through diffusion, they will exchange gases and metabolites with the embryo (these lacunae are more prominent near the embryonic pole). This is the beginning of uteroplacental circulation or maternal circulation.

Around the same time, a new population of cells appear between the inner surface of the cytotrophoblast and the primitive yolk sac (exocoelomic membrane), known as the extraembryonic mesoderm . It’s a membrane formed by loose connective tissue, found intervening between the trophoblast and embryoblast. As it forms, spaces/vacuoles also begin to appear within this extraembryonic mesoderm. Spaces known as extraembryonic cavities.

[PDF p. 98]

13th DAY OF DEVELOPMENT

On the 13th day of development, extraembryonic cavities found within the extraembryonic mesoderm gradually fuse to form one large cavity known as the chorionic cavity or extraembryonic cavity. Therefore, the original blastocyst cavity (blastocele) now becomes known as coelom, surrounded by the chorionic cavity. It divides the extraembryonic mesoderm into 2 regions: one surrounding the trophoblast (parietal or somatic) and another lining the yolk sac (visceral or splanchnic ) .

Around day 13, a large portion of the exocoelomic cavity is pinched, forming a smaller remaining cavity, now known as secondary yolk sac (smaller than the primary).

The part of the membrane found binding the embryoblast to the cytotrophoblast is known as the connective salk (the future umbilical cord).

Additionally, at this stage the cytotrophoblast penetrates into the syncytiotrophoblast and forms villi-like structures, known as primary villi. The extraembryonic mesoderm also begins to penetrate into the cytotrophoblast forming finger-like projections.

DECIDUALIZATION REACTION

When the blastocyst is found borrowed into the endometrium, the endometrial wall becomes known as the decidua (decidua basalis, capsularis and parietalis). This occurs because implantation of the blastocyst induces decidualization of the endometrial stroma, through the decidual

reaction.

This decidual reaction (decidualization)

consists in the transformation of endometrial

cells into specialized cell types called decidual cells, to provide nutrition and immunological protection to the conceptus (accumulate lipids and glycogen).

[PDF p. 99]

  • Why isn’t the embryo rejected and perceived as a foreign structure?

Because the syncytiotrophoblast (found surrounding and protecting the embryo) expresses specific antigens known as human leukocyte antigen-G (HLA-G), which act as a ligand to leukocytes and inhibit them. They downregulate the maternal immune response.

Implantation window: limited time span when blastocyst competence is at its priming point.

● Dating of pregnancy

    **Fertilization age:** date pregnancy from the time of conception/fertilization

    **Menstrual age:** dates pregnancy from the woman’s last menstrual period

A normal pregnancy would be 38 weeks after fertilization and 40 weeks after the onset of the last normal menstrual period.

ABNORMAL SITES OF IMPLANTATION

The most frequent abnormal site of implantation is the uterine tube. Tubal pregnancies Tubal pregnancies aren’t seen to term, and the uterine tube ruptures by the 2nd month of gestation.

Other abnormal pregnancies can occur due to an abnormal placenta. One example of this is “Placenta previa”, where the placenta develops in the lowest part of the endometrium, covering the cervix. Bleeding often happens as the lower part of the uterus thins during the third trimester of pregnancy. This causes the area of the placenta over the cervix to bleed. The risk of bleeding is higher if a lot of the placenta covers the cervix, as is the case of placenta previa. There is much more bleeding than there would normally be and this leads to hemorrhage.

● Hydatidiform moles

Hydatidiform moles are abnormal blastocysts with no embryonic tissue. However, the trophoblast develops and forms a placenta. Most often, a hydatidiform mole comes from an abnormal fertilized egg that develops into a hydatidiform mole rather than a fetus (a condition called molar pregnancy). However, a hydatidiform mole can develop from cells that remain in the uterus after a miscarriage, a full-term pregnancy, or a mislocated pregnancy (ectopic pregnancy).

[PDF p. 100]

Rarely, a hydatidiform mole develops when there is a living fetus. In such cases, the fetus typically dies, and a miscarriage often occurs.

Women who have a hydatidiform mole feel as if they are pregnant. But because hydatidiform moles grow much faster than a fetus, the abdomen becomes larger much faster than it does in a normal pregnancy. Severe nausea and vomiting and vaginal bleeding are common. As parts of the mole deteriorate, small amounts of tissue, which resemble a bunch of grapes, may pass through the vagina. The moles secrete high amounts of hCG (as this hormone comes from the syncytiotrophoblast), which can be detected for diagnosis. About 80% of hydatidiform moles are not cancerous, but they may become invasive and develop into malignant tumors (choriocarcinomas) which rapidly spread into the lymphatic system and the bloodstream.

[PDF p. 101]

3rd week of development

MAIN EVENTS

Gastrulation, which involves the formation of the 3 germ cell layers which will give rise to all of the tissues and organs in the body.

Neurulation, which consists in the beginning of the formation of CNS (but won’t be completed until the end of the 4th week).

GASTRULATION

In summary, gastrulation is the process by which the three primary germ layers of the

trilaminar disc are formed.

The first cells to invaginate through the primitive groove form the definitive endoderm. The remaining cells of the epiblast are called the ectoderm. Cells that remain in the space between ectoderm and definitive endoderm form a layer known as the mesoderm.

At the end of the 3rd week, the embryo will be known as trilaminar disc.

How does gastrulation occur?

1. Firstly, we have the formation of the primitive streak

  • On day 15, this new structure can be observed by looking at the epiblast from above. It’s a groove that initiates at the caudal end of the embryo. In the bilaminar disc, the epiblast belongs to the dorsal part of the embryo. As soon as the primitive streak appears, the cranial-caudal axis is defined, marking where the tail and head of the embryo will be. In addition to this, we imminently know what is left and what is right . The embryo is now totally oriented. Thus, regions of the embryo (all 3 axes) become identifiable by day 15.

[PDF p. 102]

  • The streak protuberates outwards and produces an elevation known as primitive node (“the organizer”, it releases factors that will induce differentiation), which possesses a hole known as the primitive pit, through which prenotochordal cells will migrate to form the notochord.

    During gastrulation, epiblast cells migrate toward the midline/primitive streak, detach from the epiblast and invaginate. They start to pass underneath, giving rise to a new layer formed between the epiblast and hypoblast. Epiblast cells are epithelial cells, interconnected by adhesion molecules. As they migrate, they lose their adhesion molecules, change their shape and acquire the ability to migrate, and thus become mesenchymal cells, undifferentiated cells with proliferative capacity.

    From here, epiblast cells expand as mesenchymal cells in this new layer between epiblast and hypoblast and form the mesoderm layer, giving place to the

trilaminar disc.

The mesenchyme corresponds to embryonic loose connective tissue.

2. Fate maps: cells in specific regions of the epiblast and entering the primitive streak will form specific structures in the mesoderm.

  • Epiblastic cells which migrate and invaginate via the primitive pit will form the axial mesoderm. This axial

mesoderm will form the notochord.

The notochord serves as the basis for the axial

skeleton.

The formation of the notochord is very complex: cells enter via primitive pit and extend cranially. The migrated cells fuse with the hypoblast, forming the notochordal plate, which eventually detaches from the hypoblast

and forms the notochord.

  • The mesoderm expands through the space between epiblast and hypoblast except for 2 regions:

    • Oropharyngeal membrane, where there is no mesoderm. Epiblast and hypoblast are directly in contact, tightly together. This is the region

from which the mouth is formed.

    **Cloacal plate/membrane,** Once again, there is

no mesoderm. It gives place to the anus, urethra, urinary bladder and rectum.

At this point, we can also observe the Allantois, a region of protuberant hypoblasts which will lead to the formation of part of the bladder.

[PDF p. 103]

The remaining mesoderm is the paraxial and lateral mesoderm.

    The cells that extend at both sides of the notochord, close to

the midline, will form the paraxial mesoderm.

    Cells closer to the caudal region form the **lateral plate**

mesoderm

    The epiblasts that enter at the caudal-most part become

continuous with the extraembryonic mesoderm, and

contribute to its formation.

3. Formation of the endoderm: mesenchymal cells also begin to replace the hypoblast cells, forming a totally new layer known as endoderm (definitive endoderm). As the mesoderm mesenchymal cells migrate to replace those of the hypoblast, they differentiate and once again become epithelial cells. Therefore, the initial epiblast cells will give place to the endoderm and the mesoderm. The epiblast that remains is known as the

ectoderm.

Cross section:
