  • Ectoderm : skin, CNS, PNS, eyes, internal ear, neural crest cells (in addition, bones and connective tissue of the face and part of the skull)
  • Mesoderm (+ notochord, which is part of the mesoderm): bones, connective tissue, urogenital system and cardiovascular system.
  • Endoderm : gives rise to the gut and mucosa of the gut + gut derivatives (liver, pancreas, lungs,…)

PATHOLOGIES

SIRENOMELIA: caudal dysgenesis. Occurs due to a lack of mesoderm in the lumbosacral region, which results in the fusion of the limb buds, accompanied by other defects in the organs of the body. They generally die after birth.

Tumors associated with gastrulation include SACROCOCCYGEAL TERATOMA. After gastrulation, the primitive streak usually regresseses, but if it doesn’t totally disappear, remnant pluripotent cells can give rise to

these teratomas.

[PDF p. 104]

GENETIC REGULATION OF LATERALITY

There is a laterality in the body that comes about due to the expression of signalling molecules. The expression of Nodal on the left side of the body regulates left-sidedness and leads to a cascade of events that positions the organs.

SITUS INVERSUS

There is an inversion in the positioning of organs.

It can be a partial situs inversus, seen in only one or a few organs positioned in the wrong side of the body (heart = dextrocardia, liver,..), or it can be a complete

situs inversus.

Complete situs inversus is more serious and involves left-right asymmetry (all the blood vessels and organs). “20% of people with situs inversus also present KARTAGENER’S SYNDROME (respiratory system problems + infertility, due to cilia malfunction in PCD)

NEURULATION

Neurulation consists in the formation of the neural tube.

During the 3rd week of development, we can observe the

initiation of neurulation.

Formation of the neural plate

  • The notochord forms and starts secreting molecules

that stimulate the conversion of surface ectoderm

into neural tissue .

These molecules act on the overlying ectoderm and induce the transformation of its epithelial cells into polygonal cells. The cells change their form. As the notochord grows, the area of influence is larger.

  • By the end of the 3rd week, as the embryo grows mostly cranially, the surface ectoderm is converted into neural tissue, forming the neural plate. Why does only the surface ectoderm undergo neurulation? Due to back inhibition. An inhibitor is being produced laterally, so the conversion is topped and restricted to a given area. Combination of induction and inhibition patterns.

Formation of the neural tube

Once the neural plate is formed, there is an increase in the height of cells, forming a thickened neural plate.

  • The plate forms a central neural groove and two lateral neural folds

The 2 folds elevate along the groove

  • The two folds fuse. Once this occurs, they detach from the overlying ectoderm surface, becoming invaginated. The ectoderm is found surrounding the neural tube, which will give rise to the skin

[PDF p. 105]

4th week of development

At the end of the 4th week, the embryo looks like:

During the 4th week of embryonic development, neurulation is complete and the embryo passes from being a fat trilaminar disc to adopting a 3D structure.

NEURULATION AT MID 3rd WEEK

At mid 3rd week (18 days), the neural plate invaginates and the folds fuse. However, this is a gradual process: the tube doesn’t fuse and invaginate all at once. It first fuses and then progressively invaginates during the 4th week.

NEURULATION AT THE END OF 3rd WEEK

(Day 20)

Many factors contribute to invagination, such as

somites.

Neurulation takes place while the paraxial mesoderm becomes thick, differentiates and forms

compact segments known as somites. Somites are independent, differentiated paraxial mesoderm which protrude and press against the neural tube to favour invagination.

The formation of these structures

progresses along with neurulation. The protruding paraxial structures grow in

number and increase .

CLOSURE OF NEURAL TUBE DURING 4th WEEK

(days 22 - 28) By the end of day 28, the neural tube is totally invaginated.

[PDF p. 106]

FOLDINGS OF THE EMBRYO

At the same time, both lateral and cranial-caudal foldings of the embryo take place. The embryo undergoes rapid development in size and shape.

● Lateral body foldings

The lateral folding is produced by the rapidly growing spinal cord and somites.

  • The ectoderm on the sides extends ventrally. The paraxial mesoderm begins to form the somites while the neural tube continues to invaginate. The yolk sac becomes thinner at the sides, and begins to narrow, forming what will be the gut

tube.

  • As the amniotic cavity and the ectoderm continue to extend ventrally, the mesoderm differentiates into somatic mesoderm (in contact with the ectoderm) and splanchnic mesoderm (surrounding remaining endoderm, which will form the gut).

As the amniotic cavity continues to surround and approaches the yolk sac, a new cavity arises: the intra-embryonic body cavity. The connection between gut and yolk sac continues to get narrower and narrower. They will remain connected by a

canal known as the vitelline duct.

  • Eventually, the amniotic cavity will surround the whole body of the embryo. The body will be immersed in that cavity, floating in amniotic fluid. Within the body of the embryo, we find the intra-embryonic body cavity / intraembryonic coelom surrounding the gut and yolk sac. The gut remains attached to the rest of the body by a stalk known as the dorsal mesentery (mesoderm). Note that although not represented in all images, the ectoderm deriving from amnioblasts still lines the inner face of the amniotic cavity, forming an epithelial layer (see picture at top of the page)

[PDF p. 107]

  • Cranial-caudal folds

In the cranial-caudal folds, head and tail move ventrally, and the embryo will adopt a “C” shape. As a result:

  • The primitive heart (heart mesoderm) bends ventrally and moves towards the midline, compressing the yolk sac. The pericardial cavity and heart tube also begin to arise within the heart mesoderm.

  • Neural tube also bends ventrally (anterior and posterior neuroscope)

  • Oropharyngeal membrane (the future mouth ) bends and ends up being located in the head; cloacal membrane is also invaginated at the caudal end

  • The allantoid is also compressed anteriorly/ventrally until it presses against the yolk sac. The upper-most part will contribute to the formation of the urinary bladder, and the lower part will insert into the connecting stalk/umbilical cord, known as the urachus (thick hollow tube which connects the primitive urinary bladder to the umbilical cord)

  • ‐ As the body folds progress, part of the endoderm lined yolk sac is incorporated to the embryo as gastrointestinal tract (gut)

The endoderm will give rise to the gastrointestinal tract. As embryonic folding continues, the endoderm moves towards the midline and fuses, incorporating the dorsal part of the yolk sac to create the primitive gut tube . This tube differentiates into 3 main parts:

  • Foregut (cranial end). Temporarily closed by the oropharyngeal membrane, which at the end of the 4th week will rupture and give rise to

the mouth .

[PDF p. 108]

  • Midgut, which lies in between and remains connected to the yolk sac until the 5th week of development. As embryonic development continues, the connection to the yolk sac will narrow into a stalk known as the vitelline duct. Yolk sac remains connected through the midgut, which is why in some cross sections (fore or hindgut) we only see gut tube.

  • Hindgut (caudal end). Temporarily closed by the cloacal plate/membrane, until the 7th, when it ruptures to form the urogenital and anal openings (anus).

➢ Folding at the rostral end

 The cardiogenic area is found anterior to the oropharyngeal membrane. Two

tubes in this bulge fuse to form the primitive heart. As soon as they fuse, the primitive heart starts beating. Originally anterior to the oropharyngeal membrane, but as craniocaudal folding proceeds, the heart is displaced ventrally. - The oropharyngeal membrane will therefore be anterior to the heart as a result of the folding. - The yolk sac (umbilical vesicle) is incorporated into the embryo as the anterior wall of the primitive gut.

➢ Folding at the caudal end

 - As craniocaudal folding proceeds, the **connecting stalk and allantois** are

displaced and compressed ventrally. They come together and press onto the yolk sac, eventually forming the definitive umbilical cord.

[PDF p. 109]

FORMATION OF THE INTRAEMBRYONIC COELOM

Spaces begin to form within the lateral mesoderm, as the paraxial mesoderm gives rise to the somites. These spaces become bigger and fuse, forming the intraembryonic coelom, a flattened, horseshoe‐shaped cavity which will originate the definitive intraembryonic cavity.

Upper view: the cavity extends in a “U” shape around the lateral sides of the embryo (seen from above). The upper coelom will become known as the pericardial coelom and the centre-most, peritoneal coelom (peritoneum = abdominal cavity lining).

  • Seen in the cross-section: the intraembryonic cavity initially becomes continuous with the extraembryonic cavity. As the folding progresses, the lateral plate mesoderm splits into 2 layers:

    Parietal (somatic) layer: will be found lining the inside of the ectoderm as the embryo closes. It will form underlying tissue below the ectoderm, in other words, the dermis +

other structures such as muscles.

    - **Visceral (splanchnic) layer:** It’s continuous with the

somatic layer and is found surrounding the primitive gut tube. Will give rise to the smooth muscle of the gut.

The intraembryonic cavity will correspond with the abdominal and pericardial cavities. As the closing continues, the embryo closes on itself and the continuity between intra and extraembryonic cavity is lost. The amniotic cavity is now found surrounding the whole body of the embryo, which is at the same time found within the chorionic / extraembryonic cavity. The thin connection with the remnant of the yolk sac will become thinner and thinner and eventually form a ligament within the umbilical cord.

EMBRYO AT THE END OF THE 4th WEEK

Lateral view of a 27-somite embryo, approximately 26 days. The embryo is curved, especially its tail-like caudal

eminence. We can observe the lens

placode and the otic pit, indicating early development of the internal ear.

[PDF p. 110]

We can also see that the rostral neuropore is closed and that three pairs of pharyngeal arches are present. They are transient structures which will give rise to others.

The main features of the embryo at this stage (26-28 days dev) are the three pairs of pharyngeal arches and the somites.

This 28-day embryo is surrounded by the amniotic cavity, found floating in the chorionic/extraembryonic cavity and suspended by a ventrally located umbilical

cord.

OVERVIEW OF THE EMBRYO-FOLDING OUTCOMES

  • The neural tube becomes fully invaginated.

    - rostral brain vesicles will give rise

to the brain

    - caudal neural tube will give rise to

the spinal cord

  • The primordial heart is found in a ventral position in relation to the mouth

  • The intraembryonic coelom is formed

  • Connective stalk, yolk sac and allantois

form the umbilical cord

  • The primitive gut is fully formed

VENTRAL BODY WALL DEFECTS

They are caused due to abnormal closure of one or more of the body folds.

ECTOPIA CORDIS GASTROSCHISIS BLADDER EXSTROPHY

[PDF p. 111]

Placentation

The Placenta is a highly specialized organ. It nurtures the fetus when diffusion is no longer sufficient, protects it and secretes hormones, as it takes over the corpus luteum‘s function of progesterone secretion to maintain the endometrium from degenerating.

Fetal component: bushy chorion

  • Maternal component: decidua basalis

VASCULAR SYSTEM

Going back to 2nd week embryo:

By day 6, cells are already determined to form cells of the body of the embryo or of the trophoblast. The extraembryonic mesoderm and the 2 trophoblast layers will form the chorion. At the same time, at the end of the 2nd week primary villi appear (prolongations of the cytotrophoblast that penetrate into the syncytiotrophoblast).

During the 3rd week of development, a first vascular network is formed in the embryonic and extraembryonic mesoderm. It begins to form during the 3rd week to connect maternal vascularization with the embryo.

How does it form? Through vasculogenesis

A set of mesenchymal condensations (blood islands) in the extraembryonic mesoderm, surrounding the yolk sac, differentiate into hemangioblasts under the effect of FGF2

and differentiate into:

  • Epithelial cells, that will give rise to blood vessels.

- Embryonic blood cells

[PDF p. 112]

Blood vessels will grow through angiogenesis to form higher networks of blood vessels. They appear in other regions such as the connecting stalk and the embryonic mesoderm. Within the embryonic mesoderm, they start to form at both sides of the embryo. Initially form a dorsal aorta, from which primitive cardiac tubes arise and fuse to form the primitive heart by the 4th week. It starts to beat as soon as tubes fuse.

Formation of Chorionic vessels

Changes in the chorion during the 3rd week:

● Primary villus: Cytotrophoblastic prolongations towards the endometrium

  • Formation of secondary villus: during the 3rd week, extraembryonic mesoderm penetrates within the villus, forming a core of mesenchyme, along with cytotrophoblast and syncytiotrophoblast layers.

  • Formation of tertiary villus: days after, this mesenchyme will differentiate into hemangioblasts to eventually form blood cells and vessels. These will become known as chorionic blood vessels, as they arise from the chorion.

Cross section:

[PDF p. 113]

  • We can also observe other changes, such as separations between villi. Syncytiotrophoblast continues to erode and include maternal blood vessels, which surround the villi and increase the separation between them.

    • Formation of the cytotrophoblastic shell: cytotrophoblast cells continue to prolong and contact decidua basalis. From here, they extend to form the cytotrophoblastic shell, which anchors the whole future placenta into

the endometrium.

Close to this shell, we find anchored villis known as anchoring villis. On the other hand, free and unanchored villi will expand and branch a lot, and will constitute the site where

exchange takes place.

Within the chorionic vessels, we find:

    - **Chorionic arteries** = carrying out baby’s **deoxygenated blood**, which they

receive from the umbilical artery - Chorionic veins = bringing in oxygenated blood, draining it into the umbilical vein (note: the colour doesn’t signify the type of blood they carry, just that they are arteries or veins)

Vascularization at the end of the 4th week

A complex cardiovascular system is already formed and working by the end of the 4th week. Embryonic, umbilical

and chorionic vessels are formed and the cardiac tube is

beating by day 21 ‐ 22 of embryonic development. It’s the 1st system to be functional.

PLACENTA

As the embryo grows, the placenta becomes larger and more efficient through a complex maturation.

Primary and secondary villi form all around the embryo, but are more prominent and develop more at the embryonic pole (site where implantation started). By the end of the 2nd month, chorionic villi concentrate in the embryonic pole. The rest of the villi don’t develop much and finally disappear; only the mentioned ones fully develop into the placenta.

At the end of the 2nd month they concentrate mainly at one point, and we can distinguish a villish/bushy chorion and a smooth chorion (depleted of villi).

[PDF p. 114]

Embryo-Endometrium relation

During pregnancy, the endometrium becomes decidualized.

  • End of 2nd month: we can differentiate between decidua basalis, capsularis and parietalis, separated by uterine cavity. The embryo is found inside the amniotic cavity, which is at the same time within the chorionic cavity.

  • End of 3rd month: amniotic cavity/membrane becomes fused with the chorionic membrane, and they join to form the amniochorionic membrane, which is now the only membrane which is found surrounding the embryo. This membrane compresses against the decidua capsularis, which degenerates, and expands towards the uterine cavity. The embryo and amniotic cavity uptake the uterine cavity and will continue to push up against other organs.

In this process, the yolk sac and allantois become totally lined by the amnion. As the amniotic cavity expands and obliterates most of the extraembryonic coelom, the amnion forms the epithelial covering of

the umbilical cord.

At the same time, decidua basalis ends up penetrating within groups of villi and forms decidual septa. This maternal stroma penetrates and divides the villi into several groups. These rounded, isolated areas are known as cotyledons, and give place to the mature placenta.

[PDF p. 115]

Therefore, by the end of the 3rd month, the chorion frondosum and decidua basalis are tightly associated, forming the mature placenta.

Blood circulation in hemochorial placenta

Hemochorial placenta (human) = the blood is in contact with the chorion

Anchoring villi = no exchange

Free villi, expanding = exchange

The chorionic structure is found deep between lacunae of maternal blood, where maternal blood vessels are draining blood. The maternal blood is released at high pressures into these lacunae. It goes all the way down (due to high pressure) and returns slowly. Slower rate allows the exchange of nutrients, excretion materials, CO2,.. so it slowly returns deoxygenated. Decidual septa doesn’t fully separate cotyledons. Blood therefore also flows laterally, between cotyledons.

Placenta-functions

The placenta is the primary site of nutrient and gas exchange between the mother and

the fetus.

Exchanges gas and nutrients

  • Protection

  • Metabolism

Hormone secretion (hCG)

  • Excretion

Maturation of placental villi

Initially, separating maternal and fetal blood, we find 4 layers forming tertiary villi. Syncytium, cytotrophoblast, connective tissue and endothelium.

However, as the placenta matures, villi ramify, and these barriers become thinner and thinner with time. Only a few residual trophoblastic cells will remain: blood vessels of the fetus end up being embedded in 2-layered mature placental villi. But the 2 bloods are never in direct contact. They are always well separated by the placental

membrane.

[PDF p. 116]

➢ Complications. Placental membrane

Oxygen and other nutrients can easily flow through this membrane. IgG antibodies ( small) can also pass, and provide some immunity to the fetus.

However, harmful substances such as drugs, poisons,… and microorganisms like parasites and viruses, can also cross this membrane.

  • If the mother has a viral infection (ex: rubella or cytomegalovirus, parasites such as toxoplasma gondii (toxoplasmosis),…), it can be transmitted to the embryo.

  • Bacteria generally can’t pass, but some break the placenta membrane and can

cross.

  • Fetal RBC generally don’t pass through the membrane; although some might pass at the very end of gestation. If RBC from the fetus are Rh+ and the mother, Rh-, this RBC membrane-crossing induces a reaction and the production of maternal antibodies against fetal RBC. If, in the next pregnancy, she has another Rh+ baby, there will be a problem as the primary exposure during the 1st pregnancy means the mother has already developed antibodies. These

attack the RBC of the fetus. This condition is

known as HEMOLYTIC DISEASE OF THE

NEWBORN .

MULTIPLE PREGNANCIES

[PDF p. 117]

BIRTH DEFECTS

Types of birth defects

  • Malformations : occur during organ formation (organogenesis, 3rd-8th weeks)

Disruptions: alterations in already formed structures (vascular accidents, amniotic bands)

  • Deformations: mechanical forces which alter a structure ( clubfeet)

Teratology is the science that studies birth defects. Teratogens = factors that cause birth defects. They can be:

  • Genetic factors (15%)
  • Environmental factors (10%)

Causes of birth defects : 15% are Genetic, 10% environmental, 20% multifactorial and

50-60% remain unknown.

Genetic factors

1. Numerical anomalies (aneuploidies correspond to 50% of spontaneous abortions. They are mainly caused by nondisjunction during meiosis, wither in meiosis I or II)

    - Turner 's syndrome _(X0, 45. It’s the only_

monosomy compatible with life)

’ - Klinefelter s syndrome (47, XXY)

’ - Down s syndrome (trisomy 21)

’ - Edward s syndrome (trisomy 18)

2. Structural anomalies (ex: microdeletions)

’

    Angelman s syndrome _(microdeletion in the mother’s chromosome)_
    - Prader-Willis syndrome _(microdeletion in paternal chromosome)_

Environmental factors

Alcohol, Antiepileptic drugs, antibiotics (streptomycin, tetracycline), Endocrine disruptors, Radiation, Infections (viruses: Rubella, Zyka, CMV; Toxoplasma gondii, Treponema pallidum (syphilis)), Maternal diabetes, Obesity and Nutritional deficiencies.

Most structural birth defects are caused early in gestation (3 to 8 weeks), when organ systems are forming and when most women don’t know they’re pregnant.

[PDF p. 118]

Development of the nervous system

INTRODUCTION

The nervous system is in charge of all the other systems, making it possible for them to work in unison towards a common goal. How is this achieved? By being aware of the external environment and internal environment. The nervous system is able to gather information and transmit/convey it via the nerves (PNS) to the main centre of control (CNS, brain and spinal cord) which integrates the info and elaborates the most appropriate response. There are different types of responses, but we will mainly focus on motor responses. The response is sent to effector organs.

Main parts of the Nervous System

  • Central Nervous System (brain and spinal cord)

  • Peripheral Nervous System (mainly nerves, although we also have neurons).

Subdivided into:

    - Somatic: It’s related with the muscles and voluntary movement
    - Autonomous: visceral, non-voluntary movements

Functional unit of NS

Functional unit = the nerve cell or neuron.

Santiago Ramón y Cajal was the Spanish physician and early neuroscientist who first discovered that the nervous system was not continuous but was composed of interconnected individual cells. He

was awarded the Nobel Prize in 1906.

Nerve cells are the main signaling unit of the NS. Made up of:

- Dendrites (processes around cell body)

- Cell body

- Axon

Incoming information reaches these processes (dendrites), it’s integrated in the cell body and the response is conveyed via the axon to the next neuron or to a muscle cell (effector). This is done through a chemical synapse (release of chemical signals).

[PDF p. 119]

Other parts of the NS:

  • Glial cells: they outnumber the nerve cells. These are supporting cells, found around neurons within

nervous tissue.

How do we define the incoming and exciting

information?

Incoming information = perception

  • Efferent information = motor

FORMATION OF THE NERVOUS SYSTEM

Induction of the neural plate. Neurulation

  • At the beginning of 3rd week, notochord forms from cells migrating from the primitive streak through the primitive pit.
  • Ectoderm cells become polygonal, thicken and extend rostrally, forming the neural plate.
  • A neural groove forms, the plate elevates forming two neural folds which approach one another and end up fusning and invaginating, detaching from the overlying ectoderm, during the 4th week.

The ectoderm becomes continuous again. Therefore, the origin of the neural tube is the ectoderm.

It’s thought that the tube invaginates first at the rostral end, and then caudally. The structures that remain open in the final stages are the pores, at the rostral end and caudal end (known as anterior and posterior neuroscope ). The caudal end finally closes by the end of the 1st month, when the neural tube becomes fully invaginated.

Others think that the closure takes place at different places at the same time.

[PDF p. 120]

Neural tube defects at this stage

  • SPINA BIFIDA: defect caused by neural tube not closing properly, hence the vertebral arch doesn’t correctly form. This leads to different types of pathologies. Defect in the vertebral arch (unfused): it’s not continuous, has two tips = bifida.

    - **Spina bifida occulta:** In the **mildest** case, as it’s **asymptomatic** and doesn’t

really affect. There is incomplete closure but the tips are very proximate. The site of defect is usually marked by a tuft of hair.

Problems come when the formation comes with lack of proper closure. The content of the central canal (spinal cord within the meninges) protrudes, since there is no boundary.

    **Spina bifida cystica: cyst** formed by **protruding meningeal sac,** containing

cerebrospinal fluid. The meningeal sac is protruding towards the skin and forms a mass. This situation, in which only the sac is protruding, is known as meningocele.

    - **Meningomyelocele:** both the **meningeal sac and CNS structures** are found

displaced and disorganized, protruding outwards. Patients present more complications in walking, etc, but it’s a situation compatible with life.

    - **Rachischisis:** the **whole neural tube is open** and **exposed to amniotic**

fluid during formation (open spinal cord) . This situation is incompatible with

life.

Neural tube defects at separate sites: further evidence in support of

multi ‐ site closure of the neural tube

in humans. Triple neural tube defects.

  • ANENCEPHALY : cranial bones aren’t formed properly . The cerebral cortex doesn’t form, only the brain stem. The baby is born but dies hours later, as it is not compatible with life.

To avoid these defects (Defects in the neural tube closure, NTDs), women are given folic acid, which has been seen to prevent to a great extent neural developmental defects.

[PDF p. 121]

1. CENTRAL NERVOUS SYSTEM (CNS)

The CNS derives from the neural tube.

Brain

The anterior part is enlarged and expands, forming brain vesicles which will give rise to the

brain. 5 vesicles:

Telencephalon, will give rise to the cerebral hemispheres (cerebrum) - Diencephalon = thalamic areas + optic vesicle

  • Mesencephalon, will give rise to the midbrain
  • Metencephalon, that in the adult will give rise to the cerebellum

- Myelencephalon, which will give rise to the medulla oblongata

Spinal cord

Neuroepithelial cells form the wall of the neural tube. They derive from ectoderm cells that invaginate and end up forming pseudostratified epithelium, known as neuroepithelium. From this point, cells start dividing and proliferating. 3 stages in the formation of the spinal cord:

1. Proliferation

  • Undifferentiated neural stem cells start dividing close to the lumen of the tube, giving rise to immature neurons (neuroblasts) that will eventually differentiate.

The closely packed dividing cells form the ventricular layer (neuroepithelial layer), which is pseudostratified . Will be found lining the cavities in the brain.

[PDF p. 122]

2. Migration

Cells in the ventricular zone stop dividing and begin to migrate and leave the ventricular zone, forming a second layer known as the marginal layer.

  • As the wall thickens, an intermediate zone forms, known as the mantle layer.

  • The marginal zone is in contact with the somites (forming the meninges), which limit the amount of space available. Intermediate zone and marginal zone will quickly fill the neural canal and start differentiating into neurons and glial cells.

This neural canal, which becomes known as the central canal, is lined by ependymal

cells derived from the neural tube.

  • Once the mantle layer becomes more and more thick, axons are formed through

maturation.

  • Cells concentrate and mature in the mantle layer, and once matured their axons position themselves and flow towards the marginal layers (cranially and caudally).

Therefore:

 - **mantle layer** = we mainly find **cell bodies**
 - **marginal layer** = we mainly find **axons** . The marginal layer becomes **thicker**

at midgestation due to the myelination of the axons.

Neuroepithelial cells in the mantle layer will take on different roles depending on their position relative to the sulcus limitans:

 cells posterior to the sulcus = **alar plate** → will become sensory neurons
 - cells anterior to the sulcus = **basal plate** → will become motor neurons

  • During specialization and differentiation, alar plate will become known as the dorsal sensory horn and the basal plate, as the ventral motor horn

  • The cells in the ventricular layer which remain lining the ventricle will give rise to ependymal cells

[PDF p. 123]

3. Regional specialization-differentiation

The spinal cord has a specific organization. Notochord secretes molecules (ex: SHH) and induces differentiation in the lower part of the spinal cord. Cells begin to express particular genes and differentiate. It allows the cells to acquire the particular shape and functions of neurons from that particular region.

  • The alar plate of the mantle layer will give rise to neurons with a sensory function (afferent). The basal plate will originate the ventral layer, which will have a motor function (efferent).

  • Motor neurons from the motor horn will extend their axons (PNS) towards muscles. Sensory neurons in the dorsal horn (CNS) will receive info from sensory neurons that arise from neural crest cells (see below, explained). These belong to the PNS and will extend their processes out to the skin and other portions. These neurons are spinal nerves and make up the spinal system (PNS). We will also find interneurons that connect sensory to motor neurons, which will

allow us to have coordinated reflexes.

Many of the formed neurons enter apoptosis and die, as to make space for the supporting cells in the nervous tissue (glial cells), which also arise from the neuroepithelium

  • The mantle layer will become grey matter in the spinal cord The marginal laye r will give rise to white matter

2. PERIPHERAL NERVOUS SYSTEM (PNS)

Neural crest cells will give rise to dorsal root ganglia and sensory

neurons.

They are a specific population of cells located above the neural tube of the embryo. As the tube invaginates, they are found at the tips of the neural folds. When the folds fuse, these tips join and detach, forming the neural crest. The origin of these cells is therefore the ectoderm (ectodermal cells). These cells become mesenchymal (ectoderm to mesoderm transition). As they become mesenchymal, they will give rise to many cell types. To such an extent that they are sometimes referred to as the 4th germ layer.

[PDF p. 124]

The most important type of cell type they give rise to are all the sensory neurons in the body, those that receive information from the periphery.

All the soma of those sensory neurons are located within sensory ganglia (dorsal root ganglia, containing the soma). Sensory neurons are a special type of neuron: they have two processes, are pseudounipolar (Why pseudounipolar? because exiting the body we see only one process, which then splits into two). They have:

  • A central process (synapse region with sensory neurons in dorsal horn)

Peripheral process (going towards the periphery, join motor fibers to form spinal nerves)

Derivatives of neural crest cells

Many other cell types are derived from neural crest cells:

Melanocytes

- Cells from autonomous nervous system

Sensory neurons (already mentioned)

  • Schwann cells (form the myelin sheath in the PNS). Oligodendrocytes myelinate axons in the CNS (1 oligodendrocyte can give rise to many internodes) and Schwann cells myelinate axons in the PNS (1 SC per internode).

[PDF p. 125]

Spinal nerves

There are 2 ganglia, one on each side at every segment of the spinal cord (segments = sets of neurons at each level of the spinal cord).

The spinal nerve comprises:

The peripheral processes of sensory neurons coming from many parts of the body. Incoming neuron (sensory) = dorsal or posterior root

  • The axons of motor neurons. Exting (motor) = ventral or anterior root

Spinal nerves are mixed, containing sensory fibers (axons) and motor fibers.

As they approach the spinal cord they split, as sensory neurons have to reach the dorsal horn and motor neurons are exiting from the

ventral horn.

The spinal cord ends at the level of L3. But we also have the sacral cord. They have to travel long distances to reach their corresponding foramina and exit to innervate many parts of the body. This is why myelination will be vital. Myelination starts around the 4th month of gestation.

[PDF p. 126]

Development of axial skeleton and muscles of the trunk

All these structures are forming at the same time

INTRODUCTION

The skeletal system is composed of bones and joints. It’s dense connective tissue (bone tissue). Associated to tissues, cartilage and ligaments.

  • Axial skeleton = vertebral column, ribs and cranium. The trunk

is ribs + spinal cord + sternum

  • Appendicular skeleton = the rest

EMBRYONIC ORIGIN OF THE AXIAL SKELETAL SYSTEM

Metamerism or metamery: repetition of alike elements (segments)

In the embryo, the somites form structures that resemble the axial skeleton. They look like vertebrae. The vertebrae will come from somites, which are derived from paraxial mesoderm in the embryo. Therefore, mesenchyme giving rise to the axial skeleton of the trunk arises mainly from the paraxial mesoderm (somites). Somites are metameric structures, repeated elements that are similar along the body of the embryo. Many structures have this metameric disposition, and many maintain this metameric, segmentary organization, such as the vertebrae and the ribs. This is so because they originate from somites.

The number of somites increases from 3rd to 5th week, when they start to disappear . They will give rise to:

  • Axial skeleton (vertebral bodies and ribs)

- Portion of striated muscle

- Portion of skin

Spinal nerves will be innervating these somitic derivatives (somatic = derivative of somitic).

Every somite will give rise to a portion of skin, which will be innervated by the corresponding spinal nerve.

  • Correspondingly, a portion of muscle will be innervated by the spinal nerve of the corresponding segment. (neurologists know where to explore nerve innervation, which spinal nerve they are activating. Person cannot flex their thumb = lesion in C6)

[PDF p. 127]

Transition from ectoderm to mesenchyme

Somites are mesoderm which acquires a roundish structure. During the 4th week of development, the ventral portion of the somites decondensate and become mesenchymal, forming the many regions, one of them being the sclerotome We can also find the dermatome (→skin) and the dorsal and medial muscle cells.

Sclerotome : sclero- = hard, hardened; - tome = segment. Sclerotome is the portion of somite-derived mesenchymal cells which will give rise to bone structures.

The somite cells are mesenchymal and can therefore migrate.

  • They migrate and come together surrounding the notochord. They condensate around the notochord and will form the body of the vertebrae.
  • These cells will also migrate and surround the neural tube, forming the vertebral arch which will enclose the neural tube, giving rise to the vertebral canal.

Vertebral body formation enables the passage of spinal nerves between every two

vertebrae to innervate the somitic derivatives.

[PDF p. 128]

Ossification

Long & short bones undergo endochondral ossification at diaphysis. Flat bones undergo intramembranous ossification* . Ossification begins by the 2nd month:

  • At the 7th week, the whole vertebral column is cartilaginous and begins to ossify at primary

ossification centers.

  • At birth, the whole vertebral column is ossified (except for some portions of cartilage at the terminal ends)
  • After birth new ossification centers appear, secondary ossification centers, enabling growth of the vertebral body during childhood and puberty.
  • Ossification ends around 25 years of age.

Irregular bones like vertebra have one or more primary ossification centers:

What determines the shape/type of vertebrae (cervical, thoracic, lumbar,…)?

The patterning of the rostrocaudal axis of the body is regulated by Homeobox genes. Homeobox genes control the differentiation of each vertebra. The specific combination of these genes that every vertebra expresses is what causes them to be different, as they confer them with fundamental regional characteristics.

Anomalies in the axial skeleton

  • Vertebral column : there are vertebral anomalies associated with neural tube

defects (NTDs), such as incomplete closure of the vertebral arch that leads to spina bifida.

Other anomalies of the axial skeleton include:

  • Ribs: they also originate from the somites and

sclerotome

    - **Cervical rib:** An additional rib is found at the

cervical level, which can cause problems in breathing. - Hemivertebra: the vertebral column is bent,

giving rise to scoliosis

- Forked rib

[PDF p. 129]

  • Sternum: the sternum arises from the parietal mesoderm located at the ventral-most part. 2 mesenchymal condensations fuse and join the costal formations, where 2 sternal bars are formed and ossification centers arise. Malformation: Pectus excavatum : there is strong evidence of autosomal recessive genetic control.

DEVELOPMENT OF THE MUSCLES IN THE TRUNK

Striated muscle of the trunk and limbs originate from the myotome that derives from somites. Therefore they ultimately derive from the somites.

Muscles in the trunk, at least initially, have a segmentary organization. Some maintain that segmentary organization in adult age: for instance, intercostal muscles, as they are found next to the ribs, which during development make it difficult for muscles to fuse and mesh together, maintaining them separated in their initial segmentary disposition.

Skeletal muscle and dermis originate from the dorsolateral portion of the somites, which differentiates into myotome and dermatome. This is why this dorsolateral portion is known as dermo-myotome, as one part will give rise to a portion of dermis (dermatome) and another, to a portion of muscle (myotome)

The part of the somite that will give rise to muscle are the dorsomedial and ventrolateral regions of that embryonic dorsolateral portion.

Dorsomedial and ventrolateral portions

We have a dorsomedial region

A ventrolateral region Each portion expresses different genes that will give rise to different muscles:

In the dorsomedial region: myoblasts won’t migrate very

far and stay close to the vertebral column. This portion of muscle will give rise to the intrinsic muscles of the back. They arise from this epaxial portion / dorsomedial portion of the myotome. Will allow to extend the back.

  • In the ventrolateral portion: cells will migrate and form the

muscles of the anterolateral walls of the body (hypaxial muscle). At this level, we find muscles such as the

intercostal muscles and rectum muscles.

[PDF p. 130]

Each portion of the somite is innervated by 1 spinal nerve, very soon in development. A functional unit is formed very early on. As myoblasts migrate, those from the same myotome carry with them their corresponding segmentary nerve .

To know where a muscle originated from, we can know by paying attention to which nerve innervates it. In this way we can know from which somite it arose/which portion of myotome.

Spinal nerves

Spinal nerves contain both motor and sensory

neurons.

  • Motor neurons originate from the spinal cord (neural tube) and extend their

axons to innervate effector muscle cells

  • Sensory neurons arise from neural crest cells which migrate and concentrate in ganglia. They transmit signals coming from the corresponding portion of skin

The muscles will have the motor fibers, and the corresponding portion of skin which originated from the same somite will be innervated by the sensory neuron of said spinal

nerve.

Epaxial and hypaxial muscle innervation

2 sets of muscle: Epaxial (back) Hypaxial (anterolateral). The spinal nerve branches into 2 rami, a dorsal one and a ventral one.

  • Epaxial muscles (intrinsic muscles of the back) are innervated by dorsal rami of spinal nerves

- Hypaxial muscles ( muscles of the anterolateral walls and muscles of the limbs) are innervated by ventral rami of spinal nerves

The dorsal ramus is thinner as it innervates many less structures than the ventral one. Both ramus contain sensory and motor neurons.

[PDF p. 131]

Congenital malformations regarding the muscles of the trunk

Children with prune-belly syndrome: hypoplasia or absence (aplasia) of

abdominal musculature.

The belly is distended as the organs are not enclosed by the muscles of the

trunk.

It’s a rare disorder characterized by the following triad of anomalies:

  • partial or complete absence of the abdominal muscles (hypoplasia or aplasia)
  • failure of both testes to descend into the scrotum (bilateral cryptorchidism)
  • and/or urinary tract malformations

* What is the difference between intramembranous and endochondral ossification?

- Intramembranous ossification is the direct deposition of bone on thin layers of connective tissue and is characteristic of the bones on the top of the skull (flat bones)

- In endochondral ossification, cartilage is present: endochondral ossification involves the replacement of hyaline cartilage with bony tissue. Most of the bones of the skeleton are formed in this manner. Involves primary and secondary ossification centers, and allows bone growth through epiphyseal plates where cartilage remains

un-ossified.

[PDF p. 132]

Development of the appendicular skeleton and limb formation

LIMB FORMATION

Limbs are formed between the 5th and 8th week of development. Almost totally formed by the end of the 2nd month. Limb buds however appear by the end of the 4th week (28days), at this stage they can already be appreciated on the embryo.

What are they made of? They are an evagination of the lateral aspect of the embryo: therefore, they are made up of a chore of mesenchyme (coming from the parietal layer of lateral mesoderm) lined by a layer of ectoderm.

Very early on, a thickening of the ectoderm forms at the tip of the limb bud. This tip becomes known as the apical ectodermal ridge (AER), which is the structure that induces the outgrowth of the limb (outward, distal pull).

Molecular regulation of patterning and growth in the limb

The growth is molecularly regulated. 3 axis:

Proximodistal axis

The apical ectodermal ridge (AER) promotes limb growth in the proximodistal direction.

  • FGF10 secreted by lateral plate mesoderm (underneath the ectoderm layer) in the limb forming regions leads to the formation of the AER
  • The ridge secretes molecules (FGF4 and FGF8) that induce proliferation of the mesenchyme in areas proximal to the ridge , such that these cells proliferate and remain undifferentiated .
  • As the limb grows, the most proximal part, not under the action of the ridge, starts differentiating.

It follows a proximal-distal differentiation.

[PDF p. 133]

Dorsoventral limb axis

There is also a dorsal-ventral axis. It is directed by WNT-7, which is expressed in the dorsal ectoderm, specifying these cells as dorsal.

Anteroposterior axis (what is the thumb, etc…), determined by SHH, with a mediolateral differentiation (from the medial towards the lateral side, from pinky to thumb)

However it’s the HOX genes that regulate the regional patterning of the limb. In other words, the shapes and types of bones in the limb.

Development of the limb buds

  • 4th week: around day 28, limb buds begin to appear at the end of the 4th week. The upper limb is slightly ahead to the lower (3 days ahead).

  • 5 week embryo : all limb buds are clearly visible at this point

  • 6th week: the limb develops and forms a flat end, the hand plate in the upper limbs and foot plate in the lower limb. We see thickenings within the hand plate, elevations known as the digital rays (future fingers). We also see a distal constriction. (externally we first see differentiation in the distal, growing apical area)

  • 7th week: digital rays appear in the footplate. The fingers in hands and feet are slightly separated and we can begin to see the elbow and the knee.

Also during this 7th week, the upper and lower limbs rotate in opposite

directions.

Initially, thumb and toe are pointing upwards.

➢ The upper limb will rotate laterally 90 degrees (supine anatomical view),

implying:

    - The **elbow will be facing dorsally**
    - The **extensor muscles** will be located **posteriorly**

➢ The lower limb rotates medially almost 90 degrees.

    - The **knee** is **facing anteriorly**
    - **Extensor muscle** s lie **anteriorly**

○ 8th week: in the 8-week embryo, we see the rotated limbs, and separate digits in hands and feet

How do the fingers separate?

Due to induced apoptosis . Programmed

cell death causes the destruction of

the interdigital spaces, and fingers become fully separated.

[PDF p. 134]

Maximum point of vulnerability in relation to limb formation:

Between the 3rd and 8th week, during their period of formation. The defect will be more severe during the 4th-5th week as damage here will cause the limb to maybe not form at all. Defects further along in development will have more mild consequences.

Congenital defects

  • MEROMELIA: congenital defect due to exposure to teratogens like thalidomide. Characterized by a partial absence of a limb.

Children born with limb abnormalities between 1957 a 1962 in

Europe after thalidomide (antiemetic) intake by pregnant women between weeks 3-5 of gestation

  • AMELIA : full absence of the limb. Most are due to genetic factors (limb defect)

● CLEFT HANDS/FEET: autosomal dominant abnormality with incomplete penetrance (limb defect)

  • POLYDACTYLY : extra finger. Polydactyly is inherited as a dominant trait (digital birth defect)

  • SYNDACTYLY : interdigital membrane which has not been fully removed (digital birth defect) . Can be resolved with surgical intervention

  • CLUBFEET, the most common musculoskeletal defect. Appears to be caused by multifactorial inheritance (genetic and environmental factors acting together). It’s postnatally reversible.

[PDF p. 135]

DEVELOPMENT OF THE APPENDICULAR SKELETON

The appendicular skeleton originates from mesenchymal cells arising from the parietal layer of the lateral mesoderm

  • During the 5th week, mesenchymal bones arise in the limbs

  • During the 6th week, mesenchymal bone models in the limbs undergo chondrification to form hyaline cartilage bone models. Bones will become cartilaginous.

○ During the 7th week, bones continue to develop as cartilaginous structures

  • At the 8th week, ossification begins in the long bones in primary ossification centers, and initially occurs in the diaphyses of the bones.

In a 12-week fetus, primary ossification centers are present in nearly all

bones of the limbs.

Secondary ossification centers appear after birth.

Skeletal malformations

In a 3 month fetus, there are primary ossification centers (black) in all bones of the body. Secondary ossification centers appear afterwards, in the epiphysis, from where bones will grow. Disruptions during these ossification processes lead to malformations:

● ACHONDROPLASIA is caused by a dominant autosomal mutation in the receptor fibroblast growth factor 3 (FGFR3). Consists in a disturbance of endochondral ossification during fetal life at the epiphyseal cartilage plates, particularly of long bones.

● OSTEOGENESIS IMPERFECTA: autosomal mutation, dominant or recessive, for the gen of type I collagen. It results in extreme bone fragility with spontaneous

fractures.

[PDF p. 136]

SKELETAL MUSCLES IN THE LIMB

Skeletal muscles in the limbs originate from the somites, specifically from the ventrolateral myotome. These cells, which migrate for the original dermo-myotome, also expand into the growing limb buds and extend with them (hypaxial muscle) . Give rise to flexor and extensor

muscles in the limb.

However, as they enter into the limb, the muscles fuse together forming muscle bands and lose their segmentary organization.

As this is hypaxial muscle, the muscles in the limbs will be innervated by ventral rami. The nerves also extend with the migrating cells and combine, forming plexuses . We will find networks / plexuses of ventral rami that have fused to finally form terminal

nerves in the limbs.

  • Brachial plexus: made up of ventral rami of spinal nerves C5-T1 - Lumbosacral plexus: made up of ventral rami of spinal nerves L2-S2

Dermatome in the limbs

In contrast to the skeletal muscle in the limbs, the dermatome cells in the limb maintain

their segmentary pattern. Although regions are slightly overlapped, they are well

differentiated.

Therefore, sensory innervation to the limb maintains a segmental pattern, reflecting the embryological origin of each dermatome and its innervation. Forelimbs with their sensory innervation to the dermatomes represented:

There is also a cutaneous distribution of the

dorsal rami (towards the intrinsic muscles in the back+dermatome) of the spinal nerves.

VASCULARIZATION

Blood vessels follow the same trajectory and accompany the spinal nerves in extension during muscle cell migration.

Blood vessels receive sympathetic innervation:

                           - Skeletal muscle bv = Ach

                           - Rest of bv = adrenaline and noradrenaline

[PDF p. 137]

Autonomous nervous system

Aside from the somatic nervous system, found innervating structures derived from somites, we will also have an autonomous nervous system (visceral plexuses) . It innervates smooth muscle in glands and viscera . It’s not a voluntary muscle. (Gray’s anatomy book. Final part) Somatic plexuses = striated muscle; visceral plexuses = muscles in viscera and glands The Autonomous nervous system is further divided into Sympathetic (S) and parasympathetic (PS) nervous systems.

Where does smooth muscle come from?

It originates from the splanchnic layer of the lateral mesoderm. The parietal/somatic mesoderm lines the body walls and this other layer surrounds the endoderm of the gut, the forming endocardium, and

other viscera.

  • The endoderm will give rise to the mucosa of the GI tract, whereas
  • The smooth muscle that forms the walls of the gut originates from the splanchnic layer of the lateral mesoderm.

Di�erences between somatic and autonomous system

  • Somatic or spinal nerves innervate striated muscle via one

motor neuron.

  • Autonomic nerves innervate smooth muscle via two

neurons.

Autonomic nerves have second neurons:

  • The 2nd neuron of the SNA is always located in ganglia, and is therefore found originating outside the CNS. We therefore call it postganglionic neuron. The nerve (accumulation of fibers) will be referred to as postganglionic fiber. It makes reference to the trajectory followed by the axons.
  • The 1st neuron is located in the CNS (spinal cord or brain stem) and activates the 2nd neuron, located in ganglion. It’s known as preganglionic neuron, and its fiber, preganglionic fiber, as its synapse occurs in the ganglion.

Where do those ganglionic neurons come from?

They come from the neural crest cells. NCCs give rise to the ganglion of sympathetic trunk where the somas of these neurons will be found, and from which their processes will extend. Axons of the CNS finally target and penetrate them to be in connection. Neural crest cells will give rise to both Sympathetic and Parasympathetic ganglionic neurons.

[PDF p. 138]

The autonomic nervous system is made up of afferent branches (sensory) and efferent branches (motor).

VISCEROMOTOR FIBERS

These efferent visceromotor branches belong either to the Sympathetic or Parasympathetic SNA. They are both characterized by having autonomic nerves, made up of 2 neurons (pre and post ganglionic). It is a two neuron motor system:

preganglionic neuron in the brainstem or spinal cord

postganglionic neuron in a ganglion. The postganglionic axon synapses with its target.

Sympathetic and parasympathetic systems

  • Sympathetic system : fight and flight response. It prepares the body for this situation, by activating organs in order to respond to a danger/challenge. Involves the activation of muscles, change to far sight rather than near site (dilates pupils), increased heart rate, inhibits digestion…

Parasympathetic: “rest and digest” response It involves stimulation of digestion, slow contraction, reduces blood flow to skeletal muscle,… Near sight is also facilitated by the activation of the parasympathetic system (pupil constriction).

Each of these two systems are complementary, but have different effects.

Location of preganglionic neurons

Preganglionic axons exit the spinal cord through the ventral root with other motor axons. Where are they found? In the CNS .

Parasympathetic system: preganglionic cells originating from the brainstem which innervate the visceral wall (through the vagus nerve mainly), and preganglionic cells located at the sacral level (S2-S4).

We will see more about them later.

  • Sympathetic system: whole column of preganglionic cells along the spinal cord (originate from T1 to L2 ).

a) SYMPATHETIC SYSTEM

Sympathetic ganglia

We find 2 sets of sympathetic ganglia: 1- Sympathetic trunk or chain: a series of paired ganglia at each side/level of the spinal cord, found forming a chain. Also known as paravertebral.

2 - Preaortic single ganglia: located ventral to the aorta (= preaortic). They are located in the abdominal and pelvic cavities. Also known as prevertebral. These ganglia are located beneath the diaphragm, in the mentioned cavities.

[PDF p. 139]

Preganglionic sympathetic axons will synapse either in sympathetic chain ganglia (paravertebral ganglia) or preaortic/prevertebral ganglia. The Postganglionic sympathetic axons will then go and synapse with blood vessels, glands and other tissues throughout the body.

Preganglionic sympathetic neurons

Preganglionic sympathetic neurons originate in the intermediolateral cell column (lateral horn) in thoracic and upper lumbar spinal cord (spinal segments T1 to L2 ). Only these portions have said intermediate horn. Therefore, in order for upper and lower postganglionic neurons to be innervated, preganglionic neurons will have to originate in the intermediate horns at T1-L2 levels, and travel through the sympathetic trunk to reach their corresponding ganglion.

Therefore, we will see that preganglionic axons can ascend or descend in the sympathetic chain on the sides of the vertebrae, depending on which ganglion they are targeting.

What viscera will they innervate?

  • Dermal smooth muscle: there are other viscera that are only innervated by the sympathetic nervous system: the dermis, such as the dermis in sweat glands, arrector pili muscle (hair follicles) and blood vessels (endothelium). The postganglionic neuron originates at the sympathetic trunk ganglia .
  • Thoracic viscera: the synapse between pre and postganglionic neurons won’t occur in preaortic single ganglia. Therefore their 2nd effector neurons will be located in the sympathetic chain. The postganglionic fibers form cardiac nerves.
  • Abdominal viscera : They will have their motor neurons located in preaortic single ganglia. The long preganglionic fibers will form splanchnic nerves (thoracic and lumbar)

Dermis, sweat glands, arrector pili → sympathetic trunk

Thoracic viscera → sympathetic trunk. Cardiac nerves = postganglionic

Abdominal and pelvic viscera → preaortic single ganglia. Splanchnic nerves = preganglionic

[PDF p. 140]

As the distance between the trunk and preaortic ganglia is small in the SNS (both are very close to the spinal cord):

- preganglionic fibers are quite short

postganglionic fibers will be very long and extended.

The preganglionic fiber is myelinated, so it has a whitish color. These myelinated preganglionic fibers join together to form a cord, known as the white communicating ramus = all the nervous cords that are connecting the sympathetic ganglia to the spinal cord. The second, visceromotor neurons, the postganglionic neurons whose soma originates in the ganglion are unmyelinated. When they exit the ganglion they form another cord known as the gray communicating ramus = unmyelinated, so they have a Gray-ish

color.

Sympathetic fibers from T1-L2. Dermal smooth muscle Innervation of dermal smooth muscle . It’s innervated exclusively by Sympathetic fibers. Bodies of the preganglionic neurons originate in a special horn in the spinal cord: the intermediate horn / lateral horn. This horn is found only from T1 to L2. It contains sympathetic preganglionic visceromotor neurons. As it is a motor, it will take the ventral root to exit and extend towards the neuron it has to connect with, which is in the ganglion. Therefore it exits the nerve and enters the ganglionic chain through the white communicating ramus. Here, the postganglionic fiber exits and overlaps:

  • If it goes towards the back, it will take the dorsal ramus. These glands in the dorsal portion (back) also have a segmentary distribution as they are in every portion of skin. Therefore, these postganglionic nerves follow the same route as spinal nerves, taking the easiest route, in order to reach sweat glands, hair follicles and blood vessels of that part of the dermis.
  • If it goes towards the front, it will take the ventral ramus. These visceromotor sympathetic neurons exit along with many other motor neurons, going to innervate other bodily

structures.

Dermal sympathetic fibers beyond T1-L2 What happens above T1 or below L2? We don’t have preganglionic fibers entering the corresponding ganglion of the sympathetic chain, as at these levels of the spinal cord, we don’t find an intermediate horn. Therefore, no preganglionic fiber.

Who innervates the postganglionic fiber then? For this purpose, preganglionic neurons originating at T1 go into their corresponding ganglion; but instead of innervating the postganglionic neurons at that level, they go up to innervate

these others that are at levels that lack intermediate horn. If it

were at a lumbar level, it would go down from L2 level to connect with the lower sacral ganglia.

[PDF p. 141]

They enter the ganglionic chain and either go up or down to innervate postganglionic

nerves which are at a level in which there is no intermediate horn.

Sympathetic innervation of thoracic viscera

The thoracic viscera are innervated through both the sympathetic and the parasympathetic divisions of the autonomic nervous system. The sympathetic nerves in question arise from the cervical and thoracic sympathetic trunk ganglia. At the thoracic level we have an intermediate horn. The postganglionic fiber/axon directly exits the ganglion. It won’t follow the route taken by spinal nerves ***** as it is not going to innervate muscles of the back,…etc, but rather thoracic viscera (such as the heart, esophagus,… ). All together, the postganglionic nerves which arise from the sympathetic trunk are called cardiac sympathetic nerves, which join to form the cardiac sympathetic plexus. ***** Postganglionic fibers which go to innervate smooth muscle in peripheral dermis and body walls do follow the same route as spinal nerves = exit through dorsal and ventral rami

Sympathetic innervation of abdominal and pelvic viscera

They do have single preaortic ganglia. The preganglionic nerve will enter the sympathetic trunk but exit the ganglion to directly enter the preaortic single ganglion, where the postganglionic neuron is found. There, they will synapse. These preganglionic neurons which exit the sympathetic trunk and meet up in preaortic ganglia are known as splanchnic nerves, (greater splanchnic, lesser splanchnic and least splanchnic plexuses). We find both thoracic splanchnic (innervating abdomen) and lumbar splanchnic. All these are splanchnic nerves = preganglionic fibers. Sympathetic nerves generally enter the viscera together with the blood vessels. This is so because all the arteries that also go to the kidneys, to the gut,… are exclusively innervated by sympathetic nerves. These are visceromotor neurons.

[PDF p. 142]

b) PARASYMPATHETIC SYSTEM

Parasympathetic ganglia Parasympathetic ganglia are generally only seen microscopically . They’re located very close to the viscera or even in the wall of the viscera itself. Therefore, in the PS:

  • Axons of preganglionic neurons are very long
  • Postganglionic fibers will be quite short

Parasympathetic preganglionic neurons The preganglionic neurons will be located in:

  • The brainstem

  • In the spinal cord from S2 to S4. The postganglionic neurons, as they’re very close to the viscera, have very short axons.

Innervation from the brainstem

We have one nerve, the vagus nerve, which exits via the jugular foramen, passess through the neck and provides parasympathetic innervation for abdominal viscera and

thoracic viscera.

It slows down the heart rate, contracts the bronchi,… and

stimulates the GI tract.

It innervates all the way down to the distal third of the colon, and is therefore “in charge” of ⅔ of the transverse colon (AC, TC, DC). Therefore, it innervates thoracic and abdominal viscera all the way to the Transverse Colon (TC). (There are also other clusters of neurons innervating facial structures, which as well as the vagus, also arise in the brain stem. But we will focus on the vagus nerve).

Innervation from S2-24

Preganglionic fibers arising from S2-S4 are known as pelvic nerves. They innervate the rest of the GI tract (remaining abdominal viscera: AC and DC + ⅓ of TC) and pelvic viscera, the remaining structures. We call these nerves pelvic splanchnic nerves (don’t mix with thoracic and lumbar splanchnic, from the sympathetic). These pelvic splanchnic nerves are preganglionic fibers which reach the bladder, rectum, prostate in males, clitoris in females,… they are involved in erection, therefore also known as erogenous nerves. These nerves form the inf. hypogastric plexus.

                     - PS innervation of the abdominal viscera: There is a

whole network of tiny ganglia containing postganglionic neurons and fibers which stimulate peristaltism .

[PDF p. 143]

PS innervation of pelvic viscera: there is no intermediolateral horn in S2-S4, but we do have motor neurons. They exit from the motor horn, go through the ventral root and exit it. They follow the route towards the midline and innervate for example, the bladder, to trigger and regulate micturition.

VISCEROSENSORY FIBERS

Sympathetic and parasympathetic systems stimulate smooth muscle. But viscera also have sensory innervation (stomach pain, menstrual pain,…). We will also find sensory fibers conveying information about pain or how full the viscera are (distention in the stomach, rectum,..)

2 modalities of sensory innervation:

- Pain

- Distention

[PDF p. 144]

Trajectory of these viscerosensory fibers

  • Pain: as a general rule, pain information travels with motor sympathetic fibers (although in the opposite direction ). They generally accompany the sympathetic system in the opposite direction, except for pelvic pain which will accompany the

PS.

Therefore, they will convey pain information and reach the CNS, first through white communicating rami and then through the dorsal root.

  • Distention: distention information accompanies Parasympathetic fibers ( vagus nerve and other cranial nerves) in the opposite direction. They

enter the CNS at the brainstem and sacral level.

Distension information

Therefore, distension sensory info from thoracic viscera, abdominal viscera, till the distal third of TC will travel following the vagus nerve.

Distension information of the pelvic viscera travels with the pelvic splanchnic nerves. Pain in pelvic viscera will also follow this trajectory, accompanying the pelvic splanchnic

nerve.

Pain information

Pain information is conveyed by sensory neurons accompanying Sympathetic nervous system. It will accompany either cardiac nerves, thoracic splanchnic or lumbar splanchnic nerves, in the opposite direction. They reach the spinal nerve via the white communicating ramus.

As said, this is so except for pelvic pain, which follows the same route as pelvic splanchnic nerves in the PS.

Where are their sensory neurons located? Sensory neurons are ALWAYS located in sensory ganglia, in this case, in the ganglia prior to entering the jugular nerve which contains all the bodies of the sensory neurons. In the brain stem, it makes synapse in an area similar to the dorsal horn.

Chroma�n cells

Some neural crest cells enter adrenal structure and stay there (chromaffin cells). These cells release norepinephrine and epinephrine to the bloodstream upon stimulation, as if it were a sympathetic ganglion located in the adrenal gland. When activated, the adrenal medulla releases epinephrine and norepinephrine into the blood, which potentiates sympathetic activity throughout the body. Needed to coordinate the bodily “fight-or-flight” response to fear, stress, exercise, or conflict.

[PDF p. 145]

NEUROTRANSMITTERS

All preganglionic fibers release Acetylcholine (Ach) neurotransmitter (cholinergic). The neurotransmitters vary between the postganglionic fibers of the Sympathetic and Parasympathetic nervous systems:

Sympathetic neurotransmitters

As said, the preganglionic fiber will release Ach

- The second neurotransmitter will generally be Norepinephrine and Epinephrine (adrenergic), as is the case of arrector pili muscles, blood vessels of the skin,…

  • However there are exceptions: postganglionic sympathetic fibers innervating sweat glands and blood vessels in skeletal muscle will release Ach

Adrenal medulla

Adrenal glands are innervated by preganglionic sympathetic neurons. They receive stimulation through the release of Ach, and they themselves release Norepinephrine and Epinephrine into the bloodstream. They are not postganglionic neurons, but are considered as such as they fulfill the same function, with the exception that instead of innervating smooth muscle, they release their neurotransmitters into the blood. Adrenal glands are adrenergic .

Parasympathetic neurotransmitters

The preganglionic fiber will release Ach. Here in the PS, the postganglionic fiber will also release Ach to innervate smooth muscle (cholinergic)

[PDF p. 146]

TBL. Autonomous Nervous System

  • Soma of sensory neurons are located in ganglia of spinal nerves, which then enter the dorsal horn to convey sensory information

Sympathetic and parasympathetic neurons are visceromotor : they convey motor

information to smooth muscle of the viscera

  • Pain information is conveyed through sensory fibers which travel along with sympathetic nerves (cardiac, thoracic splanchnic and lumbar splanchnic). This is so except for pelvic pain, which travels along with nerves from the parasympathetic system (S2-S4). Distention information travels along with the PS (pelvic splanchnic nerves and vagus nerves)

    Cardiac nerves = mainly innervate heart + esophagus

    Thoracic splanchnic = abdominal viscera

    Lumbar splanchnic = lower abdominal and pelvic viscera Intercostal nerves are somatic, and innervate intercostal striated muscle

All preganglionic fibers release Ach. Preganglionic are all cholinergic (Ach)

    Sympathetic system = postganglionic fibers release **norepinephrine and**

epinephrine neurotransmitters. They are adrenergic. Chromaffin cells act as postganglionic sympathetic fibers, as they release norepinephrine and epinephrine.

    **Parasympathetic neurotransmitters** = postganglionic fibers release

acetylcholine . Ach = both for the pre and post ganglionic PS fibers. They are cholinergic.

  • The vascular system is innervated exclusively by the sympathetic system. Heart and lungs both receive parasympathetic stimulation in addition to the sympathetic.
  • An effect of the vagus nerve stimulation = triggers rest and digest response. Will induce acid release in the stomach to carry out digestion (gastrocolic reflex)
  • The substance majorly released by the adrenal medulla is Epinephrine . Adrenal medulla secretes 80% of epinephrine, 20% of Norepinephrine. Majorly epinephrine
  • Sweat glands are only innervated by the sympathetic system. They are special, an exception: they use Ach as second neurotransmitter, for postganglionic

innervation

CLINICAL CASE

A 27-year-old pregnant woman is delivering her first child. In the last stage of labor the anesthetist carries out a lumbar puncture and administers a local anesthetic through a catheter located in the epidural space. The woman experiences immediate relief and thanks

the anesthetist for the intervention.

Before the delivery of the anesthetic the arterial pressure was 130/85 mmHg Heart rate, 98 beats per minute.

After 20 min the arterial pressure is 80/45 mmHg and the heart rate 120 bpm and the patient feels dizzy. epidural = space between the vertebral arch. Anesthesia is administered there to block sensory information.

[PDF p. 147]

What complication has appeared as a result of the anesthesia? Arterial hypotension as a direct consequence. Anesthesia has the intention of blocking sensory information, but has had other effects. The consequence that the anesthesia has brought this patient is:

Arterial hypotension (low bp)

Tachycardia: heart rate increases to compensate the hypotension, to maintain blood supply to all the organs

Why is the woman suffering from hypotension? Because the anesthesia has reached the sympathetic system, which is found innervating

smooth muscle in the walls of the vessels.

The vascular smooth muscle is innervated only by the sympathetic system which is normally activated at a basal level. This small muscle contraction is needed to maintain a constant pressure in the vessels, by keeping them constricted. Inhibition of the sympathetic nerves after the epidural anesthesia decreases the stimulation of alpha1 receptors in the arterioles, increasing the diameter of arterioles and leading to arterial hypotension.

By trying to block the afferent sensory system, we somehow block the motor sympathetic nerves. The vascular smooth muscle is normally activated, to induce a basal sympathetic tone. If inhibited, it induces ↓ in neurotransmitter release (alpha1 receptors) = smooth muscle in the vessels relaxes = increase in arterial diameter = ↓ bp = hypotension

Anatomically, how has this happened?

  1. Epidural space

  2. Dorsal root (goes towards the dorsal horn) + dorsal root ganglia

  3. Ventral root (towards ventral horn)

  4. Ventral ramus of the spinal nerve spinal nerve

  5. Dorsal ramus of the spinal nerve

  6. Lateral/intermediate horn

  7. White communicating ramus (preganglionic, myelinated)

  8. Grey communicating ramus (postganglionic, unmyelinated)

  9. Sympathetic trunk ganglia

What neurons have been affected by the anesthetic, what neurotransmitter do they use and where do they make synapse?

- Anesthesia is blocking the synapse between the preganglionic and postganglionic sympathetic fibers.

acetylcholine (all preganglionic fibers release Ach)

Ganglionic neurons of the sympathetic chain

Where else may ganglionic neurons of the sympathetic system be located?

At preaortic ganglia, next to the main branches of the abdominal aorta

  • As chromaffin cells in the adrenal medulla

What drug would you administer to revert the arterial hypotension? In the blood vessels, the adrenergic receptors expressed are alpha-1. By administering an agonist of alpha 1, we can reverse the hypotension.

[PDF p. 148]

Firstly, physicians provided a saline solution to increase blood volume (fluid therapy, to increase vascular and interstitial extracellular fluid)

They then proceeded to provide agonists of alpha-1 adrenergic such as ephedrine or phenylephrine.

As the sympathetic tone is restored, arterial pressure goes back to normal.

If bradycardia appears (45 bpm), how would you explain it? Because phenylephrine has no beta-adrenergic activity, it does not increase contractility force and output of the cardiac muscle. It may increase blood pressure resulting in a slow heart rate through stimulation of vascular baroreceptors. In the presence of high mean arterial pressure, the baroreceptor reflex produces a reflex bradycardia as a method of decreasing blood pressure by decreasing the cardiac output, to counteract the unbalance. This takes place in the brain stem.

Alpha-1 receptor agonist = increased receptor stimulation = ↑ bp = detected by vascular baroreceptors = baroreceptor reflex = decreased heart rate (bradycardia)

Baroreceptor reflex The changes in distension of the carotids is detected by the glossopharyngeal nerve (9th spinal nerve). The sensory neuron’s somas of this nerve are located in the inferior ganglion

of the IX cranial nerve.

Picks up distension vessel information in the bifurcation of the carotid artery (carotid sinus). Its peripheral process goes towards the brain stem, where it reaches its some, located in ganglion prior to the jugular foramen. The central process enters through the jugular foramen for synapse

Arterial pressure is high = glossopharyngeal nerve detects it and goes from carotid sinus to solitary nucleus (similar to dorsal horn) in the brainstem = excite vasodepressor neurons (green) = synapse with PS motor neurons in the dorsal vagal nucleus = they run down through the vagus nerve, which stimulates postganglionic PS neurons = release Ach which binds M2 muscarinic receptors on cardiac muscle cells = bradycardia

Arterial pressure is low = induce tachycardia through another pathway glossopharyngeal nerve transmits distension information = d excite vasopressor neurons that project to the intermediolateral column (T1-L2) = has to go down the intermediolateral cell column and synapse with the corresponding sympathetic motor neuron, to induce tachycardia

How would you then treat the patient? As a response, if arterial pressure goes up, the vagus is stimulated. Some patients experience bradycardia after agonist alpha-1 administration To block this effect (PS response inducing bradycardia), we would administer antagonists for muscarinic receptors, as they use Ach as a neurotransmitter.

[PDF p. 149]

Key ideas:

  • Organs and systems such as the vascular system are exclusively innervated by the sympathetic nerves. Other examples would be the kidney and the system regulating glucemia.
  • There is a basal sympathetic tone in the sympathetic system and the regulation of function under that control depends on the increase or decrease of that basal tone. That is why our patient responded to the treatment with alpha-1 receptor (like ephedrine or phenylephrine)

Other organs have both sympathetic and parasympathetic innervation and their regulation depends on the basal sympathetic and the parasympathetic tones . An example of this is the heart. That is why if our patient experiences bradycardia she would respond to a treatment with antagonists of muscarinic receptors (like atropine).

[PDF p. 150]

HISTOLOGY AND PHYSIOLOGY OF NORMAL TISSUES

[PDF p. 151]

Cartilage

Connective tissue: cartilage

Cartilage is a specialized type of connective tissue. It consists of cells (chondroblasts and chondrocytes) and ECM made of fibers and ground substance (proteoglycans, glycoproteins,..)

The whiter areas, with lots of ground substance, represent loose connective tissue which is found surrounding cartilaginous tissue. Within the cartilage, we can appreciate chondrocytes embedded in lacunae which are differentiated and secreting matrix. This matrix which surrounds the chondrocytes and lacunae is formed by fibers and ground substance (it’s not fibrous ECM though).

As part of the components of the ECM we can find collagen type II, which is seen only in cartilage. Other components include:

Type II collagen, already mentioned

Hyaluranan (hyaluronic acid)

Proteoglycans

- Aggrecan

- Chondroitin sulfate

  • Keratin sulfate

Glycosaminoglycans

➢ Different types of collagen. What’s their importance?

Knowing the type of collagen is useful to aid in identifying the structures.

- Connective tissue = type I collagen

- Cartilage = type II collagen

- Reticular connective tissue = type III collagen

- Basal membrane = type IV collagen

[PDF p. 152]

Separating loose connective tissue and cartilage, there is a kind of capsule (a little denser and more compact than cartilage) found surrounding cartilage. This layer is known as perichondrium. This layer of dense irregular connective tissu e acts as the reservoir for chondrocytes. Signalling molecules can activate the cells in the perichondrium, causing them to differentiate into chondrocytes through gene activation and inactivation. Fibroblasts have the potential to differentiate into other tissue cells, such as those of cartilage: the fibroblasts in loose CT start to move towards cartilage and begin to change their morphology (becoming round) and secreting matrix.

We can differentiate 2 layers of the perichondrium: outer fibrous layer (fibroblasts) and inner chondrogenic layer (contains chondroblasts, which secrete matrix)

CHARACTERISTICS

  • It’s an avascular tissue; in general connective tissue is highly vascularized, except in the case of cartilage. This is why this tissue is compact and forms thin layers, as to easily obtain nutrients through diffusion from surrounding tissues, drawn in through osmosis. They proliferate very slowly as they have a scarce supply of nutrients which they have to store. As an adaptation to this, chondrocytes have developed a way to store energy, in the form of glycogen. Therefore, cartilage is very rich in glycogen. (chondrocytes replicate very slowly that’s why it is difficult to recover from lesions affecting cartilage).

  • It serves as a precursor/model for embryonic development and subsequent growth of many long bones. Cartilage is replaced by bone tissue in adult life, except for the surfaces that articulate with other bones.

FUNCTIONS

  • The cartilaginous ECM allows the surrounding tissues to bear mechanical stresses without being permanently distorted ( it’s very resilient).

  • Serves as a framework to give support to other tissues

  • It’s shock-absorbing and acts as a gliding area for joints

  • It facilitates bone movements

  • It’s responsible for development and formation of long bones through ossification

[PDF p. 153]

TYPES OF CARTILAGE

  1. Hyaline cartilage (type II collagen) - It’s the most abundant cartilage in the body. It’s

found on articular surfaces (articular cartilage = specialized form of hyaline cartilage, has NO perichondrium) , forming the nasal septum and the tracheal rings, sternal end of ribs, … It also forms the template for developing long bones (epiphyseal plate). Hyaline cartilage forms the temporary skeleton in the fetus, until it’s replaced by bone through ossification.

  1. Elastic cartilage (type II collagen) - found in pinna of the ear,

epiglottis, eustachian tube (connects your throat to your middle ear). Abundant elastic fibres.

  1. Fibrocartilage (large amount of type I and type II collagen) - found
