in intervertebral discs, pubic symphysis and the insertion sites of tendons and ligaments. Chondrocytes are found in parallel in between collagen fibres. Due to this parallel alignment, they’re present in structures which bear high stresses, as it is more

resilient.

CARTILAGE ECM

Some areas of the matrix stain more blue than other areas; these darker areas represent regions with more fibers, specifically type II collagen. In the lighter areas of the matrix, we find more proteoglycans and therefore, water. The matrix is not homogeneous (not the same amount of substances in each of the regions) because we need areas which draw in water and therefore, nutrients and oxygen through diffusion, and others which provide protection.

  • The darker matrix (more basophilic = seen in blue/dark red in this case) which is adjacent to the chondrocytes is known as the territorial matrix . Richer in proteoglycans, stains darker.

  • The lighter matrix ( eosinophilic areas = clearer), found between isogenous groups is the interterritorial matrix . Less proteoglycans, stains darker. All the O2 and nutrients can easily flow and diffuse into the different cells.

CARTILAGE FORMATION / GROWTH

Cartilage begins to form from embryonic mesenchymal cells. It can either originate from fibroblasts and chondroblasts found in the perichondrium layer, or from interstitial growth through the mitotic division of chondrocytes, which results in the formation of isogenous groups that accumulate in lacunae.

[PDF p. 154]

Appositional growth: consists in the deposition of new cartilage on the surface of existing cartilage: it grows from the outside, originating from the perichondrium. This occurs through the differentiation of fibroblasts, a layer of cells with proliferative capacity. Each layer contains more and more differentiated cells until they finally derive to chondrocytes (fibroblasts-chondroblasts-chondrocytes). This differentiation by layers is known as appositional growth.

Interstitial growth: formation of new cartilage within existing cartilage: this is another way in which cartilage can grow (which is really slow) and is known as interstitial growth (through the middle). It occurs through the division of chondrocytes. All the daughter cells will be a clone of the mother cells, which will give place to clonal groups found within the lacunae, known as isogenous groups.

Interstitial growth is not an efficient kind of growth, as it is a slow process that takes a long time to happen. This is why it takes lots of time to recover from a cartilage injury; the cells will divide very slowly. These cells can be stimulated to grow through physiotherapy: throughout movement and exercising, we are inducing these cells to proliferate.

CLINICAL CASE

AGE: 38, GENDER: female. She presents a right middle finger distal phalangeal swelling. The mass is being caused by aberrant proliferation of tissue; but not bone tissue. The nature of the tissue isn’t bone, but cartilage. This is detected on the MRI: we can see a black line surrounding the phalanges, which is found enlarged in the lesion.

We do a biopsy of the lesion: the yellow area represents bone tissue,

with lines that are bone travicle

(spongy). Growing outside the bone we can see a large mass of cartilage.

Under the microscope: The lesion of the patient is just an outgrowth of cartilage, which can be resected.

[PDF p. 155]

Bone tissue

Connective tissue: bone

Bone tissue is a specialized type of connective tissue with a calcified extracellular

matrix in which characteristic cells are embedded.

BONE TISSUE FUNCTIONS

  • To support and protect vital organs, and fleshy structures such as hematopoietic

organs.

  • Provides a storage site for phosphate and calcium (bone contains about 99% of the body’s calcium)

TYPES OF BONES

Long bones (humerus)

  • Flat bones (sternum)
  • Short bones (talus)

Irregular bones (vertebrae)

↳ Structure of long bones: epiphysis, metaphysis

and diaphysis.

BONE STRUCTURE

Bone can be divided into compact/cortical (dense) bone or spongy (cancellous) bone.

  • On the outside: compact or cortical bone. More dense. Formed just by bone tissue.
  • On the inside: cancellous bone. We find bone, medulla,

blood vessels,…In between the cancellous bone, we find

hematopoietic tissue: bone marrow.

Within the bone, we find:

  • Cells: osteoblasts and osteocytes
  • Fibers: collagen fibers, specifically type I collagen. They serve as a scaffold for mineralization of calcium and phosphate
  • Ground substance: we find water in between, but very little as it’s highly dense tissue. Therefore, bone needs a way for the 02 and nutrients to diffuse. This is why it is highly vascularized.

[PDF p. 156]

  1. BONE MATRIX

Organic matrix / Unmineralized : More immature, mostly made up of type I collagen (95%) and type V, as well as ground-substance (low water content) which contains chondroitin sulfate, keratan sulfate and hyaluronic acid. This immature matrix is secreted by osteocytes and will serve as a scaffold for calcium and phosphate.It’s known as osteoid. Its function is to allow mineralization.

Inorganic portion / Mineralized : it’s the calcified part of bone, more mature . Formed after calcium and phosphate are deposited on the collagen matrix. Made up of calcium, citrate, phosphate, bicarbonate,…

Cartilage

  • Water content: aprox 70%

  • Collagen: type II, 40% of organic content

  • Grows interstitially and by apposition

  • Avascular tissue

Bone

- Water content: 25%

  • Collagen: type I, 90% of organic content

  • Grows only by appositional growth

  • Highly vascularized

Other Ground substance components in bone (bone proteins, located in between collagen fibers, which also aid in scaffolding for calcification) :

  • Osteonectin: it anchors collagen to bone mineral
  • Osteocalcin: calcium-binding protein, involved in bone calcification

Osteopontin: carries out binding of osteoblasts and osteoclasts to bone

  1. PERIOSTEUM

Periosteum is a layer of noncalcified connective tissue, found covering bone on its external surfaces (except at synovial articulations and muscle attachments). It presents an outer dense, fibrous collagenous layer and an inner cellular osteoprogenitor ( osteogenic ) layer.

Sharpey fibers (type I collagen) attach the periosteum to the bone surface. It serves as the place through which blood vessels enter and reach the bone. Its function is to provide cells, fibroblasts, that will differentiate into osteoblasts which will later on differentiate into osteocytes . This type of growth (one layer on top of the other) is known as appositional growth.

  1. ENDOSTEUM

Endosteum consists of thin, specialized connective tissue that lines the marrow cavities (inside). It contains undifferentiated cells that give rise to osteoblasts, which begin to secrete collagen type I. Therefore, its function is to supply osteoprogenitor cells (fibroblasts) and osteoblasts for bone growth and repair.

[PDF p. 157]

  1. BONE TISSUE CELLS

    • Osteocytes : mature bone cells, secreting type I collagen.

- Osteoprogenitor cells: cells derived from mesenchymal stem cells, which will originate

osteoblasts.

  • Osteoblasts: once they start to secrete osteoid matrix, they become surrounded by it and differentiate into osteocytes.

- Bone lining cells: found on bone surface when there is no active growth. They are derived from osteoblasts that did not originate osteocytes.

  • Osteoclasts: they carry out a process of remodeling, by destroying bone while osteoblasts secrete new bone tissue. They are present where bone is being removed/remodeled or where bone has been damaged.

MATURE BONE ORGANIZATION

  • On one hand, we find the periosteum, a dense fibrous connective tissue layer made up of 2 sublayers:

    - An **outer fibrous layer** _(fibrous tissue)_
- An **inner cellular layer** _(more cell content)._ It

contains the osteoprogenitor cells which will give

rise to osteoblasts.

It contains many collagen fibers, commonly known as Sharpey’s fibers. In ligaments and tendons, these fibers extend directly into the bone tissue, where they are continuous with the collagen fibers of the extracellular matrix of the bone tissue. It connects bone to surrounding tissues (ex: muscles)

Bone is a highly vascularized tissue. It’s vascularized by blood vessels that penetrate the matrix from the periosteum . We can differentiate:

           **Perforating (Volkmann’s) canals**

- Central (Haversian) canals

Collagen has a concentric deposition surrounding these concentric canals, forming layers known as

lamellae.

[PDF p. 158]

  • The endosteum is a part of bone tissue found both in compact bone, facing the marrow cavity, and in the trabeculae of spongy bone. It is often formed by only one cell layer (endosteal cells) of osteoprogenitor cells that will differentiate into osteoblasts, for growth and repair.

  • Osteons: Mature bone is largely composed of cylindrical structures known as osteons or Haversian systems, inside which we find the Harvesian canal containing blood vessels and

nerves.

By definition, osteons are concentric lamellae of bone matrix surrounding a central canal, the osteonal (Haversian) canal, which contains the vascular and nerve supply of the osteon (long axis of an osteon is usually parallel to the long axis of the bone).

Collagen fibers are laid down:

 - **parallel to one another in any given lamella,** but
 - in **different directions in adjacent lamellae**

This is done to make the structure even stronger and confer more resistance, by creating a meshwork of collagen type I. The deposition within the lamellae is parallel, but each layer is oriented in different directions.

  • Lacunae and canaliculi: lacunae are “holes” / lagunes located within the bone tissue, where we will find osteocytes. These osteocytes are secreting bone matrix.

Once the osteocyte is totally surrounded by organic and inorganic matrix, it produces prolongations of the cytoplasm to coordinate the process of degrading and regenerating bone. Inorganic matrix makes it difficult for communication to occur. Therefore, these

prolongations contact each other and exchange

molecules for communication.

These prolongations are known as canaliculi and are found in between lacunae, connecting cells found inside said lacunae.

[PDF p. 159]

IMMATURE & MATURE BONE TISSUE

CHARACTERISTICS:

IMMATURE BONE

  • Non-lamellar arrangement of collagen

  • Matrix = osteoid, also known as woven bone (unmineralized, prior to maturation)

  • Matrix can also be found calcified in IMM-B. It presents different levels of calcification

  • ++ cells than mature bone, arranged randomly

  • Likely to be found in the trabeculae/bone marrow

We can observe some darker pink lines, which correspond to collagen fibers. The matrix is rich in type I collagen, which is found in this organization: fibers laid one on top of the other, becoming mineralized. Mineralization can be appreciated as a more

blue area.

- Blue = mineralized

- Pink-ish = unmineralized.

MATURE BONE

  • Organized arrangement of collagen, found surrounding a central canal + osteocyte-containing lacunae

  • Contains cylindrical units = osteons / Harvesian systems

  • les cells, arranged concentrically

  • Can be found in compact bone - also known as lamellar bone

Image 1: no Haversian organization. Collagen presents different colors, corresponding to different stacks of calcification. Lighter pink corresponds to the osteoid, unmineralized matrix, and the blueish areas are calcified. Likely to be found in the bone marrow/trabeculae Image 2: several cylinders, osteons with haversian canals + lamellae where we find lacunae, containing osteocytes. Can be found in the compact bone. Lamellar bone.

[PDF p. 160]

➢ What is the main difference between trabeculae and cortex bone?

The organization .

  • In trabecular bone (immature bone), we don’t find osteons, just bone tissue and osteocytes. Collagen isn’t being deposited in concentric lamellae, but in lines. There are no central vessels (no Harvesian canal) , so they are therefore not concentric. In addition to this, in immature bone, we can find different ranges of calcification. Woven bone/osteoid will start to become calcified in certain areas, and remain

unmineralized in others.

  • In cortical bone (mature bone), we can observe these concentric structures known as osteons. In this mature bone, the matrix is all calcified.

CLINICAL CASE LESION. In the image, we can distinguish cartilage within the bone. Inside, bone marrow cells. We can find the

cartilage next to trabecular bone. which contains osteocytes and osteoblasts lining the surface, synthesizing collagen which is being deposited in a linear manner.

Calcified cartilage is gradually demolished by osteoclasts while new lamellar bone is laid down by osteoblasts. After a period of remodelling, the bone is restored to its previous anatomy.

➢ What do we mean by lamellar and non-lamellar bone?

  • Lamellar bone = calcified bone. It can be concentric on non-concentric.

Cortical bone (concentric calcified) and trabecular bone (non-concentric calcified)

  • Non-lamellar = unmineralized (non-concentric decalcified) . Also known as “woven” bone, unmineralized spongy bone.

In trabecular bone, the linear deposition of collagen becomes calcified and therefore matured (woven → lamellar). Therefore, it becomes lamellar bone. It’s still, however, a non-concentric structure. Spongy bone tissue does not contain the osteons that constitute compact bone tissue. Instead, it consists of trabeculae, which are lamellae that are arranged as rods or plates.

BONE MICROENVIRONMENT

This bone microenvironment is comprised of a mineralized extracellular matrix and specific cell types that are under the control of local and systemic factors. Bone is constantly being built and broken down through the process of bone remodeling

               - Once the **osteoblast** is totally

surrounded by a calcified matrix, it’s known as osteocyte. They originate from mesenchymal and osteoprogenitor cells.

               - **Osteoclasts** come from

Hematopoietic stem cells.

[PDF p. 161]

REMODELING PROCESS

Bone mass is maintained by a balance between formation ad destruction, controlled by the activity of osteoblasts, which form bone, and osteoclasts, which break it down. Bone remodeling has to go through several phases

OSTEOCLASTS

Osteoclasts are in charge of bone degradation, and secrete acid, collagenases and proteases that demineralize the bone matrix and degrade matricellular proteins such as type I collagen. Hydroxyacetate = calcium phosphate. Main component of bone, which is liberated upon demineralization. They are derived from myeloid lineage cells (monocytes). Signalling molecules (growth factors such as M-CSF and RANKL , mainly released by osteoblasts), which support osteoclastogenesis, cause the transformation of monocytes into osteoclasts, characteristic due to their polynucleated features. Osteoprotegerin is an endogenous receptor of RANKL that inhibits osteoclastogenesis.

Parts and characteristics

Osteoclasts are highly polarized (EM image) . Invaginations of the membrane on the basal face, found forming the ruffled border, increase the area of the membrane, and therefore increase the surface area for bone reabsorption. This zone is known as the sealing zone, where reabsorption process takes place. Enzymes are released from this zone onto mineralized bone, and as the osteoclast

advances, the bone becomes demineralized and all the minerals are freed, becoming readily available for the body through

circulation.

* Excessive bone loss = osteoporosis

OSTEOBLASTS

Osteoblasts are in charge of bone formation, which originate from mesenchymal stem cells coming from within the bone marrow . Osteoclasts resorb bone, thus allowing the release of factors usually stored in the bone matrix that recruit osteoblasts in the resorbed area. Once recruited, osteoblasts produce the new bone matrix and promote its mineralization, thus completing the bone remodeling process.

CLINICAL CASE

We order an X-ray and CT scan. On the CT we can appreciate an outgrowth of the fractured rib. The outgrowth is white, corresponding to compact bone.

[PDF p. 162]

As this is a fracture, we see a mass of cartilage. A process of bone formation is occurring. White big circles = Harvesian canal, in which we find blood vessels and nerves. On the periphery we find periosteum. We can also appreciate the way in which collagen is being distributed in a concentric way, forming units known as osteon.

Osteoclasts = multinucleated.

[PDF p. 163]

Nerve tissue

It enables the body to respond to internal and external stimuli, by integrating the

information.

Divided anatomically into:

- Central nervous system (CNS)

    brain (in the skull)

    spinal cord (vertebral column)

- Peripheral nervous system (PNS)

    peripheral nerves

    associated ganglia

    - connections to the CNS

CELL TYPES

  • Neurons : most characteristic cell/structural functional unit, responsible for conducting electrical impulse

Supporting cells (neuroglia): support, nurture and protect the neurons. Both central and peripheral neuroglia.

NEURONS

Neurons are cells with lots of cytoplasmic processes (multiple dendrites and usually, a single axon) . Regarding the body/soma of the cell: it has a central nucleus and a very developed nucleolus. In the cytoplasm we can also find dark, basophilic granules (chromatophilic substances).

Identified structures/components:

Cell body (soma)

Centered nucleus + developed nucleolus

  • Dendrites + dendritic branches

  • Axon hillock

  • Axon

Synaptic terminals

Some neurons are really large, but they are of variable size. Range from the smallest to the biggest cells in the body (ranging from 5 to 150 μm in diameter)

Morphofunctional regions:

  • Receptor region: formed by the dendrites and soma of the neuron. Receives the signal from another neuron or cell.
  • Generation and conduction of the electrical impulse: formed in the axon hillock and conducted through the axon.
  • Synapse region: the impulse will be transmitted to another neuron or effector cell.

[PDF p. 164]

Depending on how these regions are distributed, we can differentiate different regions in CNS and PNS. For example, in the spinal cord:

Spinal cord. Neuronal cell distribution

Darker inner region and paler outer region.

The distribution of neuronal cell bodies and axons

allows us to differentiate between white and

gray matter in CNS and ganglia and nerves in

the PNS.

Gray matter and ganglia : Neuronal cell bodie s (CNS and PNS, respectively)

  • White matter and nerves: No neuronal

cell bodies, just neuronal processes (and supporting cells)

Functional classification of neurons

Neurons are spatially organized in chains and networks. We have 3 different chains/components:

  • Sensory neurons: receive information from the internal and external environment. They conduct impulses to the CNS for processing and analysis.

  • Interneurons: conduct information and connect different neurons in a chain of

information. They generally connect sensory and motor neurons. In charge of regulating signals transmitted to neurons. Most abundant type of neurons we have.

  • Motor neurons: transmit the information from the CNS to the motor cell/effector

cell in order to exert the response (muscles and glands), or to other neurons.

Morphological classification (focusing on the processes):

  • Pseudounipolar neurons: a single process that extends from the cell body and subsequently branches into a peripheral process and central process. Not very abundant, found mainly in spinal and ganglia.

  • Bipolar neuron: a single axon and a single dendrite. Found in some sensory organs. Also not very abundant.

  • Multipolar neurons: a single axon and more than one dendrite, with different

branching degrees. These are the most abundant type in vertebrates.

[PDF p. 165]

Neuronal cell body. Soma

The region of a neuron containing the nucleus, various cytoplasmic organelles and inclusions, and cytoskeletal elements.

● The nucleus

    - It's a **large, spherical nucleus** (euchromatin, uncondensed

= pale staining ). - Centrally located within the soma - Large, well developed nucleolus within abundant

euchromatin. We see a dark nucleolus in the center of a

very euchromatic (pale) nucleus (owl-eye).

  • Cytoplasmic organelles

    - **Nissl bodies** : granular structures containing

polyribosomes and RER, which stain basophilic . Very abundant in the cytoplasm, they appear as clumps under light microscopy. Abundant in large,

motor neurons.

    - **Lysosomes:** We can also find **abundant** lysosomes

    - **Mitochondria:** scattered through the cytoplasm

    - **Golgi apparatus:** located near the nucleus.

  • Inclusions

    - **Lipofuscin-containing granules:** formed by **residual bodies** of lysosomes.

The number increases with age

    **Lipid droplets** (occasionally present)

- Melanin-containing droplets

● Cytoskeletal components

We will also find a very well developed cytoskeleton, for the transport of synaptic vesicles from the region of production through the axon all the way to the region of synapse. They also maintain the structure of the neurons.

    **Neurofilaments (10nm):** abundant, throughout

the soma

- Microtubules (24 nm): in the soma

- Microfilaments (actin filaments, 6 nm): associated with the plasma membrane.

[PDF p. 166]

Dendrites

Receive stimuli (signals) from sensory cells, axons, or other neurons and convert these signals into small electrical impulses (NOT action potentials yet) transmitted toward the

soma.

Generally branched (arborized structures) (except in bipolar neurons) , allowing neurons to obtain stimuli from very different neurons . Besides this, within the branches they contain very different spines which play a very important role in transmission as they increase the area available for synapse formation. These spines decrease with age and poor nutrition.

  • Its cytoplasm is similar to that of the soma
  • With reduced number of organelles as they are not necessary, with the exception of mitochondria
  • Abundant mitochondria (necessary)

Axons

Axons conduct impulses away from the soma to the axon terminals without any diminution in their strength.

Axon hillock

Axons originate from the axon hillock, which is continuous with the soma (can be considered as a specialized region of the soma) ; however, in this

axon hillock:

  • There are no Nissl bodies, which are characteristic of the soma.
  • They also have a very abundant network of microtubules and neurofilaments which will allow the passage of mitochondria and

vesicles into the axons.

It is the region where the electrical impulse is generated.

Axon

  • The proper axon has a surrounding plasma membrane, known as axolemma

Cytoplasm known as axoplasm:

    **No RER or Golgi** as it won’t be in charge of any synthesis

    However, it contains **abundant cytoskeleton**
    - Also contains **SER and elongated mitochondria.**

It derives into many small branches = axon terminals, regions where synapsis will take place. Impulses are passed to another neuron or other types of cells.

- Protected by glial cells, in general because the axon is a very thin, long structure and if it gets damaged, we risk losing many connections. They can be up to 1m long, so they have to be protected, and this is done by glial cells.

[PDF p. 167]

Axonal transport systems

In the axons, mostly in the membrane, we have to transport molecules. This is a bidirectional

mechanism:

Anterograde transport: from the cell body to axon terminals (periphery), where there is regeneration of proteins and vesicles. MAPs, such as kinesin, plays an important role in anterograde transport.

Retrograde transport: from axon terminal to body. It’s the way of recycling material. Dynein plays important role in this retrograde transport.

Recognizing neurons under microscope:

Clear, pale nucleus, clearly well-centered

Dark, condensed and developed nucleolus

Basophilic clumps, Nissl bodies, found within the cytoplasm of the soma

Large size with a characteristic shape

NEUROGLIAL CELLS

Found both in CNS and PNS. The neuroglia is a web of tissue composed of modified

ectodermal elements.

Main function of neuroglial cells is to support and protect neurons . But they’re also involved in synapse. They don’t conduct neurological impulse, but are involved in synapsis by providing some regulation of neurons in the process of neural transmissions through:

Actively monitoring synapses

Removing neurotransmitters from synapses

Delivering molecules known as gliotransmitter substances into the synaptic space

From an imaging point of view, we can’t really differentiate the different neuroglial cells under conventional staining. We have to use specific staining, gold and silver stains.

- IN THE CNS:

Astrocytes

They are very branched, with long extended processes. Astrocytes are the largest of neuroglial cells, characterized by having many processes, with different functions:

Surrounding blood vessels through expanded pedicles (vascular feet). They participate in reinforcing the blood-brain barrier: it tightly regulates exchange and passage of substances from blood to brain stroma.

  • Surrounding neurons, scavenging for ions involved in synapses after they have completed their function, and supplying energy for metabolism

In contact with meninges (pia mater), also creating a protective barrier. They form a protective sealed barrier between the pia mater and the nervous tissue.

  • In the brain, astrocytes form scar tissue (glial scar) after injury to the CNS.

[PDF p. 168]

Different types of astrocytes :

  • Protoplasmic astrocytes: .

    Mainly found in **gray matter**
- Have **short processes but very arborized**, which have the mentioned

functions (enveloping blood vessels, neurons, and synaptic areas) . - In these cells, we can find some intermediate filaments composed of glial fibrillar acidic protein (GFAP). - Mainly involved in the formation of blood brain barrier and its maintenance

  • Fibrous astrocytes:

    - Mainly found in **white matter**
- **long, slender processes** with **little arborization,**

carrying out characteristic functions. - Many intermediate filaments composed of GFAP

Blood-brain barrier

In CNS there is very tight regulation of the diffusion of molecules from blood into nerve tissue. This is mainly controlled by the blood-brain barrier, composed of tight junctions formed between adjacent endothelial cells in the capillary walls surrounding nerve tissue. Tight junctions exert a very restricted regulation:

  • 02, H20, C02 and certain liposoluble molecules can pass via diffusion

Some Ions can pass through active transport

Macromolecules and drugs have to go through receptor mediated transport.

This barrier is supported by the feet of astrocytes. These astrocytes don’t form occluding junctions, they reinforce those formed between endothelial cells by connecting through anchoring junctions. They themselves don’t create the tight junctions but reinforce them.

Oligodendrocytes

Oligodendrocytes live symbiotically with neurons. They are essential in the survival of neurons in the CNS. They protect the axons of neurons in the CNS. Can be found both in grey and white matter.

Characteristics

They have a small, condensed centered nucleus

They contain an electron dense cytoplasm.

    - Ribosomes

    - Numerous microtubules

    - Mitochondria

    - RER

    Large Golgi complex

[PDF p. 169]

  • Have a few short processes. The short processes surround the axon and create concentric layers of myelin, myelin sheaths, which will play an important role in the electrical transmission. Oligodendrocytes form several processes, and each process produces myelin for a single internode. Therefore, each oligodendrocyte can myelinate several axons.

Microglial cells

Very similar to astrocytes under the microscope. They are small, phagocytic neuroglial cells derived from the mononuclear phagocytic cell population in the bone marrow.

Characteristics

Present a condensed, elongated nucleus

  • Many short, branching processes. Very arborized

  • They are sort of the immune cells of the CNS. When activated, they change their shape, loose branching and become more similar to macrophages. They start to secrete cytokines which recruit other immune cells (T cells); they act as

APCs.

Ependymal cells

  • Derived from the neuroepithelium

Located in very particular places: they are the epithelial cells

that line the neural tube and ventricles of the brain

  • In certain regions of the brain, they contain cilia (responsible for moving the cerebrospinal fluid (CSF))

  • Modified ependymal cells contribute to the formation of the choroids plexus (It is a highly organized tissue that lines all the ventricles of the brain. Resides in the innermost layer of the meninges (pia mater) which is in close contact with the cerebral cortex and spinal cord).

[PDF p. 170]

- IN THE PNS:

Schwann cells

Their function is similar to that of oligodendrocytes . They protect and isolate the neurons, same as oligodendrocytes in the CNS. But they exert their functions in cells of

the PNS.

Oligodendrocytes are flat cells ( with basement membrane) with only a few mitochondria and a small Golgi region. They can form:

myelinated covers, surrounding the axons

unmyelinated covers : in this situation, Schwann cells are just found surrounding the

axon.

A single Schwann cell can only insulate a single axon in a myelinated fiber, whereas a single oligodendrocyte may insulate several axons.

Neuroglial cells. Origin and functions

SYNAPSES

Synapse consists in the signal transmission from one neuron to another or to another cell type (e.g., muscle cell) . Classified according to the site of synaptic contact and the method of signal transmission:

Classification according to site

  • Axodendritic synapses: most common type,

between axon and dendrite

  • Axosomatic synapses: between axon and soma

  • Axoaxonic synapses: between 2 axons, much less

common

[PDF p. 171]

Classification according to signal transmission

  • Chemical synapses. Unidirectional

    - **Most common** type of **neuron-neuron synapse,** and the **only** type of

muscle-neuron synapse (neuromuscular junction). - Occurs by the release of neurotransmitters from the presynaptic cell which reach and act on the postsynaptic cell.

    - The **transmission will be slower** than the electrical

synapse as it is a longer process. Delayed by about 0.5 ms, time required for secretion and diffusion of neurotransmitter from the presynaptic membrane into the synaptic cleft and then to the postsynaptic

membrane.

    - Also important: **neurotransmitters themselves don’t**

effect the change; they only activate a response in the receptor cell.

  • Electrical synapses. Bidirectional

    - Much **less numerous** than chemical synapses

- Consists in the **movement of ions** from one neuron to

another via gap junctions.

    They **transmit the action potential** of the presynaptic cell

directly to the postsynaptic cell - It’s much faster than the chemical synapse. Signal transmission across these synapses is nearly

instantaneous

Synaptic morphology (chemical synapse)

Axon terminal:

  • Axon terminals can have different shapes. They vary morphologically, depending on the site of contact.

They contain synaptic vesicles (distinguishable morphological features)

  • Contain voltage-gated calcium channels which will play important role in the process of synapse (regulate the entry of Ca2+ into the axon terminal)
  • The region of the presynaptic membrane in “contact” with the postsynaptic has a thicker membrane in the area of

synapses.

Synaptic vesicles fuse with vesicles and release the containing neurotransmitters into the synaptic space.

The postsynaptic membrane:

The membrane of the neuron or other target cell that is receiving the impulse is also

thickened.

[PDF p. 172]

Synaptic cleft:

It’s the narrow space (20-30 nm wide) between presynaptic - postsynaptic membranes. Space neurotransmitters have to diffuse across to reach the next neuron.

Astrocytes:

Astrocytes protect the synaptic region. They aren’t actively involved in synapsis, but participate by:

removing neurotransmitters from the synaptic space (glutamate)

  • secrete molecules that will play a role in regulating synapse (gliotransmitters)

Synaptic vesicles and neurotransmitters:

Synaptic vesicles: small, membrane-bound structures in the axoplasm of the transmitting neuron. Synaptic vesicles discharge neurotransmitters into the synaptic cleft by exocytosis. Can be seen through EM like small vesicles with a membrane, found in the cytoplasm of the transmitting neuron.

  • Neurotransmitters : Produced, stored (vesicles), and released by presynaptic neurons. Released by exocytosis : they diffuse through the synaptic cleft and bind different receptors in the postsynaptic neuron, facilitating the transmission of the electrical impulse. They generate an action potential:

- Transmitter-gated channels

- G-protein–coupled receptors

EM image:

Neuromuscular junction

Particular type of synapses: Neuromuscular junction / Motor end plate (muscle innervation). It’s a type of chemical synapse.

1 Axon can innervate several muscle cells, and therefore:

  • Motor unit refers to the group of muscle cells innervated by the same motor neurons
  • Motor end plate is the large area where the different synapses take place. We can see that the axons branch

into several axon terminals.

[PDF p. 173]

Focusing on one motor end plate, we focus on a neuromuscular junction:

The general structure is the one already seen in chemical synapses. The special feature is that in the postsynaptic membrane, there are junctional folds. These are deep invaginations of the muscle cell plasma membrane ( sarcolemma ) which increase the surface area for the neurotransmitter (ACh) to interact.

NERVE FIBERS

1 axon = 1 nerve fiber.

Motor neurons:

The axon leaves CNS, and in the PNS the nerve fibers (individual axons) group, forming bundles which give place to nerves.

  • In the CNS, enveloped by Oligodendrocytes, with myelin sheath
  • In PNS, enveloped by Schwann cells, myelinated or unmyelinated sheaths

Myelin sheath

Axon recovered by concentric myelin layers

  • Produced by oligodendrocytes in the CNS and by Schwann cells in the PNS

Deposited in concentric layers forming an insulating sheet. Seen as several spiral layers of the plasma membrane of an oligodendrocyte or Schwann cell wrapping

around the axon

  • However this layer is not continuous. It has the length that a single Schwann cell/oligodendrocyte can cover. These gaps left between adjacent glial cells are

known as nodes of Ranvier.

  • The thickness of these layers is usually constant along the length of an axon; however, thickness usually increases as the axonal diameter increases.

Process of formation:

Schwann cell/oligodendrocyte starts to wrap around a portion of an axon; their cytoplasm and plasma membrane begin to form consecutive spiral layers

around the axon

Overlapping inner layers of the Schwann cell/oligodendrocyte plasma membrane form the myelin

sheath.

  • The nucleus gets pushed to the periphery of the cell.

[PDF p. 174]

Image 1: nucleus of the Schwann cell (SC) + axon (A) embedded in its cytoplasm and recovered by myelin sheath (M); inside said axon, we see microtubules (MT) and neurofilaments (NF); on the top right, we see another SC surrounding an axon and a forming myelin sheath (FM); on the bottom right, another axon-surrounding SC, but in this case the recovering is unmyelinated (UM).

Image 2: axon, with microtubules (MT) and NF. We can find organelles (mitochondria, Mit) + a surrounding myelin sheath (M). The surrounding cytoplasm corresponds to that of a schwann cell. Extracellular granules (C) correspond to collagen fibers, transversal cut.

Schmidt-Lanterman clefts

The Schwann cell or oligodendrocyte’s cytoplasm is found surrounding the myelin sheath, but also in between the myelin sheath and the

axon. There has to be communication between the inner and outer

cytoplasm of the recovering cells. This is done through incisions in myelin, known as Schmidt-Lanterman clefts. They are oblique discontinuities of the myelin sheath due to the presence of Schwann cell (or oligodendrocyte) cytoplasm within the myelin.

Nodes of Ranvier

Nodes of Ranvier are periodic gaps in the insulating sheath between Schwann cells or oligodendrocytes. It facilitates the rapid conduction of nerve impulses.

Differences between PNS and CNS:

  • In PNS, the small unprotected area (node) is covered by interdigitated cytoplasmic processes of the adjacent Schwann cells, which protect the axon.
  • In CNS, the myelin free surface at the node is covered by a foot plate of an astrocyte, protecting those unmyelinated regions.

In these regions there are abundant Na+ voltage gated channels to facilitate “saltatory” conduction.

[PDF p. 175]

Internodes

Length between adjacent Nodes of Ranvier (extension of the recovering cells which will give place to the myelin sheets). Vary in length from 0.08 to 1 mm, depending on the size of the Schwann cells or oligodendrocytes associated with the fiber.

Myelinated nerve fibers

Cross section and longitudinal view of a peripheral nerve. We can see the axons and epineurium on the left image. We can also distinguish Schmidt-Lanterman clefts and Nodes

and Ranvier.

Unmyelinated nerve fibers

Surrounded by a large number of Schwann cells. In the case of SC unmyelinated fibers, a single SC is seen to recover many axons at a time. Many axons embedded within its cytoplasm. Remember: only SC give rise to unmyelinated fibers.

[PDF p. 176]

Myelinated vs Unmyelinated

  • In CNS, oligodendrocytes only produce myelinated fibers. In PNS they can be myelinated or unmyelinated (SCs)

Since impulses in myelinated axons jump from one node of Ranvier to another, propagation of the impulse is much faster in myelinated axons. For unmyelinated fibers, it has to be continuously regenerated so as not to lose action potential.

- Myelinated SC → 1 axon; Unmyelinated SC → several axons

Myelinated-related diseases

Multiple sclerosis, (MS), the typical disease related to unmyelinated fibers which should be myelinated.

NERVES

Nerves are cordlike bundles made up of nerve fibers, surrounded by connective tissue sheaths. Within each nerve bundle it is difficult to distinguish any other cells. Whitish surrounding structure (light microscopy): myelin

Connective tissue components: we can differentiate three layers:

  • Epineurium: the outermost . It consists of dense fibrous connective tissue (fascia), found surrounding nerve bundles. Often embedded in adipose tissue.

  • Perineurium: surrounds each bundle of nerve fibers (fascicle) . Made up of specialized connective tissue cells, which block and regulate the transmission of specific molecules by binding through tight junctions. Form the blood-nerve barrier, preventing the transmission of toxic components.

  • Endoneurium: connective tissue layer that surrounds each individual nerve fiber. Made up of a thin layer of reticular fibers and collagen, containing some

fibroblasts.

[PDF p. 177]

GANGLIA

Encapsulated aggregations of neuronal cell bodies (soma) outside the CNS + abundant small glial cells (satellite cells). Found containing the neuronal bodies. Neurons surrounded by satellite cells (glial cells marked by blue arrow heads)

WHITE MATTER & GRAY MATTER

Both present in the CNS. We will differentiate them by identifying the different parts of the

neuron.

Gray matter

  • Neuronal cell bodies (soma)

  • Dendrites

Some axons: mostly unmyelinated fibers + some myelinated fibers

Neuroglial cells

White matter

  • Neural axons: mostly myelinated nerve fibers

  • some unmyelinated

    Neuroglial cells

There are 2 very clear regions in the CNS: In the spinal cord:

  • Grey matter located in the center of the spinal

cord

  • Outer white matter

In the cerebellum:

  • Grey matter in the outer layer
  • White matter in the inner layer

MENINGES

Membranous coverings of brain and spinal cord, formed by connective tissue. Found protecting the inner nerve tissue. 3 layers:

- Dura Mater: Outermost, lining the bony skull and close to vertebrae. - Arachnoid Mater: Intermediate, abutting the dura - Pia Mater: Innermost, highly vascular. Lying directly on the surface of the brain and spinal cord.

[PDF p. 178]

[PDF p. 179]

Muscle histology I. Skeletal muscle

Muscle cells are specialized elongated cells with the primary roles of contraction.

OVERVIEW

Muscle can be classified into 2 major groups:

  • Striated muscle (cells present striations)

- skeletal muscle

- cardiac muscle

● Smooth muscle (no striations)

Muscle cells are contractile filaments, whose major components are actin and myosin

filaments.

Regarding contraction, we find:

- Voluntary contraction (skeletal muscle)

Involuntary contraction (in cardiac and smooth muscle)

Skeletal muscle is found attached to bones and / or skin; cardiac muscle is located in the walls of the heart; smooth muscle located in almost all the visceral organs of the body: arteries, airways,… The appearance of the different types varies greatly.

CONNECTIVE TISSUE IN MUSCLE

Found surrounding and giving structure to the muscles. Connective tissue conveys neural and vascular elements to muscle cells and provides a vehicle that harnesses the forces

of muscle contraction.

Organization:

  • Epimysium : surrounds the entire muscle and forms a connection between skeletal muscle to other muscles and tendons, which at the same time connect to bone . It

will be important in conveying the contraction to tendons = pull on bones.

  • Perimysium : layer of connective tissue surrounding each muscle bundle. Also mostly found in skeletal muscle.

  • Endomysium : surrounding each individual muscle cell. It’s composed of collagen fibers, reticular fibers and some fibroblasts. In skeletal, smooth and cardiac muscle.

[PDF p. 180]

SKELETAL MUSCLE

Skeletal muscle in longitudinal view:

  • Multinucleated cells (fibers)
  • Nuclei are located at the periphery of the cell
  • Long, large cells , cylindrical in shape
  • The cytoplasm presents striations which corresponds to the myofibrils and thin filaments

Skeletal muscle in cross-section:

In the cross sectional views, we can also see these cylindrical

structures

- Multinucleated

  • Peripheral location
  • Surrounded by connective tissue layers and many blood vessels (BV), as it is highly vascularized.

Types of skeletal muscle

We can distinguish 3 types:

- Red fibers (type I)

- White fibers (type IIb)

- Intermediate fibers (type IIa)

Different coloring patterns. Specific staining for metabolic components also reveals that the metabolic components for each of these cells are different .

These muscle types differ in:

Contractile speed

Metabolic profile

  • Size

RED FIBERS = slow fibers (type I)

Slow contraction, but don’t fatigue easily (due to high ox. activity)

  • Abundant myoglobin: O2 storage point, contains iron → gives red fibers their color

  • Abundant mitochondria

Strong oxidative capacity

Smaller, thinner fibers

  • More vascularized

In muscles that contract frequently (diaphragm, lower extremities,..)

WHITE FIBERS = fast fibers (type IIb)

- Fast contraction but fatigue easily

Low myoglobin

  • Few mitochondria, as they have a fast anaerobic glycolysis metabolism

Larger, more myofibrils

  • Less vascularized

  • Found in muscles that contract less

frequently (eyeball muscles)

[PDF p. 181]

Skeletal muscle cell characteristics

  • 1 cell = 1 fiber.

  • Multinucleated and peripheral location

Long, cylindrical nuclei

Enveloped by an external lamina (basement membrane) and reticular fibers

Has a plasma membrane known as sarcolemma; The sarcolemma forms deep invagination known as T tubules → together with terminal cisternae, form triads

The cytoplasm is known as sarcoplasm, where the most abundant component are cylindrical collections: myofibrils

In the cytoplasm we will also find the sarcoplasmic reticulum, surrounding the myofibrils. It’s specialized

SER which stores Ca2+

  • Small abundant Golgi, surrounding the nucleus

- Mitochondria

- Glycogen

Myofibrils

The abundant myofibrils are 1-2 micrometers in diameter and extend the whole length of the cell. They’re formed by bundles of thick and thin fibers, aligned in a specific pattern that gives place to striations (Banding pattern visible in light microscopy: dark bands = A bands, light bands = I bands, which are bisected by Z discs). This repeated pattern forms the contractile unit of the muscle

cell: the sarcomere .

➢ Sarcomere: formed by portion between two Z discs.

Green arrows: mitochondria

Yellow: sort of vesicles, surrounding the myofibrils = sarcoplasmic reticulum + T tubules

Try to identify the banding pattern and the different lines

Structure of the sarcomere

Contractile unit of striated muscle.

Basic components:

  • In the middle, darker A band
  • Lighter I band, cut through the middle by Z discs (each I band on the sides of the sarcomere is bisected by the Z discs = we have two halves of different I bands)

In the middle of the A band, paler region = H zone,

  • In the middle of the H zone we can find darker lines

corresponding to M lines

Each sarcomere = A band + 2 halves of 2 different I bands

[PDF p. 182]

Structural components: Myofilaments:

Thick filaments = mostly myosin

  • Thin filaments = mostly actin In between, many proteins stabilize them and maintain

the structure:

  • Titin (myosin associated), an elastic protein that

binds thick filaments to Z disc

  • alpha-Actinin (at Z disc), bound to actin
  • Desmin (at Z disc), which binds Z discs to the plasma membrane to transmit the force of the contraction to the whole cell)

- Dystrophin (binding actin to cell membrane)

Myofilaments. Thick and thin They lie parallel to the long axis of the myofibril in a precise arrangement that is responsible for the sarcomere banding pattern.

Thick filaments

Diameter and length: 15nm diameter, 1.5 micrometers long

  • Each thick filament consists of many myosin molecules, whose heads protrude at opposite ends of the filament
  • Each myosin molecule (= myosin II): composed of 2 heads (at each end) and 2 tails The heavy chains which form the tail constitute the central bare zone. Central part of myosin molecules lack myosin heads
  • Light chains form the myosin heads, with bipolar location (heads at the two terminal regions of the thick filament) Located at the interaction sites with actin.

Accessory proteins:

  • Titin : bound to titin, elastic protein which acts as a spring and anchors thick filaments (myosin) to the Z disc.
  • Myomesin : located at the central region (M line), to maintain thick filament cross-linking

Thin filaments

  • 7 nm in diameter (half the thickness) and 1.0 μm long
  • Thin filament (F-actin) consists of 2 strands of actin subunits (G-monomers) twisted into a helix

In addition, 2 regulatory proteins are bound: troponin and tropomyosin, covering the actin active sites - F-actin: a polymer of G-actin monomers arranged in a double helix, which posses an active site that will interact with myosin

[PDF p. 183]

Accessory proteins:

Tropomyosin: two-stranded alpha-helical coiled protein, located in the grooves of the F-actin helix (bound to its active sites )

  • Troponin : binds to calcium and reveals active sites

    Troponin T (TnT): binds troponin complex to tropomyosin

    Troponin I (TnI): binds to actin and troponin T

    • Troponin C (TnC): binds Ca2+

- Alpha-actinin and CapZ

Tropomodulin and nebulin: other associated proteins that help in stabilization

They are organized in a parallel disposition which gives muscle its characteristic striations.

Sarcomere bands and striations

How structure corresponds to the images:

  • A bands: overlapping between thick and thin filaments. 6 thin

filaments surround each thick filament. It stains and is seen as a dark

band.

When muscle contracts, the A band doesn’t change size.

  • I bands: The band corresponds to a region where there are only thin filaments. Appears as a lightly stained band. Bisected by the Z discs.

  • H bands: light zone in the middle of the A band. Corresponds to the bare region of thick filaments, where there are no myosin heads, only myosin tails.

  • M lines: narrow, dark lines in the middle of the H zone. Correspond to cross-connections (M bridges) between adjacent thick filaments. They are crosslinked by myomesin (accessory protein of myosin) located on the M line.

  • Z discs: sarcomere stabilization

Z discs are found bisecting the I bands. Their function is to stabilize the sarcomere. They have many different proteins, highlighting:

    - Titin, for the stabilization of **thick filaments**
    - alpha-actinin and Cap Z, which bind **thin filaments to the Z**

discs to stabilize them with the assistance of nebulin

Also important to have the different sarcomeres bound among them + to other components of the cell:

    - Desmin: In this regard desmin plays a major role:

   - Connecting sarcomeres between them by

anchoring Z discs

   - **Binds myofibrils to other components** of the cell,

like cytoskeleton and cytoplasm = distributes contraction force throughout the cell

[PDF p. 184]

  • Costameres: Peripherally located Z disks are anchored to the sarcolemma through costameres. They are important to transmit contraction to the

whole cell and external environment.

Connection to adjacent cells or fibres also occurs through costameres. - Dystrophin: binds the cytoskeleton of the cell to the ECM through the sarcolemma. Important to transmit contraction force to the extracellular region . Alterations in dystrophin cause effects on muscular movement that lead to muscular dystrophies.

Longitudinal view: Image: N= nuclei; A band, I band, Z bands and H zone; a darker nuclei,

more heterochromatic and flattened

= fibroblast, corresponding to a connective tissue nuclei (CT).

Transverse view: ( B&W)

T-Tubule system

Deep invaginations of the sarcolemma, very important for regulating the contraction of the muscle cell. Responsible for conducting the depolarization deep into the muscle fiber, thus propagating the signal and triggering release of Ca2+ T (transverse) tubules:

  • Run perpendicular (transverse) to the myofibrils

Conduct membrane depolarization deep into fibers

Sarcoplasmic reticulum

Found surrounding the myofibrils.

  • It’s specialized SER, found forming a meshwork surrounding each of the myofibrils.

Very important in contraction as it regulates calcium content inside the cells:

- Sequestering Ca2+ ions – **Relaxation**

- Releasing Ca2+ ions – **Contraction**
  • There are dilations in the SER in the areas proximate to the T tubules, known as terminal cisternae.
  • This structure formed by the T tubule + the terminal

cisternae at both sides are known as triads. In

mammals, these triads are in the intersection between I

and A bands. Important structures as they help to provide uniform contraction throughout the muscle cell.

[PDF p. 185]

Longitudinal cross section: we can observe the triads; but there is something abnormal, their location, as this section corresponds to an amphibian and not mammalian cell. The triads are located on the Z discs, not between the A-I bands.

Muscle motor innervation. Neuromuscular junction

Bunch of muscle fibers innervated by the same neuron = motor end plate (several neuromuscular junctions). However, each fiber is innervated by 1 and only 1 neuron.

Synapse :

  • Action potential in the motor neuron stimulates release of acetylcholine from axon terminal into synaptic cleft
  • Junctional folds → grooves of the sarcolemma to increase surface area, where Ach-receptors are located.

Acetylcholine in synaptic cleft binds Na+ ligand-gated channel receptors and initiates sarcolemma AP

Signal propagation:

  • In the process of transmitting action potential, T tubules play a very important role. They conduct membrane depolarization deep into the cell

  • The terminal cisternae of the SER

(sarcoplasmic ret.), closely located to T tubules, detects this depolarization through voltage-gated channels, which releases calcium in response.

After contraction, Ca2+ pumps put calcium back into the SER to stop the process

Contraction. Sliding filament theory Changes in the amount of overlap between thick and thin filaments within the sarcomeres

allows for contraction and relaxation of muscle fibers.

  • Sliding theory → Sliding of the thick filaments over the thin filaments = contraction

Makes the sarcomere become shorter, and the cell contracts.

Relaxes when sarcomere goes back to its original position.

  • Many sarcomeres contracting at the same time results in muscle contraction .

  • It’s a calcium and ATP (energy) dependent process During contraction:

  • Z discs move closer together

  • I band becomes smaller as the actin

filaments slide towards the M line,

over myosin.

  • H band also becomes smaller : as

the actin slides onto myosin, this area is no longer a bare region of only tails

  • A band (myosin) remains unchanged

[PDF p. 186]

Muscle sensory innervation

Muscle spindle (stretch receptor): muscle spindles are proprioceptors (somatic sensory system), therefore detect the degree of stretching and tension in a muscle.

Forming this muscle spindle, we find intrafusal muscle fibers and other components:

Spindle cells are modified skeletal muscle cells. These cells which make up the muscle spindle are also known as intrafusal fibers. Extrafusal fibers are only involved in contraction, not proprioception.

  • Nerve fibers: spindle cells receive both sensory and autonomic motor innervation:
- Sensory: convey stretch information. Afferent fibers I and II

- Motor: intrafusal fibers are innervated by **gamma motor neurons** _(allow_

contraction of the intrafusal fibers), and extrafusal, by alpha motor neurons. Difference? Alpha motor neurons control muscle contraction involved in voluntary movement, whereas gamma motor neurons control muscle contraction in response to external forces acting on the muscle. Gamma motor neurons induce the involuntary, reflexive movement called the

stretch reflex.

Capsules:

    - Internal: surrounds group of spindle cells and nerve fibers
    - External connective tissue sheath around the whole spindle

Skeletal muscle regeneration. Satellite cells

Skeletal muscle can hypertrophy by building up protein. Skeletal muscles cannot divide, but there are progenitor cells: satellite cells (probably residual cells coming from embryonic development).

  • They are wrapped within the same basement membrane (within the external lamina) as skeletal muscle cells
  • Connected to them through M-cadherin molecules

Progenitor cells = already committed to the myogenic lineage.

  • Function: to repair muscle cells in the case of muscle degeneration: Satellite cells become activated under the influence of myogenic regulatory factors and become myoblasts, finally differentiating into mature muscle cells. Difficult to differentiate them from fibroblasts, but we can see they are within the same

basement membrane as the skeletal muscle cells

[PDF p. 187]

Muscle histology II. Cardiac & smooth

CARDIAC MUSCLE

  • Cardiac muscle is striated: it has the same contractile machinery as skeletal muscle, although with some differences in innervation and contraction
  • It’s self excitatory
  • It’s electrically coupled
  • The rate of contraction is regulated by the SNA
  • We don’t have motor end plates. The innervation is neuroendocrine

Cardiac muscle cells features

  • nuclei are centrally located

1 or 2 nuclei per cell

  • Accumulation of organelles surrounding the nucleus = paler region
  • The shape differs: they are not cylindrical, there is branching of the fibers
  • The fiber is formed by many different cells, connected by

structures seen as darkened lines: intercalated disks

Intercalated disks are found connecting the cells

  • Striations are weaker, more difficult to see

Other features:

  • More abundant mitochondria (up to 40% of cell volume)
  • Some Golgi, mostly found in the pale region surrounding the nuclei

Glycogen

Sarcoplasmic reticulum surround the myofibrils, but it

doesn’t form terminal cisternae around the T tubules

  • T tubules, which are wider than in skeletal muscle and

less abundant

As there are no terminal cisternae, we don’t talk about

triads, but diads

In cardiac muscle, sarcoplasmic reticulum & T-tubules appear as diads at Z lines

To distinguish cardiac and skeletal muscle in cross

sectional view:

Look at the nuclei: peripheral or centered

T tubule/SR diads, on the area

corresponding to Z lines.

https://youtu.be/v5KBJuT9QRc Cardiac muscle 2 - cells. Wendy Riggs

[PDF p. 188]

Intercalated discs

Interdigitated blunt cytoplasmic expansions which are responsible for mechanically and electrically coupling heart cells. They are aligned with the Z discs. They run both transverse and laterally:

  • Transverse portion: contains fascia adherens (aka zonula adherens) and desmosomes. Forms mechanical coupling
  • Lateral portion: contains gap junctions which transmit the electrical, contractile electrical signal through ion transport. Forms electrical coupling.

Cardiac muscle contraction

The heartbeat is regulated by modified cardiac muscle cells called cardiac conducting

cells:

- Nodes

Purkinje fibers

Contraction: overall, the process is similar but with some differences:

As in skeletal muscle, contraction is Ca2+ dependent

Contraction initiates through contact between Purkinje fibers and cardiac myocytes.

Signal from Purkinje fibers is transmitted from cardiac muscle cell to cardiac muscle cell through GAP junctions (electrical coupling → ion transport).

  • Upon signal and depolarization, Calcium enters from extracellular region, through the T tubule
  • Ca2+ from the T tubule is transported to the sarcoplasm
  • Once in the cell triggers the release of more intracellular calcium

from the SER.

[PDF p. 189]

SMOOTH MUSCLE

Smooth muscle characteristics

  • It’s a fusiform, non-striated muscle. No striations in the cells.
  • Each fiber will be formed by just a single cell.
  • We will find layers of smooth muscle in many organs (vessels, GI, urogenital organs,…), and isolated fibers in other organs (dermis, spleen, eye,..)

Contraction

  • Contraction is non voluntary
  • Contraction in smooth muscle is modulated by

different mechanisms:

- **Innervation** (varicosities)

- **chemical stimuli** (hormones)
  • Its contraction is slower than that of skeletal muscle

Organization

Layers in different orientations . Muscle layers run at right angles to each other (90º) :

Longitudinal layer of smooth muscle

Circular layer of smooth muscle

Smooth muscle, longitudinal section: In the longitudinal view, pay attention to the nuclei, as not to confuse it with connective

tissue.

  • Muscle nuclei are elongated but more rounded, and more euchromatic.

  • Fibroblasts are heterochromatic and much flatter

Smooth muscle, transverse section:

In transverse view: individual cells with nuclei in the center

In electron microscopy: euchromatic, elongated nuclei

[PDF p. 190]

Ultrastructure of smooth muscle

- Fusiform

Single, centrally located nucleus. As the cell gets contracted, nuclei take an typical cork-screw shape

  • Muscle cells are arranged in compact groups
  • These bundles are kept together by connective tissue.

The cells themselves are surrounded by basal membrane (type IV collagen) + reticular fibers (type III collagen), which form the endomysium .

They don’t have T tubules or sarcoplasmic reticulum; instead possess many pinocytic caveolae and SER ( in the periphery)

Secretory phenotype: have a relatively active nucleus, RER, Golgi, mitochondria Produce: Collagen (type I, III and IV) laminin, elastin, proteoglycans

They don’t form fibers, but connect through GAP junctions

The most abundant component in the cytoplasm will still be contractile fibers, although

’ they don t form sarcomeres → lack of striations (distinctive feature)

Thin filaments: very abundant

- Actin

Tropomyosin (smooth muscle form)

Caldesmon and calponin (actin binding proteins) . Instead of troponin, there are other binding proteins.

Thick filaments:

Smooth muscle myosin (special type of myosin. In unmodified state, it has low affinity for actin = no need for troponin covering the actin binding sites)

  • Side polar orientation, with no central bare zone (vs. bipolar in skeletal muscle). There is no bipolar organization of the heads, but a side-polar orientation (no bare region = no H zone or central line)

We need some sort of structure to stabilize and transmit the

contraction to the rest of the cell. For this purpose, we find dense bodies (similar to Z lines in skeletal and cardiac): These dense bodies, like Z lines, are membrane associated. Made up of:

  • Intermediate filaments plaques that help to stabilize and transmit

the contraction

○ Desmin

○ Vimentin

- alpha-actinin

Other adhesion proteins (vinculin, talin)

They bind actin, connecting filaments between them and with the ECM to

transmit the force.

[PDF p. 191]

Smooth muscle contraction

  • Much slower than in skeletal muscle

  • Also calcium dependent

  • No motor end plate: the innervation occurs at a distance . The neurotransmitters diffuse from the axon of the neuron to receptors. Therefore, we don’t talk about synapse regions but varicosities

- 2 types of innervation:

- Visceral (unitary): very **few nerve endings,** but the cells

are connected through gap junctions. Therefore, they are electrically coupled, which ensures a rhythmic or tonic

contraction

- Multiunit contraction: very **few gap junctions,** practically

every cell is individually innervated. This allows them to contract rapidly and individually for more precise control.

MUSCLE REGENERATION AND GROWTH

Skeletal muscle

  • Skeletal muscle can grow, increase in size → hypertrophy (depositing collagen)
  • Can increase in number. Satellite cells are the ones thought to provide a source of regeneration, as skeletal muscle cells cannot divide themselves.

Smooth muscle

  • Smooth muscle can increase in size and hypertrophy

  • Can increase in number:

- Can **divide and proliferate** _(ex: uterine myometrium)_

- There are also **mesenchymal cells** which can differentiate and regenerate

smooth muscle. Vascular pericytes

Cardiac muscle

  • Cardiac muscle can hypertrophy and increase in size .

There is some potential capacity of regeneration, but there are limited possibilities of increase in number. Can occur through:

    circulating progenitor cells _(post-infarction tissue remodeling by fibroblasts)._
    - Resident stem cells _(new evidences)_

MUSCLE HISTOLOGY. SUMMARY

[PDF p. 192]

Integration of basic tissues

In the cyst (abnormal cavity), we find areas formed by epithelial tissue, and others formed by specialized connective tissue: adipose tissue. We can also observe hair, something you wouldn’t expect to find in an ovary, within areas formed by a creamy substance (probably keratin) and a more solid substance (adipose tissue) on the left.

- Stratified squamous keratinized epithelial

tissue

- Hair follicle Exocrine glands next to hair follicle = sebaceous glands, acinar

structure

[PDF p. 193]

White adipose tissue, formed by adipocytes (big cells with a large lipid droplet which displaces the nuclei to the side)

Hyaline cartilage, surrounded by a layer of perichondrium, containing chondroblasts and chondrocytes (within lacunae, totally surrounded by type II collagen). We can also observe matrix: territorial (more fibers) and interterritorial matrix (more proteoglycans). Isogenous groups within the same areas of territorial matrix. Made up of type I collagen. Basal membrane = type IV collagen; reticular fibers = type III collagen.

  • We can observe bone tissue: totally mature bone tissue (calcified). We can see cortical bone, in which concentric structures are observed, surrounding a Harvessian canal. Containing osteocytes, surrounded by a concentric matrix of type I collagen.

[PDF p. 194]

  • Very fibrillar matrix area, supporting a very important and delicate tissue. In between the lines, small round cells. From time to time, bigger round cells can be observed. These round cells are glial cells (giving support) . We are looking at nervous tissue (very fibrillar) . The bigger cells that can be found from time to time

are neurons.

Nervous tissue calcifies with age. This is what we can see within this nervous

tissue. Concentric calcifications.

  • We can find muscle (redder) surrounded by dense irregular CT. Muscles can be striated (skeletal or cardiac) or smooth. In this muscle we cannot see striations, and nuclei are located in the center of the muscle fibers. Therefore, this is smooth

muscle.

The surrounding CT is lighter pink as it contains less proteins, and has more elongated cells (fibroblasts).

Name the lesion: this tumor is known as teratoma .

[PDF p. 195]

Electrical properties of cell membranes

Basic concepts

INTRODUCTION

Key ideas about electric properties

  • Electric forces are conservative forces. The work that is done by an electric force only depends on the initial and final point, but is independent from the trajectory. If we do a circular trajectory, W=0.

  • An electric field is a real quantity. It’s a vector, not a scalar quantity: depending on the direction of the field, we will obtain different results.

  • The electric current in the human body is caused due to the displacement of ions. This displacement is the source of electric current. In electric circuits, it’s caused by the displacement of electrons.

  • As this is a conservative system, mechanical energy remains constant. It consists in the balance of kinetic and potential energy. If one increases, the other decreases, as to maintain a constant Em.

Electric field lines and isopotential lines are perpendicular.

Electricity comes in 2 flavours: positive and negative charges, whose charge corresponds to 1.6 · 10^ -19 C, either with + or - sign.

  • There are repulsive forces (interaction between particles with same electric charge) or attractive forces (particles with different electric charge). Different to gravity, where we only find attractive forces.

Charged particle interactions

Situation: we place a + charge and place a “probe” charge. If the probe is also +, the force exerted will be a repulsive force. This can be explained either by the appearance of an interaction between the particles, or because this charge interacts with another magnitude known as electric field, in such a way that the force will be equal to the charge of the affected particle (probe) times the electric field created by our positive particle.

This last version is correct: the interaction appears between the charge and the electric field.

[PDF p. 196]

  • When we have a positive charge, we say we have a source of electric field lines, found pointing outwards.
    • When we have a negative charge, we have a sink of electric field lines, found pointing inwards.

(convention: we establish this supposing that we will be using a + charge. Therefore, when the field is created by a + charge = repels, outwards lines. Source; when it’s created by a - charged = attracts, pointing inwards. Sink)

In the human body it’s rare to find isolated charges. We usually find many charges. To analyze their behaviour, we must know, what is the global behaviour of the electric field? To analyze it and answer this question, we can apply the superposition principle . We will

be able to recover a total value.

Example: a sodium ion perceives the existence of an electric field formed by another ion. There will be an electric force created on the system (formula) due to the field : if we suppose there is no friction, the result will be that the ion will begin to move in the direction of the field lines. There will be an acceleration, and the kinetic energy will increase. As previously mentioned, because this is a conservative system, the kinetic energy has to be in balance with the potential energy. If one increases, the other will decrease. Here we can introduce a new magnitude, known as electric potential voltage (V):

POTENTIAL ENERGY

For a + charge:

Potential energy decreases from point 1 to point 2 when we move along an electric field line in the same direction as the field (away from a + charge creating the field, or towards the - charge creating it, as Ek increases) .

If we are moving in the opposite direction, the potential energy of the system will increase (towards a + or away from a -, as Ek decreases) For a - charge:

  • Potential energy (Ep) increases when we move in the same direction
  • Ep decreases when we move in the opposite direction

ELECTRIC POTENTIAL

When we move along a field line, the electric potential changes, independent of the charge:

  • If we move in the opposite direction, potential (V) will increase
  • If we move in the same direction as the field lines, V will decrease

FOR ANY CHARGED PARTICLE, + or -

[PDF p. 197]

ELECTRIC BEHAVIOUR OF A CELL

Impermeable membrane

We will focus on the membrane: when considering a cell, we have a small volume of fluid

enclosed inside a membrane.

In principle, this membrane is impermeable (closed barrier), permeability = 0. Inside this cavity we find water but also other components, ions or small charged proteins.

What is the interaction between these particles and other ions found on the outer part of the cell?

We introduce a few sodium ions. What will happen? Due to the presence of all the other particles, on each of them there will be a repulsive force. The expected result is that these ions will try to separate, and will finally be placed on the inner side of the membrane. This is what would be expected if we only have sodium ions. However, in a real system we find many other charged particles, + and -, placed randomly inside this volume. There is a huge amount of positive and negative ions present. Therefore, one may ask, what is the total force experienced by one single particular ion? This total force will be 0 if the total number of positive and negative charges is balanced .

In other words, we can reach an equilibrium of that system if the total charge (Q) is 0. In order to have an equilibrium situation, we need to fulfill this condition (Qt = 0)

This is the situation found in a resting

cell: balance between + and - . There

is charge neutrality.

Membrane permeability for single ion

Now let’s consider the real situation, in which the permeability of the membrane isn’t 0, although selective. This selective permeability of the membrane means that only one of the single ions (+ or -) will be able to cross the barrier, in this situation.

We are in a situation in which Qt inside = 0 and Qt

outside = 0 (normal situation) . However, the concentration of a positive ion A is greater inside than outside; the same happens with the concentrations of negative ion B.

[PDF p. 198]

Will this situation persist?

In the human body the temperature is NOT = 0; it’s 37ºC. This is important as temperature means vibration. These ions aren’t stationary, but slightly vibrating, so there will be a small random displacement of all these ions. This random displacement is the mechanism that will create the resting potential in the cells of the human body.

If the temp were = 0, there would be no change in the equilibrium of the system. However, this is not the case. There is a small amount of kinetic energy, so each one of these molecules will follow a random trajectory and will eventually arrive at the membrane:

  • If the membrane is impermeable for the specific ion, they will collide and come back if (membrane doesn’t allow their trespass).
  • But if there is permeability (in this situation, there is, only for ion A) : if one of the incoming moving ions hits one of the pores, it can cross the membrane. It’s an event that may occur for the positive charge (ion A), but not for the negative charge as the membrane is not permeable for it. If this ion B hits one of the holes, the result will be to change its direction.

Due to the selective permeability, only one of the ions will be able to cross the barrier. This ion transports charge. Therefore, the ion that has crossed causes the Qt outside to be + and the Qt inside to become negative.

In order to see if this is a stable situation, we have to check if this diffusion from left to right can continue in time. What is the probability to cross from left to right? Can we go back to the

initial situation?

The probability will depend on the number of ions we have on each side. The probability of crossing will be higher when the concentration of ions on the other side is greater. Therefore, we will create a flux of ions, all going from left to right.

The result: the total charge inside is smaller than 0, and the total charge outside is larger than 0.

[PDF p. 199]

As we said, the system tends to transition to its stable state, when Qt = 0. Therefore, the inner volume will divide into 2 parts, as to maximize the distance between the excess of particles (due to repulsive forces, they press against the membrane):

  • The negative charges from the inside will be placed on the inner face of the

membrane

  • The positive charges which have exited the cell will place themselves on the outer

face of the membrane

There will be an excess of + on the outer

face of the membrane and an excess of -

on the inside (talking about A and B) . Only if there is an excess of particles on the inner

and outer side of the membrane will there be

Qt different than 0. When we move away from the membrane and separate, having a more global view, Qt REMAINS being 0 (we are talking in relation to proximity of the membrane)

Therefore, Qt is greater and smaller than 0 only in the regions that are proximate to

the membrane.

Coming back to our question: is this going to continue forever, or will there be a mechanism to stop this diffusion process?

Because of diffusion, positive charges will continue to try and cross the membrane from left to right. However, now there is the presence of positive charges all around the outer face, exerting a force pointing in the opposite direction. Diffusion will have to fight against the repulsive forces created by the + charges located on the

outer membrane.

In other words, due to the generation of an electric field, a voltage is created between the inner and outer part. Voltage = difference of Potential energy (E p ) between 2 points

When is equilibrium reached, putting an end to the pass of molecules? Equilibrium appears when the energy due to the electric force (created by repulsive forces on the outside of the membrane) is equivalent to the energy available because of

the vibration.

[PDF p. 200]

(Ep elect =Energy that you can obtain from the electric field. Potential energy obtained by the electric forces) The voltage potential on a membrane in an equilibrium situation will be:

This concept, in which Ep elect = E vibr is known as

MEMBRANE RESTING POTENTIAL.

What happens if the concentration is similar on both sides? the log=0. The nernst potential is 0.

Nernst constant / Kb = 26.7 mV in the case of Na+,

Cl- and K+

How would we define the membrane resting potential / Nernst potential for each individual ion? It’s the potential at which ion X stops flowing from in → out, as we have reached equilibrium between the force driving it and the force stopping it. For each particular ion, this equilibrium is reached at specific concentrations, which will determine the potential at which the process stops and the situation is sustained.

SUMMARY:

The concentration of each ions inside and outside are different (starting situation), but there is charge neutrality. Our membrane becomes permeable to ion A only. Some of the ions will be able to cross the barrier instead of bouncing off. There is a displacement of ions because of diffusion . As soon as a small amount of charges are placed on the outer membrane, there is an electric force pointing in the other direction. The process stops when diffusion is balanced by the electric field created by the outer charges. There is a voltage for which there is equilibrium between diffusion and electric potential (membrane resting potential).

KEY POINTS

  • Inner and outer concentrations remain CONSTANT, even if there is a small amount of particles crossing. It’s a very tiny amount compared to the total amount of ions. Therefore, no modification in concentration.

Total charge inside and outside the cell is ALWAYS 0 in a global view, when we

are far from the membrane.

[PDF p. 201]

MEMBRANE POTENTIAL IN A REAL CELL

In our cells, there are 3 ions for which the membrane is permeable: Na+, K+ and Cl-.

In resting potential, they have their respective ion concentrations. From these values we can obtain the logarithm by using the formula, and therefore, obtain the Nernst potential for

each ion:

Nernst potentials:

Expected results when we consider only one of these ions:

                       - +60 mV

                       - -90 mV

                       - -70 mV

The values are the results obtained when we consider

the membrane is permeable only for each of the ions. +60 mV is the value we obtain if only Na+ can cross the membrane, -90mV if only K+ could cros,…etc

However, we cannot separate them. They are all found together, and Nernst potential is only valid for when considering 1 ion.

To recover the real value of membrane’s potential, we cannot use Nernst potential only, we have to combine all the different factors; if we combine everything, introducing in the formula the concentrations of each ion (in and out) and their respective permeabilities, we get an expression for membrane potential:

P Cl- is so small, it can be excluded from the formula. And PNa = 0 ‘002 Pk. Introducing this in the formula, we get that the membrane potential on a cell is -70 mV. The limit values are -90 mV (Nernst potential for K+) and +60 mV (Nernst potential for Na+) The correct, real value for membrane potential, when considering all the factors, is that the voltage (for example in a neuron) is -70 mV.

[PDF p. 202]

What controls membrane potential?

It is controlled by permeability.

We can recover different values by modifying the permeability.

  • If the permeability for potassium (P k+ ) is really high (closed Na+ gates), we can

recover values of -90 mV.

  • If we close K+ gates, we increase permeability for sodium and recover +60 mV.

Depending on if we depolarize or hyperpolarize the membrane, the potential will increase

or decrease.

If we cross a specific threshold for Na+ permeability, we can reach an activation potential (+60 mV).

* Water is not crossing the barrier. And we are talking about very small particles. There is no great change in concentration, so there will be no flow of water across the membrane. It’s not an osmotic process

[PDF p. 203]

Electrical properties of membranes

Dynamics. https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=iBDXOt_uHTQ (Action Potential in Neurons,

Animation, Alila Medical Media)
