Previous class. Summary Nernst potential only appears because there is competition between diffusion (permeability different than 0) and an electric potential. Transfer of ions will stop when there is equilibrium between the diffusion and electric processes.

Due to the different concentrations, there is a possibility to modify the membrane potential. All this behaviour is controlled by the action of the sodium-potassium pump, which establishes a gradient (pumps Na+ out, K+ in) after each action potential so a new one can be triggered: Sodium = higher concentrations on the outside → will enter by diffusion when Na+ voltage gated channels are opened, at the beginning of a new action potential (depolarization) Potassium = higher concentrations inside the cell, will reinstate resting potential by exiting the cell after the Na+ influx (repolarization)

Equation that controls electric potential across the membrane:

Permeability for Cl- ion is roughly 0, so we can differentiate 2 situations:

- when the permeability of the membrane for Na+ is almost 0. Therefore, the potential of the membrane is close to the Nernst potential for Potassium. Found outside and inside. Corresponds to the resting potential.

- when the permeability for potassium is nearly 0. The terms on the equation are switched, and now the membrane potential resembles the Nernst potential for sodium. We will find sodium inside as well as outside. Corresponds to the initiation of the action potential.

To switch membrane potential, we have to change permeability (open canals). When we do this,

diffusion will be the mechanism that will drive the

change in potential and try to reinstate the equilibrium.

[PDF p. 204]

How does the cell modify the polarity and electric membrane potential when we switch the permeability of the membrane?

Our starting point is 55mV (action potential reached). Permeability for sodium is different from 0, and P k is similar to 0, very small compared to that of Na.

Sodium is the one which will drive the behaviour of

the system: somehow (we will see now), there is a modification of the permeability, making the P Na = 0 (approx 0) and the permeability for potassium different from 0. There is a transition, back to the resting potential. How much time is needed for this transition? Will this be a fast process?

Let’s start from the beginning. In the 1st configuration, the membrane potential is +55mV. In the final situation, polarity is reversed and we have a membrane potential of -90 mV.

  • 1st situation: 55mV = there are negative charges on the outer face of the membrane, + on the inner

inside. +Q1 inside, -Q1 outside

  • 2nd situation: -90mV = reverse situation.

Negative charges on the inner side, Positive on the outer. -Q2 inside, +Q2 outside

V (membrane potential) = V inner - V out

When we are far from the membrane, Qt still = 0.

To reverse the potential, we need a charge transport. A total charge needs to be transported from the inner side to the outer side. For this purpose, we need to transfer a specific amount of ions (around 10^ 11 ).

How many ions do we need to transport from one side to the other?

This can be answered by answering 2 questions:

- What is the total charge that needs to be transported?

How fast can these charges cross the barrier? What is the intensity? (charge/second = electric current or Intensity). The unit for this electric current (I) is Ampere (Amp).

[PDF p. 205]

1. What is the total charge that needs to be transported?

In this system we have a membrane with + and - charges, and a 55mV potential (AP).

When reaching a specific membrane potential, we know there is a relation between the charges being transported and the potential being applied on the system. The more charges which are transported, the higher differential potential (voltage) between both sides of the membrane.

Can we determine the value of the constant which relates charge and voltage relationship?

We can do an analogy with a battery: a battery consists of 2 metallic plates, separated a certain distance . In between these 2 plates, electrical field lines cross from the + (source) to the - (sink). This structure is known as a capacitor, used in many electrical devices as it can use its electrical field to store charge, and therefore, store energy. Take our cell phones for instance: the charges cannot cross this separation. But when we charge a battery, what we do is inject + on one side and - on the other, which allows us to store charge and therefore, energy. The plates are connected and the energy stored on both sides is able to be

used.

This analogy is similar to what happens in the cellular membrane. The cell membrane would be equivalent to the capacitor . Thanks to the analogy, we

can derive the formula:

Where the constant C corresponds to a value known as capacitance (measured in Farads, F): it measures the ability to store charge in a specific device.

The point which is relevant: this capacitance depends on the area of the plates, the separation and the material that is in between the plates (known as permittivity ).

  • In the cell membrane, the distance corresponds to the thickness of the membrane (roughly 4- 10 nm).
  • The area corresponds to the area of the specific cell

Epsilon (permittivity) corresponds to the material found in between the membrane. The constant value, permittivity, therefore depends on the material.

In a typical neuron, this capacitance value corresponds to:

10 ^ -2 F/m2

https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=u-jigaMJT10: Capacitors & Capacitance, Khan academy

[PDF p. 206]

Situation: we consider a spherical neuron. The diameter is 1 micrometer. The area will correspond to the area of a sphere, therefore A = 4pi · 0,25 · 10^- 12 m 2 , roughly 3 · 10 -12 m 2 .

Therefore, the capacitance value is 3 · 10^ -14 F, quite a small value. Applying the formula, the total charge of our system will be:

Q1 = 3 ·10 ^ -14 times the membrane potential, 55mV, which gives 16,500 · 10^- 19 C.

How many ions do we need to transfer?

Only 10^ 4 ios will need to be transported from one side to another. Q2 = 1.5 · 10 ^4 . Therefore, Qt = 25000 ions.

  • The capacitance of a cell cannot be modified. In a normal cell, it’s a constant, a property. Can only be modified by modifying the cell itself (area, distance between the sides of the neuron,..). The one that affects the dynamics will be the intensity.

2. How fast can these charges cross the barrier (Intensity)?

The membrane has channels, which make it permeable for ion A+. Through these pores, water and ions can cross. Because of the potential there is an E field acting on the system, meaning there is a force pushing the ion to the outer side of the cell (+ ion being moved to the - side).

  • If the situation were done in vacuum (no friction force), the dynamic of the ion would be an accelerated displacement of it as it goes through the membrane.
  • But this is not the situation. In this system we have water, so there are collisions with the molecules. Some of the energy being used to transport the ions is being lost through collisions with those molecules. There is an equilibrium between the force acceleration and the force produced by collisions (friction), and therefore, the velocity is constant.

[PDF p. 207]

If velocity is constant Intensity is also constant as “I” is proportional to velocity of the charged particle ( I = charges/second (v) = E current).

�V (voltage across the membrane, produced by the presence of an electric field) will cause a force that drives charged particles across the membrane at a specific intensity

A magnitude will exert friction force and establish the relationship there will be between the voltage and the intensity it will be able to induce (how many charges will be able to cross per second)

Ohm’s law establishes this linear proportionality, as for a given voltage there is a specific intensity. The proportionality between potential (V) and Intensity (I) is given by resistance (R)

In other words, whenever the ion is trying to cross, the material will exert a resistance which tries to stop these ions. We will have to overcome this resistance in order to transfer the ions . This resistance depends on:

  • Resistivity (depends on the specific medium, in this case water. Will determine the exerted Fr )

  • Area of the channel

- Distance

By using this expression, we can derive that the I that can cross the barrier at a specific voltage depends on the resistance. But this is the situation for a single channel.

The total intensity will be the combination of all the specific intensities. The value we obtain is the membrane potential between the total resistance, which is:

distance · resistivity / Area · number of channels

The point is that the total intensity is proportional to the number of channels. It depends on the number of channels: ↓ n of channels = ↓ T intensity across the membrane

[PDF p. 208]

When we are analyzing the intensity, we cannot modify the type of channel or thickness, but we can control the value of the resistance by changing the permeability (number of channels)

  • If permeability is reduced (reduced number of channels), the resistance increases
  • If we increase the permeability (increased number of channels), the resistance

decreases

We have all the gradients that we need to analyze the question initially posed.

What’s the behaviour in between both situations? How does the membrane switch the

potential by changing the permeability of the membrane?

Before P1, there is no intensity crossing the barrier as we are in equilibrium. But because of the modification of permeability, there is an increase in the intensity (charges crossing/sec) and we find an intensity circulating (V/R, R being controlled by permeability). The green voltage corresponds to the represented difference.

The intensity is not constant during the transition between P1 and P2. As we approach the final barrier, “I” will get smaller and smaller due to the change in permeabilities. This is done following an exponential behaviour which is marked by a specific time, the time constant, that is determined by resistance and capacitance (C = constant; R = can be modified by playing with permeability).

𝝉 = R · C

Situations:

  • When R is large for the ion which is causing the change in potential, the transition will be slow . It’ll take a long time to do the transition = ↑ time constant
  • When R is small, the transition will be fast = ↓ time constant

A pathology that reduces the number of open channels or the concentration of ions (permeability) will slow down the behaviour.

[PDF p. 209]

Diagram: it’s the electronic equivalent of a neuron membrane, the most simple electronic analogy to represent the electronic activity of a cell. It represents a set of discrete electronic

devices which are connected.

  • On the right = the membrane ( capacitor which controls the charge stored at the system)

To the left, the sodium potassium-pump, always working and always present, pushing Na+ outside and potassium inside to reinstate

Three on the left = represent the Nernst potentials for each ion (sodium battery = reverse orientation). The resistor they contain symbolizes the permeabilities

KEY POINTS

Modifying Temperature modifies the absolute value of Nernst potential, not the sign (as it’s found outside the ln)

Permeability

- Reduce K+ permeability = membrane potential will increase > -70mV

- Increase Na+ permeability = membrane potential will increase > -70mV

[PDF p. 210]

Time and length constants

TIME CONSTANT

“Amount of time it takes for the change in potential to reach 63% of its final value” The time constant is an index of how rapidly a membrane would respond to a stimulus in time. It depends on R and C:

  • Rm is membrane resistance, it’s inversely proportional to permeability. Decreasing permeability (n of open channels) increases resistance = increases the time constant. It will take longer to reach the final potential.
  • C is the capacitance, represents the ability to store charge across a membrane. The higher the capacitance, higher ability to store = it will take more time to “charge up the membrane” up to its maximum storing capacity = increases the time constant

In both cases, it will take longer to establish the differential potential we are trying to reach.

LENGTH CONSTANT

“Index of how well a subthreshold potential will spread along an axon, as a function of

distance”

  • Rm is once again the membrane resistance, inverse to permeability. Increasing Rm means there will be less chance for the charges flowing through the membrane to escape. Less charges leak out, therefore more moving along the membrane = ↑ length constant. Increasing Rm is like putting insulation on an axon
  • Ri is the internal resistance, represents the ability of charges to move along the axon. It’s an intrinsic property of the axoplasm.

PROPAGATION VELOCITY

High propagation velocity

How can this propagation velocity be increased?

By increasing the axon’s diameter, we decrease Ri = bigger length constant

By covering it in myelin . What myelin will do is:

- Increase membrane resistance (as it covers up channels, permeability is

decreased and resistance increases = longer length constant)

- Increases separation between the faces of the membrane = reduced

capacitance

- Decreases amount of charge stored = reduced capacitance

[PDF p. 211]

Membrane potential workshop

Situation at RMP: thanks to the action of a Na+/K+ pump, extracellular sodium concentration is higher than the intracellular, and intracellular K+ > extracellular K+. At this point, K+ permeability is quite high while the permeability for sodium is close to 0 (pNa+ = 0.02pK+, in resting situation). RMP is almost exclusively controlled by K+ at this point, but there is still a small contribution from Na+, making it -70mV and not -90mV. A change in Na+ concentration or permeability will still have a small effect on RMP.

Depolarization will occur because pNa+ increases and allows Na+ influx into the cell, making membrane potential positive.

1st scenario

A patient has severe hyponatremia . Suppose that in this situation the extracellular sodium concentration is 120 mmol/L. 1ST SCENARIO: Explain how this value affects the Nernst potential of Sodium and Potassium ions.

Levels of sodium in blood (extracellular) are very low (hyponatremia) What happens with Nernst potential for sodium and potassium in this patient?

Sodium Nernst potential will decrease

Potassium Nernst potential will remain unchanged. Only the Na potential is modified.

Hyponatremia would mean the Na+ gradient is decreased. Therefore, the pass of ions through Na+ channels will be lower. Less entry during repolarization = less + charges being deposited on the inner surface of the cell, RMP will become more negative.

2nd scenario

Now, consider the situation of a patient that has hypokalemia , that implies a decrease of the extracellular potassium concentration to 3 mmol/L (millimoles per Litre). Explain how this value affects the Nernst potential of Sodium and Potassium ions.

[PDF p. 212]

Decrease of extracellular potassium (hypokalemia)

Normal values = 5 mmol/L

Hypokalemia = 3 mmol/L

Effect on Nernst potential?

K Nernst potential will decrease

Na Nernst potential will remain unchanged

Extracellular K+ concentration decreases = potassium gradient is increased, more charges will tend to flow out of the cell and more + charges will be deposited on the outer surface, making the inner more negative = the RMP will decrease under -70mV, becoming nearly -90 mV. It’s more difficult to trigger an action potential, as the resting potential is decreased.

3rd scenario

Explain the consequences if the sodium permeability reduces to one half the reference value. Does this modification affect the resting potential?

During an action potential:

depolarization occurs when sodium permeability increases

repolarization when K permeability increases

We are analyzing the resting potential, previous to depolarization (left side of the diagram) What will happen if we are in a situation where the permeability for Na is decreased? Will it

have an effect on RMP?

The resting potential appears because there is a contribution from potassium and

sodium.

Permeability for sodium is 2% of the permeability for potassium. This is why the resting potential is -70 and not -90 mV; there is a small contribution from Na+ to the potential (K+ exiting the cell, small amount of Na+ entering it and making it less negative, not fully -90)

If there stops being Na contribution or less contribution (ex: reduced permeability, or in the case we saw before, reduced concentration gradient due to hyponatremia), the new resting potential will be below -70mV. It will decrease. PNa+ does affect RMP. Difference with the previous situation: there is a decrease in resting potential but not due to decrease in Nernst potential, but due to a decrease in permeability.

[PDF p. 213]

4th scenario

Explain how a reduction of the sodium permeability affects the depolarization and repolarization characteristic times (time constants).

In the resting state, there is K+ efflux (open K+ channels) and a small influx of Na+. However during repolarization, from the highest point of depolarization to hyperpolarization, sodium has no effect on membrane potential as the channels are closed = no Na+ permeability.

Depolarization The effect of Na+ permeability on depolarization: it will take longer to reach depolarization Repolarization Repolarization time will remain unchanged as it depends on the permeability of potassium.

SOCRATIVE

[PDF p. 214]

BASIC NEURONAL AND MUSCULAR FUNCTION

[PDF p. 215]

Membrane & action potentials

Introduction

At a basal (resting) state:

We are at the resting potential (-70 mV), for which potassium is mainly responsible.

How is it reached?

There are different concentrations in and out of the cell, for each ion. Therefore, diffusion will tend to move these ions in favour of the concentration gradient

As they are charged particles, while this movement proceeds, an electric force will appear due to the excess accumulation of charges on the opposite face of the

membrane

Diffusion will have to fight this electric force. The flow of ions will stabilize when the chemical force driven by diffusion is in equilibrium with the electric force exerted in the opposite direction

At this point, the resting membrane potential is established

The resting membrane potential is negative. Why?

At resting potential, the few resting-Na channels are open, carrying out this mentioned process. Na+ voltage-gated channels are closed. The K+ resting channels are much more abundant than the Na+ ones. Therefore, the resting membrane potential is very close to that of K+ Nernst potential (although not quite the same, as the resting Na channels play a role. PNa+ = 0.02 PK+ ) At rest, the inner side of the membrane is negative and the outer, positive.

    If the membrane were only permeable to Na+, the membrane potential would

be positive (as sodium is found at higher concentration out of the cell and it would tend to enter, increasing the n of + charges inside the cell)

    If it were only permeable for K+, V would be even more negative than it is.

The inner side of the cell would become negative as + charges would tend to

exit the cell.

The Goldman equation reflects the real situation, by taking into account all of the ions that contribute to establishing the membrane potential (combination of the Nernst potentials):

[PDF p. 216]

ACTION POTENTIAL

The action potential is necessary to transmit information through the axons of neurons. It’s a sequence of opening and closing of voltage-gated channels.

It appears due to a sequence of opening and closure of voltage-gated sodium and potassium channels. This sequence begins when the cell is depolarized to a certain

threshold

It is an all-or-nothing process

It allows the propagation of an electrical current without decrement despite the

distance

It supports electrical transmission through axons (neurons) and muscle fibers

VG sodium channels

This action potential is reached thanks to a specific type of subchannels: voltage-gated

sodium channels.

It has 2 different gates: Fast gate (involved in activation) and a Slow gate (involved in deactivation). They are proteins that change their conformation when a specific voltage is reached. Ther activity is opposite.

  • Rest : at normal potential, the fast gate is closed and the slow gate is open.

  • Activation: when we reach the threshold (-60 mV), the fast gate opens and the slow gate closes after a while. There is a 1ms time difference between the opening of the fast and the closing of the slow. During this brief period, the channel remains open (both gates are open) and sodium can enter the cell. Membrane potential will increase, becoming much less negative and reaching positive

values.

This explains why the action potential is always the same: the gates are always or always closed the same amount of time.

  • Deactivation: After a while, the channel is deactivated as the slow gate finally closes. Sodium stops flowing into the cell and the process decreases.

VG potassium channels

To go back to a normal voltage, the sodium potassium pump actively transfers Na+ ions back out. But this is a very slow

process. To reinstate the potential, there are two main events that

contribute:

  • The closing of the voltage-gated Na+ channel occurs (slow and fast gate)

Voltage-gated K+ channels open. Why now?

because their activation is slower than that of Na

voltage-gated channels.

[PDF p. 217]

The rapid exit of potassium and the slowed sodium influx will allow the membrane to recover negative values, even below the resting potential (hyperpolarization)

Action potential = purple line

- Positive

- Negative

- Even more negative

- Back to its initial value

Always the same pattern, as the open and closure of the gates is always the same. It’s an all or nothing process. If it doesn’t follow this dynamic, the action potential won’t be

reached.

Neuronal refractory period

  • Absolute refractory period = Period between the moment the threshold is reached during depolarization, until it’s reached again during repolarization (in other words, from the opening of the fast gate to the closing of the slow ). In this period of time, it’s impossible to fire up an action potential. It corresponds to values higher

than -60 mV.

Relative refractory period = Corresponds to the period in which Na+ gates are closed and K+ gates are open ( hyperpolarization ). If we want to depolarize again from this point, we would have to fight against the open potassium channels. It is harder than normal to fire up an action potential, but it is possible.

The absolute refractory period is the main factor that limits the maximal firing rate of a neuron (the number of action potentials a neuron can fire up in a period of time). There are also other limiting factors, such as energy (it’s a very energy demanding process).

In order to reach the threshold we need to slightly depolarize the membrane (PPE), needed to trigger the opening of sodium channels. But before we begin the action potential, we need to go from the deactivated Na+ channel to the resting state of said channel (fast gate closed, slow gate open).

[PDF p. 218]

To reactivate the resting state, V has to cross lower values than those corresponding to the resting state (hyperpolarization) .

  • From deactivated to resting, when repolarizing (thanks to potassium channels), the channels do not open. They only open when we are depolarizing.

The fast gate closes, and after this, the slow gate can open once again and be in the resting state. From here, the depolarization process can take place once again.

  • For a short period of time, there is double channel

closure.

Neuronal action potential

Due to the action of other neurons, there are changes in the membrane potential. If the change is big enough, we will fire up an action potential. For it, sodium and potassium voltage gated channels are needed. But they are only located in the trigger zones. The small electrical impulses detected by dendrites, coming from other neurons, are transmitted to the soma, until they reach the axon hillock (trigger zone). This is where the action potential will fire up if the threshold is reached.

Muscle cells

Action potentials are not only present in neurons; they also appear in muscle cells.

  • Striated muscle: action potential is very similar to the one observed in neurons.
  • Smooth muscle: action potentials may have different characteristics, as there are other ions involved ( ca2+)
  • Cardiac muscle: action potentials have different characteristics. In the heart the action potential is slower . We have a 1st stage similar to that in the neurons, but the diagram adopts a different shape due to the action of calcium.

NERVE CONDUCTION

We need action potentials to transmit information. The amplitude of the voltage in an electric field decreases exponentially with distance. So if we wanted to send a message to our big toe from the spinal cord, there would be no way to do it using normal electrical transmission. This is why we use action potentials. At each neuron, a new action potential is generated every few micrometers. In this way, the signal is not lost during the journey. We can transmit the same signal over a long distance.

This is what occurs in unmyelinated fibers. This type of conduction has one problem: it’s an extremely slow process as generating an action potential requires time (3-4 ms). The final conduction velocity is half a meter per second. Very slow. This means if we touch something burning, we would burn before executing a response.

[PDF p. 219]

To improve that conduction velocity, we use myelin. It allows saltatory conduction.

Myelin and conduction

Myelin insulates the axons of neurons. It’s different

in the CNS and PNS:

  • In the CNS = oligodendrocytes, which cover

the axons. Each one covers several axons. It’s

a lipid recovery.

  • In the PNS = Schwann cells. 1 SC per axon. Axons with no myelin in the PNS are also surrounded by Schwann cells (provides protection), but unmyelinated, so there is no

isolation.

Insulation improves conduction. The length constant becomes higher if we recover the axon, and instead of having to place voltage gated channels all over the axon, we do it only in a few regions. It allows us to be able to regenerate the action potential every few millimeters and not every few microns. We get an action velocity of 80 m/s. Much faster.

Those places in which we find the channels are the Nodes of Ranvier, where there is no myelin. Points where the action potential is regenerated . There is alternating action potential generation ( nodes ) and electrical conduction (myelinated regions).

We lose time every time we regenerate the action potential, but in between nodes (myelin regions, where there is electrical conduction) the

conduction is immediate.

Action potential will only be regenerated in the nodes. Therefore, the lower the number of nodes, the higher the conduction capacity (faster impulse transmission)

[PDF p. 220]

Factors that play a role in conduction velocity:

  • Fiber diameter: the thicker the axon, lower the internal resistance = ↑ length constant (better conduction)

- Degree of myelination

    - **Increases** membrane **resistance** _(Rm affects the length constant, although_

also affects the time constant. ↑ Rm = ↑ length constant) - Reduces capacitance (C only affects the time constant. ↓ C = ↓ time constant). Membrane potentials will be faster as it reduces the capacitor effect

of the membrane.

  • Temperature : the colder it is, the slower the conduction (Goldman equation)

Time and length constants

- Time constant = mainly depends on capacitance (C. Measures the ability to store

charge. Depends on permittivity)

  • Length constant = depends on internal and membrane resistance (R. Determined by permeability, the number of channels)

Demyelination

If we lose myelin:

Decreased conduction velocity: demyelination can slow conduction velocity of the axon potential. The cell will try to compensate by putting more channels in the demyelinated portion.

  • Appearance of conduction blocks: we will recover a much lower length constant = the propagation along the unmyelinated segment is much worse. Amplitude will decrease and we may not have enough amplitude when

we reach the next node of ranvier to induce the next

action potential. Demyelination slows, but can even block conduction of the action potential.

  • Interferences: if we have bare axons, one can stimulate another and give place to aberrant conductions.

SUMMARY

All cells present a negative intracellular potential (in other words, a negative membrane potential) in their resting states (usually -80mV, mainly determined by K)

The sodium-potassium pump extracts 3 Na+ for every 2 K+ it introduces. It’s always working, essential for generating the different outer and inner Na and K concentrations, which is the base for the generation of an action potential

Na/K pump also contributes to some extent to establishing the membrane potential

[PDF p. 221]

In the resting state, we have resting channels contributing to the maintenance of membrane potential. It’s very similar to K+ Nernst potential. This suggests that this ion is the main responsible in establishing said potential (the pot at which K reaches equilibrium will correspond to the membrane potential)

The small difference between Ek and Em can be explained by the contribution of Na influx (although small, it is present).

K conductivity and K permeability are higher than that of Na.

The action potential is an all or nothing event which appears due to a sequence of opening and closing of Na (depolarization) and K (repolarization) channels.

This AP allows the conduction of an electrical impulse across the axon. Myelin recovering allows this conduction to be saltatory = faster and more efficient.

[PDF p. 222]

Synapse

SYNAPSES

Why do we have synapses?

  • Divergence: we can amplify information
  • Convergence: we can integrate info
  • Modulation (excitation or inhibition)

Spatial and temporal summation

Plasticity (synapses are constantly changing )

2 main types of synapses

Chemical synapses:

Present some delay

  • Use neurotransmitters

  • These neurotransmitters can excite or inhibit

Information flows only in one direction (unidirectional)

Not necessarily linked to ion channels

Larger synaptic cleft

NEUROMUSCULAR SYNAPSE

Electrical synapses:

No delay

  • Flow of ions

  • Direct current flow: no information

changes

  • Bidirectional

Open channels between cells (gap junctions) with partial selectivity

Minimal intercellular space

History : It was the 1st synapse to be characterized.

It’s the synapse between a motor neuron axon from the spinal cord and a muscle fiber. In this case, the receptor on the muscle

fiber is an ionic channel.

Each individual muscle fiber in a muscle is innervated by one, and only one, motor neuron. A single motor neuron, however, can innervate many muscle

fibers. The combination of an individual motor neuron and all of

the muscle fibers that it innervates is called a motor unit.

Functional anatomy of the synapse:

  • Axon

Presynaptic bouton

Synaptic cleft

End plate

  • Sarcolemma

[PDF p. 223]

  1. Presynaptic bouton

We have an action potential traveling through the neuron. In the last part of the axon,this action potential is regenerated one last time to be transmitted. Therefore, we find many sodium-potassium voltage-gated channels for this purpose.

We also find many calcium voltage gated-channels in this region. When the potential reaches the presynaptic bouton, these Ca2+ channels will open and calcium will enter into the cell (Ca2+ out >> in). This calcium will trigger the fusion of the neurotransmitter vesicles with the membrane and exocytosis.

i. Regeneration of potential (Na+/K+ channels) ii. Calcium enters (Ca2+ channels) iii. Exocytosis (fusion) iv. Calcium exit (Na/Ca2+ countertransport and Ca2+ ATPase)

One it enters, the accumulated calcium has to be expelled out of the cell, through an Na/Ca countertransport pump (single calcium ion is exported for the import of three sodium ions) and Ca2+- ATPase (energy dependent). As this last one is an energy dependent process, we will find many mitochondria in the

axon.

Neurotransmitter vesicles (Ach)

Around 300000 per bouton

  • 50 nm diameter

7000-10000 Ach molecules per vesicle

Located in two areas, proximal or distal depending on their immediate use

  1. Synaptic cleft

Neurotransmitters diffuse through the synaptic cleft and reach the postsynaptic muscle

fiber. This cleft is 10-50 nm.

In addition, this cleft is full of an enzyme that destroys Ach (Ach- esterase ), because we want the neurotransmitter to have an effect on receptors for a limited time period. Therefore, neurotransmitters are immediately destroyed in the cleft, as we are not interested in permanent muscle contraction.

  1. End plate

Part of the muscle fiber with the receptors for neurotransmitters .

  • We see hyperdense folds in the membrane in order to increase the surface in contact with the neurotransmitter, where Ach-receptors are located.
  • These receptors (nicotinic) are each ligand-gated ionic channels which open

when in contact with 2 molecules of Ach.

The ligand gated channels trigger Na+ entry and local depolarization, leading to the opening of Na+ voltage-gated channels (There are no voltage-gated sodium channels in the end plate, they are in nearby areas)

  • These voltage gated channels opened upon depolarization allow the action potential to extend along the muscle fiber. There are no sodium-potassium v-gated channels in the end plate, but at a short distance from it. If the depolarization in the membrane is strong enough, it will reach them and transmit the action potential through the muscle fiber.

[PDF p. 224]

This is a safety mechanism : sometimes, there are spontaneous releases of neurotransmitters, which we don’t want to have an action on muscle fibers. Therefore, only when there is a large, induced release will the muscle contract.

  1. Sarcolemma

The sarcolemma is a specialized membrane which surrounds the striated muscle fiber cells. At the neuromuscular junction, the sarcolemma shows a series of invaginations which greatly increase the surface area for the neurotransmitters to act.

Sequence of events

Action potential reaches the presynaptic terminal

Voltage-gated Ca2+ channels open and calcium enters the terminal

Ca2+-induced vesicle fusion → exocytosis. Neurotransmitter (Ach)

release

Ach enters the cleft and reaches the postsynaptic neuron

Activation of postsynaptic Ach receptors (nicotinic)

Local depolarization of the muscle fiber

If the depolarization is high enough → reaches Na+ voltage gated

channels

Opening of voltage-gated sodium channels

Muscle fiber action potential

End plate potential (EPP)

There are no voltage-gated sodium channels in the end plate, so NO action potential is generated YET

  • EPP propagates electronically = amplitude decreases exponentially with distance, until it reaches voltage-gated Na+

channels

  • The action potential will begin in nearby areas where those

channels are found

Muscle action potential

  • Depolarization must reach -60mV for action potential to take place
  • EPP (end-plate potential), first to be generated (and if high enough, will trigger the action potential) has 65 mV of amplitude = wide safety margin

[PDF p. 225]

Nicotinic channels

They are ligand-gated ionic channels, but not voltage gated (therefore not affected by membrane potential changes) . The ligand union is very brief, as Ach- esterase will quickly degrade the ligand; therefore, they can be repeatedly activated.

How Ach is stored in vesicles

  • The vesicles have a proton pump ( ATPase) which lowers the pH in the vesicle

There is a proton/Ach countertransport, active thanks to the gradient created by the

pump

  • Allows Ach to enter into the vesicles

Role of calcium

  • The release of neurotransmitter vesicles is due to the entrance of calcium into the

presynaptic bouton

Neurotransmitter vesicle fusion with the membrane

Mediated by SNARE proteins (soluble NSF attachment protein receptors)

  • Calcium makes contact with synaptotagmin
  • This causes synaptobrevin, another protein located in the neuronal membrane, to make contact with other proteins

They will contact and push vesicles against the membrane, causing vesicle and

membrane to fuse .

Can also mediate the fusion of vesicles with membrane-bound compartments such as lysosomes .

Mechanisms of vesicle content release

2 ways:

  • Classical fusion: vesicle is fully integrated into the membrane, and then there is regeneration of the vesicle thanks to clathrin .
  • Kiss and run: no full fusion. Neurotransmitters are simply deposited and the vesicle regresses. These neurotransmitters are released through a fusion pore.

Vesicle recycling mechanisms

2 vesicle pools:

- Reserve pool

- Readily releasable pool

[PDF p. 226]

NEURO-NEURONAL SYNAPSE

- Functional anatomy of neuro neuronal synapse:

Between neurons.

Presynaptic terminal (bouton)

Synaptic cleft

Post synaptic neuron

Types of synapses

There can be different types of synapses, depending on where it takes place (soma, dendrites or axons). This is important as the

effect of them is different.

Depending on where it takes place:

- Axo-somatic

- Axo-dendritic

- Axo-axonal

From a histological point of view:

Type I : usually located in the dendritic tree, in the spines

of dendrites.

Usually excitatory . No voltage gated channels in the

dendritic tree.

  • Type II: take place in the soma and the dendritic

branches themselves.

Usually inhibitory . No voltage gated channels in the soma, except in the axon hillock

The closer we are to the beginning of the axon, the stronger the transmission of membrane potential. Synapse in the dendritic tree = very narrow branches, much resistance = low influence Synapse in the soma = very close to the axon, travels very short distance = big influence

  1. Presynaptic terminal

    Similar to the neuromuscular synapse

    However, the type of neurotransmitter in the vesicles may change

  2. Synaptic cleft

    • Usually smaller than in the neuromuscular junction (20-30 nm)

    Different degradation enzyme content (NOT ach-esterase).

Two main mechanisms:

- **Destroy the neurotransmitter** (enzyme will depend on the specific

neurotransmitter)

- **Clearing mechanism,** to **retrieve** the neurotransmitter, which is **recycled**

3. Postsynaptic neuron

4 aspects to discuss: Dendrites, soma, axonal cone (trigger zone) and postsynaptic receptors

[PDF p. 227]

Dendrites

Some neurons have a very large dendritic tree = in many cases, present great

arborization

  • Dendrites have a high density of receptors → Most synapses take place in the dendrites (80-90% are axo-dendritic)
  • Dendrites have a very narrow diameter = high internal resistance (Ri), and thus a low length constant → signal loses amplitude along the length of the dendrite, until potential is regenerated at vg Na+ channels

Therefore, we will find many synapses taking place in all the dendritic tree. They will add their effect (spatial summation) and reach the axon to transmit the depolarization. In this way, signal is not lost and can be transmitted

Soma

Neurons are generally big cells

  • Big diameter = very low resistance to the propagation of excitatory or inhibitory postsynaptic potentials (EPSP or IPSP)

Membrane potential shows values from -65mV to 75mV

It possesses few receptors (10% of synapses occur at the soma) and scarce voltage-gated ion channels = no action potential generation

Trigger zone

Corresponds to the place where the axon begins

High density of Na+ and K+ voltage gated channels (many more than the soma)

This is why it is the starting point of the action potential : when depolarization threshold is reached, it triggers voltage-gated sodium channel opening and the generation of an action potential

  • Area of maximum susceptibility to trigger an action potential

  • Considered as the “first node of Ranvier”

Postsynaptic receptors 2 main types of neurotransmitter receptors:

● Ionotropic receptors: direct effect. Ionotropic receptors are membrane-bound receptor proteins that respond to ligand binding by opening an ion channel and allowing ions into the cell.

  • Metabotropic receptors : indirect effect. The neurotransmitter binds to a G-coupled receptor . Instead of making direct effects, they cause effects mediated by secondary messengers.

Diverse functions:

    Its effect can be to open ionic channels.

The difference with ionotropic receptors is that the secondary messenger can travel to other parts of the neuron and open channels in other parts. The

effects are not limited to where the

channel is found.

    They can also have other effects such

as protein synthesis, transcription,…

[PDF p. 228]

Post-synaptic potentials

They can be excitatory or inhibitory:

Excitatory. EPSPs:

They have an electrotonic propagation. Amplitude of the EPSP decreases with distance, depending on the impedance (lower in the soma)

EPSPs propagate homogeneously in all directions

  • Excitatory postsynaptic potential begins in the trigger zone if the threshold is

reached

If we reach the -60mV threshold in the trigger zone, there will be transmision of an action potential. If the trigger zone is not depolarized, there will be no transmission. Excitation generally triggered by Na + (can also be Ca2+ in some cases)

Use both ionotropic and metabotropic receptors

Retrograde propagation of the action potential: The action potential propagates retrogradely to the neuronal soma, leaving the neuron in refractory period. There is an electrotonic propagation back into the soma (low resistance) and can even reach thick regions of the dendrite. The retrograde propagation to the dendrites can play a role in neuronal plasticity, via calcium channels

Inhibitory. IPSPs: What ions can cause inhibition? those with equilibrium potential which is more negative than the resting potential: K+ and Cl-, the most typical generating inhibitory potentials being Cl

  • Cl- channels: increase Cl- conductance. ECl = -90mV

  • K+ channels: increase K+ conductance. EK = -95mV

➢ Generation of IPSPs

To generate IPSPs: there are 2 types of inhibitory receptors, as for excitatory receptors:

● Ionotropic inhibitory receptors:

    Glycine (tetanus), via Cl        - **GABA,** vía Cl

● Metabotropic Inhibitory receptors:

    - 5HY2C = via IP3/DAG

    - 5HTA1 = via decrease in cAMP

    **Muscarinic receptor M2** = via ↓ in cAMP and opening of K+ channels

➢ Localization of IPSPs

Inhibitory postsynaptic potentials will take place near the trigger zone : Most IPSPs originate in the soma (as opposed to EPSPs). Therefore, they propagate better, faster, higher effect. It’s a compensation for 2 facts:

Excitatory synapses are much more abundant

EPSPs have higher amplitude. With inhibitory, we can only reach around 20mV of change in potential. They are less powerful This is why we need to compensate by the location (soma).

➢ Effects of IPSPs :

They are a fundamental mechanism of synaptic control. 2 types of actions on the excitability of the cell: reduce or block the probability of firing an action potential.

[PDF p. 229]

loss of inhibition = epileptic sequences. It is necessary to have inhibition.

Presynaptic inhibition

  • Axo-axonal type of synapse: they are mostly inhibitory

Acts on the presynaptic bouton

Inhibits the synapse with no additional changes in the postsynaptic neuron : only affects that individual synapse

  • Global result = reduction in the amount of neurotransmitters released by the

main axon.

Through several mechanisms:

i. Small sustained depolarization: inhibition caused by very slow and sustained depolarization (Enkephalin, widely used as a painkiller) .

         - The blocking of Na channels **decreases axonal Ea**

         Less voltage-gated Ca channels are opened

- NT release is reduced

ii. Hyperpolarization: Causes lower aperture of Ca2+ voltage-gated channels. Depolarization is smaller, less Ca2+ calcium voltage gated channels open = less neurotransmitter release. Action of GABA on neurons, via GABA B

receptors. iii. Block Ca2+ voltage-gated channels: lower amount of Ca entering = less neurotransmitter release. This is what opioids do. iv. Non-Ca mediated NT exocytosis block:

Example of presynaptic inhibition: spinal mechanism of pain control

Presynaptic facilitation

Contrary to inhibition. The axo-axonal synapse increases the number of NT released with the same action potential.

Facilitatory axo-axonal synapses

  • Don’t depolarize the postsynaptic cell; rather, they potentiate NT release = more

stimulation

  • Example: the action of Serotonin on the memory circuits in Aplysia

Synaptic integration

In synapses, we integrate by using:

[PDF p. 230]

  • Spatial summation: depends on the length constant, which depends on resistance (diameter of the dendrite) and distance (distance from each individual dendrite to the point where they meet). Spatial summation: begins at two different points, A and B. If they propagate well, the effect of A and B will be enough when it reaches point C, and the potentials will join their effect. If not well propagated, it won’t be enough when reaching the final point.

    • Temporal summation: depends on the time constant, which at the same time depends on the capacitance of the membrane. 2 in the same point. If the duration is very long (long time constant) they join = big action potential

So.. why do we have synapses?

Divergence: amplification Convergence: integration Modulation: we can modulate how other neurons discharge

Excitation and inhibition

Spatial and temporal summation Plasticity: we can add or decrease the number of synapses

[PDF p. 231]

Introduction to neuromuscular junction disorders

The disorders of the neuromuscular junction are classified according to the portion of the synapse that is affected: presynaptic and postsynaptic disorders. The differentiation is important because the clinical course, the diagnostic process and the treatment vary.

1. Presynaptic disorders

In this group of disorders, the affected portion is the terminal bouton of the motor neuron. The common effect in all of them is a reduced acetylcholine release despite a normal arrival of action potentials. Two disorders will be discussed in more detail: botulism and Lambert-Eaton syndrome

A) BOTULISM

Botulinum toxin is one of the most powerful poisons found in nature. The toxin is generated by a bacterium named Clostridium botulinum . The characteristics of the bacteria and the clinical course of the disease will be seen in detail in other courses. In short, this bacterium does not usually directly infect our organism. When it grows in an adequate manner (like improperly processed preserves) it can produce the toxin, and it is the intake of the toxin (with the food) that generates the clinical consequences.

Botulinum toxin has a heavy and a light chain (with several subtypes of the latter; only subtypes A, B and E are related to human disease). The heavy chain allows the toxin to enter cholinergic neurons (giving the neuronal specificity), while the light chain is an endopeptidase that cleaves proteins involved in neurotransmitter vesicle release.

How does botulinum toxin block the release of acetylcholine in the presynaptic terminal? As seen in the previous session (and shown in the image), there are several proteins involved in the fusion of neurotransmitter vesicles with the presynaptic membrane. Some of these proteins are located in the membrane of the vesicle (v-SNAREs) while others are located in the membrane of the neuron (t-SNAREs). Synaptotagmin is the calcium sensor that begins the fusion process. The different subtypes of botulinum toxin cleave v-SNAREs or t-SNAREs due to their endopeptidase action, making the fusion process impossible.

Although the fusion mechanism of neurotransmitter vesicles is common to all neurons (and other vesicle fusion processes), botulinum toxin only acts in some types of cholinergic neurons due to the selectivity of entry mechanism provided by the heavy chain, as mentioned above. In fact, the light chain of the tetanus toxin (produced by another bacteria of the same genre, Clostridium tetani ) has the same mechanism of action as the light chain of the botulinum toxin, but the clinical effect is apparently opposite (in the tetanus, there is

[PDF p. 232]

involuntary contraction of several muscles groups). How can this be possible? Because instead of inhibiting the neurotransmitter release in motor neurons, the tetanus toxin inhibits the release of neurotransmitter in inhibitory motor neurons (its heavy chain takes the toxin into these interneurons). The final effect of a loss of inhibition in motor circuits is an excess

of contraction.

Botulinum toxin can affect other subtypes of cholinergic neurons apart from motor neurons. Thus, autonomic symptoms and signs (which will be studied later) may be part of the clinical

course of the disease.

B) LAMBERT-EATON SYNDROME

This is an autoimmune syndrome usually observed in the context of certain neoplasms. The appearance of antibodies against the voltage-gated calcium channels of the presynaptic terminal leads to a decreased release of acetylcholine, despite the presence of normal action potentials in the motor axon. As in botulism, other subtypes of cholinergic neurons can be affected, causing autonomic dysfunction.

2. Postsynaptic disorders: MYASTHENIA GRAVIS

In this group of disorders, the postsynaptic portion of the synapse (and particularly, the nicotinic receptors) is affected.

The prototypical postsynaptic disorder is myasthenia gravis, a disease you have already

heard of.

In summary, it is an autoimmune disease in which there are antibodies against the nicotinic receptors of the muscle that lead to their elimination. As the antibodies are specific against this subtype of receptor, there is no autonomic involvement (unless there are other antibodies produced in the context of the autoimmune dysfunction).

3. Relevance of knowing both types of disorders

There are diagnostic tests that can differentiate both types of disorders depending on the response to brief or prolonged exercise, or to repetitive stimulation. It is beyond the scope of this course to enter in more detail about this. But, as a simple rule, a presynaptic defect can be compensated (in the short term) with an increase in the firing rate of action potentials in the motor neuron (leading to an increase in acetylcholine release). However, in postsynaptic defects the problem is not in the ACh release, but in the limited number of receptors available. An increased firing rate of action potentials may lead to neurotransmitter depletion without achieving any significant effect. Instead, in myasthenia gravis the patient can benefit from acetylcholinesterase inhibitors, used as symptomatic treatment. These inhibitors prolong the union of ACh to the receptors, maximizing the effect of the remaining nicotinic

channels.

[PDF p. 233]

4. Drugs that block the neuromuscular junction

In patients under general anesthesia, muscle relaxation may be convenient after intubation to avoid fighting against the respirator, and to facilitate the work of the surgeon. There are two main types of neuromuscular blockers: analogues of curare (a natural poison) that block the union of ACh to the nicotinic receptors, and depolarizing blockers, that keep permanently open the nicotinic channels, generating a sustained depolarization of the muscle fiber membrane. This mechanism is similar to the primary afferent depolarization mechanism studied in presynaptic inhibition. If the membrane is kept depolarized at a level beyond the activation threshold of the sodium voltage-gated channels, these channels remain blocked and there cannot initiate any new action potential.

[PDF p. 234]

SKELETAL MUSCLE

Muscle contraction

The nerve branches and reaches several muscle fibers, forming the terminal motor plaque.

Each muscle fiber receives innervation from 1 motor neuron.

The motor neuron can innervate many motor fibers. All those muscle fibers get depolarized and generate action potentials at the same time: it’s like a functional unit, which is why it’s all grouped together into the so-called motor plaque.

Sequence of events in the postsynaptic terminal

The release of Ach neurotransmitters occurs. These reach their respective receptors in the motor end plate, causing the opening of ligand-gated Na channels, which leads to Na influx and K efflux = depolarization. These nicotinic receptors have 2 binding sites for the Ach ligand. The depolarization extends along the cell and reaches voltage-gated Na channels, which also open. All contributes to generating muscle action potential.

Sarcoplasmic reticulum and T tubules

This series of events will lead to a Ca2+ increase in the cytoplasm of the muscle fiber. But the mechanism is somehow different than to what happens in neurons:

The action potential is generated in the center of the fiber and it propagates in both directions (fastest way to propagate).

We want it to generate an increase in the muscle’s intracellular Ca2+ levels. For this purpose: the sarcolemma is invaginated every short distance, to

increase the surface area .

These invaginations, called T tubules, allow the membrane to be in very close contact to the sarcoplasmic reticulum (SER).

  • In those invaginations the reticulum is adjacent to the membrane. This

area constitutes what is known as a triad.

This interaction between membranes will

cause the increase of intracellular

calcium. However it doesn’t come from

outside (as in the case of neurons), but from inside . In neurons for ex, it comes from the

extracellular space and enters the cell. But

not in this case.

This is the case for skeletal muscle. In cardiac muscle, Ca2+ additionally comes from

outside as well as inside.

[PDF p. 235]

DHP and RYR proteins - Ca2+ release

2 very important proteins allow this depolarization transmission and

Ca2+ release:

  • DHPR, located on the T tubule (sarcolemma)
  • And RYR, located on the SER membrane. DHPR changes its conformation upon stimulation and acts on RYR, which opens by changing its conformation and allows Ca2+ release

inside the cell. 4 molecules of DHPR interact with 1 molecule of

RYR to trigger calcium release.

DHPR will also act as a calcium channel in cardiac cells to allow

calcium entry, but in this case, it’s only role is to interact with RYR.

How do we get Ca2+ inside the SER?

Various proteins:

  • SERCA: it acts as a calcium pump, constantly pumping calcium into the reticulum. After each action potential, there is a short lasting calcium release, as this protein will quickly pump Ca2+ back into the SER. Therefore, a single action potential generates a short transient contraction, stopped by the retrieving of Ca.

Other proteins

Skeletal muscle contraction

Structure of striated muscle: we have thin and thick filaments.

  • Thin filaments = mostly actin monomers,
  • two other proteins: troponin and tropomyosin.
    • Thick filaments = mostly made up of myosin . Very large protein, with 2 heavy and 2 light chains, which together form a characteristic shape: heads (light chains) and tails (heavy chains)

Contraction will occur due to the movement of

the thin filaments over the thick filaments.

Looking at an image of a muscle fiber, we can observe different bands corresponding to each region of the sarcomere: I band, A band, M line, Z band,…

Many myosin heads will be found pointing in different directions, at different locations. In the thin filaments, we will find troponin and tropomyosin. Myosin head will bind the actin, turn and cause a displacement of the thin filament. It’ll then unbind, recover the original position and prepare to bind to a different site, more advanced. In this way, it moves along the thin filament.

[PDF p. 236]

Process of contraction in skeletal muscle

Calcium is the trigger for the beginning of contraction

  • In the muscle at rest, the binding site for myosin is covered by tropomyosin.

Muscle is relaxed .

  • In the muscle during contraction

    **Ca binds troponin (troponin C),** making it change conformation. It **pulls**

tropomyosin from the binding site, leaving it exposed. Immediately, myosin (very high affinity for actin in skeletal muscle fibers ) binds it. Calcium allows myosin to bind to actin.

    From here there is an automated process: myosin binds, and the **binding**

triggers a change in conformation in myosin (turn).

    Up till now, there has been no energy consumption. But **energy is needed to**

release the myosin head from the actin and recover the original position. At rest, myosin has an ADP bound to it. The turn causes ADP to be released, and this site is free for ATP binding. When ATP is bound to myosin, it is converted to ADP and P is released, causing the detachment of myosin

from actin.

Rigor mortis

When someone is dead, there is muscle

contraction and no relaxation, as there is

calcium in the cell due to cellular damage but no available energy as the person is dead. It causes rigidity in the corpse. This phenomenon is known as rigor mortis: the person stays rigid, as the energy needed for myosin release is not present.

SMOOTH MUSCLE

  • The common component between smooth and skeletal muscle

excitation-contraction is Calcium.

  • DHPR - RYR coupling only happens in skeletal muscle. It also occurs with slight differences in cardiac muscle. But it doesn’t occur in this way in smooth muscle.
  • There is also no troponin present in smooth muscle

Sometimes there is even no action potential needed

Smooth muscle is designed for different roles than those of skeletal muscle. It’s designed for longer contraction processes. The contraction is therefore slower, so it can be contracted for longer periods.

[PDF p. 237]

Types of smooth muscle

There are 2 main types of smooth muscle:

  • Multiunit smooth muscle: made up of individual fibers (each cell is a unit). Each fiber will contract and relax independently.

Single unit or visceral smooth muscle: they are all bound by gap junctions, as if they were a single very long fiber. Calcium increase will be transmitted through the different fibers through gap junctions. They can contract coordinately.

Varicosities

In smooth muscle we don’t usually talk about synapses, but Varicosities . The neurotransmitters are released and diffuse to many nearby areas. Not restricted to a synaptic cleft. There can be many varicosity regions per axon.

In the case of visceral smooth muscle, we don’t need many varicosities per fiber, as the cells are interconnected by gap junctions that will transmit the calcium release triggered by 1 varicosity. These muscle fibers can also be activated by circulating hormones released from the blood, not only by neurotransmitters.

Calcium release in smooth muscle can be triggered by:

  • Neurotransmitters released at varicosity regions
  • Hormones coming in from the bloodstream

Disposition and contraction/relaxation patterns

Disposition of smooth muscle is very variable Example: found in the digestive tract, where there are longitudinal and transverse movements (contractions) that cause peristalsis.

Contraction/relaxation patterns are also very variable ( smooth muscles in some regions are normally contracted and have slight periods of relaxation; others are always partially contracted, like blood vessels which have to maintain a tone,..)

We therefore need different mechanisms than the ones for

skeletal muscle.

Excitation-contraction coupling in smooth muscle

Common and differential features

The only common component shared with skeletal muscle coupling is calcium, which triggers contraction.

  • We also find thin and thick filaments, but the distribution is much different. Much more dispersed and less organized. They DON’T form sarcomeres.

- No tropomyosin and no troponin

[PDF p. 238]

The coupling mechanism is different in smooth muscle .

  • Calcium activates a kinase of MLCK (myosin light chain kinase), which phosphorylates the light chain of myosin.
  • This increases the affinity of myosin for actin (here myosin is a different subtype. In smooth muscle, if not phosphorylated, myosin has very little affinity for actin). When calcium increases, affinity increases as myosin is phosphorylated and there can be contraction .
  • The contraction process and dynamics, its movement over actin, is the same as in skeletal muscle. But the

activation is different.

In smooth muscle, the actin binding site is always exposed (no troponin or tropomyosin) as this type of myosin has very low affinity.

  • At the same time there is a phosphatase dephosphorylating myosin, removing its affinity. It’s a process in equilibrium of phosphorylation and dephosphorylation.

But how does calcium increase in smooth muscle?

  • We may have calcium coming from the outside:

➢It can enter by opening of Ca voltage-gated channels

         Caused by opening due to depolarization

➢It can also occur through ligand-gated calcium channels

         through direct activation (neurotransmitter)

         via second messengers (hormones)

The 2nd mechanism can trigger the 1st. Calcium entering the muscle fiber through ligand gated channels triggers a depolarization (as there are charged particles passing across the membrane) and allows the opening of calcium voltage gated channels.

  • Coming from inside, from the reticulum,

    - Activation via second messengers like **IP3/DAG.**

Muscle contraction modulation in smooth muscle

There are other pathways for muscle contraction modulation that don’t involve calcium.

● Calcium sensitivity regulation mediated by Rho We have a phosphorylase and phosphatase acting on myosin. These can be controlled by other pathways, most common: the one mediated by Rho (bound to GTP). Calcium activates the kinase, but many different mechanisms can control and modulate the phosphatase, moving myosin to a low affinity version and contrarrest the effects of calcium.

[PDF p. 239]

● Smooth muscle relaxation mediated by NO Viagra = relaxation of smooth muscles in the arteries. Contains NO.

How does it modulate? Combination of mechanisms that act on different stages.

  • Mechanism 1: begins at a G protein receptor and can inhibit the activation of IP3 as a 2nd messenger

  • Mechanism 2: inhibit/lowers the release of calcium from

the ER

  • Mechanism 3: acts on the phosphatase of the myosin light chain
  • Mechanism 4: opening potassium channels = reduces/contrarrests the action potential generated by

Ca channels

  • Mechanism 5: direct block of Ca2+ voltage gated

channels

All these mechanisms trigger smooth muscle relaxation

Calcium sensitization and desensitization

Calcium generates smooth muscle contraction, although

there are different mechanisms that can modulate this

contraction generated by Ca. This gives out this curve: more or less degree of contraction depending on the

concentration of calcium.

  • If we activate the phosphatase through modulating mechanisms, we will need more calcium to trigger contraction (right).

If the phosphatase is not active, we will need less calcium for contraction (left). It can be displaced to the right or left depending on these additional modulatory mechanisms. This is known as sensitization and desensitization of muscle and it only happens in smooth muscle. In skeletal muscle, contraction will be higher or lower only depending on the received action potential.

Patterns of contraction

There are many different patterns of contraction, due to the many different mechanisms

available for smooth muscle contraction.

1st graph: we may have some similar mechanism to that in skeletal muscle. Many action potentials combine and generate contraction 2nd graph: mixed: fluctuations in membrane potentials and action potentials = graph that is similar to that of membrane potential and increases when there is an action potential triggered. 3rd graph: membrane pot doesn’t change but hormones trigger the release of intracellular calcium

Main second messengers: IP3 (always generates contraction when released)

[PDF p. 240]

Long lasting contractions

Smooth muscle has different roles than those of skeletal muscle. It’s designed to be much more flexible and be contracted for a very long time (ex: sphincters, 23h per day; soleus = many h per day, whenever we are standing). It is optimized to have the minimum energy consumption during that time.

In smooth muscle, to generate a long lasting contraction, the key is to increase calcium concentration which then stays at an intermediate concentration for a long time. This is only possible in smooth muscle, as skeletal muscle only uses action potentials, but in smooth muscle we can control

intracellular calcium levels.

The key is keeping these intermediate levels of calcium so we only increase the affinity of some portions of myosin. An initial high peak intracellular calcium concentration is necessary to initiate the contraction, but it can then be maintained at intermediate levels, meaning it does things slower but can keep a low level of contraction without excessive energy consumption.

Smooth muscle structure. Adaptability

Myofibrils are very rigid and strictly parallel in skeletal muscle. But in smooth muscle they’re not strictly parallele. This allows the muscle fiber to change shape and adapt. In the case of the bladder: allows it to distend, but retain the ability to contract when needed. It can adapt to different tensions depending on the volume. Skeletal muscle is much more rigid and is not so adaptable. Smooth muscle keeps the ability to contract even when it is stretched . It’s more adaptable.

Socrative: in hypocalcemia, somehow, although striated muscle calcium comes from the inside, if there are low extracellular calcium levels they can affect K+ channels. Hypocalcemia triggers K+ channels to close, making the membrane potential more positive. It triggers spontaneous depolarization as K is no longer exiting to the extracellular space. This manifests as a higher tendency towards muscle contraction, which manifests a tetani.

Smooth muscle growth

In smooth muscle we only have one nuclei per cell. To grow, the cell may increase the genetic material (ploidy) inside the cell, but without increasing the number of nuclei (in skeletal muscle, we do find growing multinucleated cells). They can become tetraploid or even octoploid (hypertrophy).

[PDF p. 241]

SMOOTH MUSCLE SUMMARY

- Smooth muscle cells can be independent (multi unitary) or associated (unitary)

- Muscle contraction can be triggered by action potentials, but also by changes in membrane potential, or neurotransmitter/hormone action with no change in Em

- Actin/myosin coupling is triggered by myosin phosphorylation (there is no troponin nor tropomyosin). Myosin phosphorylation is regulated through different pathways (and calcium)

- Smooth muscle can adapt to distension or contraction + hypertrophy

MUSCLE FIBER TYPES. SKELETAL MUSCLE

In muscle, there are 2 ways to increase force:

  • Increase in the rate of action potentials:

    Incomplete tetanus: it’s a way to increase force in striated muscle: increasing

the number of action potentials per second, even when calcium levels go

back to baseline.

    When we launch action potentials at a rate

higher than 30%, we generate what is known as tetanus force: we don’t allow calcium to go

back to baseline level and the muscle remains

contracted for a very long time

● Recruit more motor units

Within striated muscle we have different types of muscle fibers. Type I fibers are slow, and type II fibers are fast. We find II A and II B, which differ in their resistance to fatigue.

Type I fibers: designed to be contracted for a long time. However, it is still a much faster

contraction than in smooth muscle.

Low contraction velocity

High oxidative enzymes

High resistance to fatigue They have a type of myosin that is slower in contraction, which allows them to carry out slower

contraction and be contracted for

longer. Can be contracted for hours

fast resistant fibers. Type II A fibers: They resist fatigue, as they have more oxidative enzymes than Type IIB and can carry out more anaerobic

metabolism under low 02 conditions

present during fatigue.

High contraction velocity

High oxidative enzymes

High resistance to fatigue

Can be contracted for a few minutes

Fast ATPase. Aerobic and anaerobic

Type II B fibers: fast fatigue fibers. They barely resist under

anaerobic conditions.

High contraction velocity

Low oxidative enzymes

Low resistance to fatigue Designed to be contracted for

few seconds

Fast ATPase. Only anaerobic glycolysis.

[PDF p. 242]

We can change muscle fibers from type B to type A during resistance training, as we only have to change a few enzymes. But we cannot change between type I and type II, as they have many different components and types of myosin which can’t be changed from one

to another.

muscles with type II B fibers = mostly brown-ish

Muscles with many type II A fibers and type I fibers = reddish as they have a high blood supply for fast activity

What muscle will have faster contraction?

This is due to metabolic differences in many components:

Mitochondrial enzymes . Oxidative enzyme contact will determine resistance to fatigue and length of contraction.

  • There are differences in troponin and tropomyosin. Faster troponin= faster

contraction.

  • Differences in SERCA transmembrane protein. If SERCA is very active, contraction is very short as calcium is quickly removed.

Most of these differences are induced by their

innervation. It’s the motor neurons which determine if

the muscle will become type I or type II. Motor units that make maximal force need type II B fibers, as they will only need max force, very punctually. All the motor

fibers within the same motor unit are of the same

type (I, II A II B)

Exercise induced changes

➢During resistance training we can change type II B to II A fibers, by increasing the

amount of mitochondria and enzymes (oxidative enzymes = more resistance to fatigue), etc…. But we cannot change myosin, SERCA or troponin, so we cannot change from type I to type II or vice versa.

➢When we do force training, we generate more myofibrils in

parallel . The diameter of the muscle fiber increases, which allows us to have more force but not more velocity. This velocity only depends on the capacity to trigger contraction of many sarcomeres at the same time.

    - Sarcomeres in parallel = strength
    - Sarcomeres in length = velocity

Therefore if they grow in parallel, they will increase in strength but not in velocity. Only sarcomeres in series are related to speed.

Skeletal muscle metabolism

[PDF p. 243]

i. In the muscle, when we begin to contract, we use the available ATP in the cytoplasm. ii. To obtain more, the phosphocreatine system will convert ADPs to ATP s for about

8-10 seconds.

iii. If we still need energy, glucose metabolism begins.

    If we need fast energy, we carry out many successive **glycolysis**

(anaerobic) . It generates lactic acid, which will then be reused. But this only gives 2ATPs per glucose. It’s fast but all the glycogen would finally be

consumed.

    - **To keep the muscle contraction,** we need to carry out **aerobic metabolism**

(yields 32 APs per glucose). However, it is slower. To carry out aerobic metabolism we have to lower energy consumption, which explains the presence of type I fibers: they maintain the muscle contracted but with lower energy consumption as the contraction is slower.

  • Low distances, fast sprints = anaerobic metabolism
  • Resistance, long distance = aerobic metabolism

Why do we hyperventilate?

If we do anaerobic metabolism for prolonged time, we will need to compensate at some point. We need to recover. Lactic acid needs to be turned into pyruvate and through the Krebs system, to be turned back to glucose or consumed. This can only be done through aerobic metabolism. This is what is known as oxygen debt: the O2 needed to carry out aerobic metabolism which compensates for that anaerobic metabolism which we’ve done to quickly perform a task. This is why we hyperventilate.

Differences between aerobic and anaerobic exercise:

What is soreness (“agujetas”)?

The cause is not fully established. Some theories say the accumulation of lactic acid was the cause, caused by the generation of lactic acid crystals. But this is false. This soreness is probably caused by

microtears in the muscle and inflammation.

[PDF p. 244]

Accessory proteins and disorders

Myofibrils contract due to the displacement of thin filaments (actin) over thick filaments (myosin), shortening the sarcomeres. But myofibrils must be attached to the

sarcolemma, and the sarcolemma must be linked to the connective tissue around it, in

order to transmit the force generated by the contraction. Moreover, if the myofibrils are not properly attached to the sarcolemma, and the sarcolemma is not anchored to the connective tissue around, the muscle fibers will suffer damage.

Let’s go step by step. Muscles usually exert their force through tendons that insert into a bone (although there are exceptions, like the tongue). Muscle fibers (muscle cells) do not extend up to this insertion. Tendons are formed by connective tissue (they are acellular); in fact they are formed by the fusion of the different connective layers that surround the fibers (endomysium), the muscle fascicles (perimysium) and the whole muscle (epimysium) (see the image below).

Thus, muscle fibers must be firmly attached (through the sarcolemma) to the

connective tissue that surrounds them in order to transmit their contractile force to the

tendon (or any other insertion). But inside the fiber, myofibrils must be attached to other myofibrils, to the cytoskeleton and to the sarcolemma in order to generate and transmit force adequately. Force transmission should therefore be both longitudinal (along the main axis of the fiber, to the tendon) and lateral (circumferential, through the minor axis, linking myofibrils, cytoskeleton, sarcolemma and connective tissue).

Thin filaments are attached to the Z bands by means of alpha-actinin. Thick filaments are also attached to the Z bands, by means of an “elastic” protein, titin. Titin needs to

be elastic because the distance between the end of the thick filaments and the Z band

[PDF p. 245]

depends on the overlap between thin and thick filaments (if the sarcomere is relaxed, the distance is longer than if the sarcomere is contracted). Myomesin links thick filaments in the M line (see figure).

Myofibrils are attached to the cytoskeleton (that includes a different subtype of actin fibers) by desmin (from Z lines) and dystrophin. Desmin links the different myofibrils, and through the cytoskeleton, attaches them to the nuclei and mitochondria. It also attaches the myofibrils to the membrane (integrins). Dystrophin is the main responsible of attaching the cytoskeleton of the muscle fiber to the sarcolemma and the

extracellular connective tissue.

The different protein complexes that attach the membrane both to the extracellular

tissue and to the intracellular structures are named costameres. In the skeletal muscle,

the most relevant costameres are the integrin complex (that interacts with desmin) and the complex that includes dystroglycan/sarcoglycan/dystrobrevin/sarcospan and other proteins, where dystrophin binds. The costameres bind to the extracellular collagen by means of laminin alpha 2.

[PDF p. 246]

It is clinically relevant to know these attachment systems because many muscular dystrophies are due to defects in some of these proteins. Other forms of muscular dystrophy are due to damage in other structural proteins or in intracellular signaling pathways related to them. A defective attachment of the myofibrils to the sarcolemma and the surrounding connective tissue (that will form the tendon), besides impeding an adequate force transmission, also damages the muscle fibers due to an excess of movement. Alterations in desmin usually associated with cardiac muscle disorders; cardiac muscle (as you will learn later) shares many structural similarities. Absence of dystrophin causes Duchenne muscular dystrophy (DMD), a severe disorder, while its dysfunction causes a milder form of muscular dystrophy named Becker muscular dystrophy. Alterations in the genes that encode the different sarcoglycans cause a different type of muscular dystrophy, globally known as “limb-girdle” muscular dystrophies (LGMD), as the muscles affected initially are located around the pelvic and scapular areas. Defects in alpha 2 laminin, extracellular collagen VI, titin, or the complex dysferlin/caveolin 3 (related to sarcolemmal repair; the sarcolemma may suffer damage during normal contraction) also cause muscular dystrophies.

[PDF p. 247]

AUTONOMIC NERVOUS SYSTEM

[PDF p. 248]

Autonomic nervous system II

INTRODUCTION

Division of the nervous system that controls bodily functions in a largely unconscious manner, supplying innervation to smooth muscle, glands and internal organs.

General functions

                             Heart and respiratory rates

                             Digestion

                             Pupillary response

                             - Urination

                             - Sexual arousal

                             Body temperature

                             - Nutrition

Classified into the afferent branch (sensory) and efferent branch (motor, further subdivided into Sympathetic and Parasympathetic SNA)

EFFERENT BRANCH

Efferent branch organization

Preganglionic fiber: stems from the CNS and ends in an autonomic ganglion

Postganglionic fiber: stems from the autonomic ganglion and ends in its target

effector

A. SYMPATHETIC SYSTEM

“Fight-flight” response

Characteristics

  • CNS: sympathetic motor neurons arise at the intermediate cell columns, which are found from T1-L2 along the spinal cord.

Autonomic ganglion: from where the postganglionic neuron will originate. These neurons extend their processes either to:

- **Sympathetic trunk ganglia** (cervical ganglia and ganglia which give rise to

the cardiac nerves)

- **Preaortic single ganglia** (thoracic and lumbar splanchnic nerves)

As the preganglionic fibers extend either to sympathetic trunk or preaortic single ganglia (further, but still pretty close to the spinal cord), we find that:

- Preganglionic fibers are quite short

- Postganglionic neurons have very long fibers, extending all the way to effectors

[PDF p. 249]

Levels of innervation

The sympathetic trunk extends from T1-L2 / L3

Cervical ganglia: at this level, there is no intermediolateral horn in the spinal cord to give rise to sympathetic motor neurons. Therefore, preganglionic neurons from lower levels where there is this horn, extend their processes up to cervical ganglia, from where the postganglionic neuron will arise. Provided by T1-T2 short preganglionic fibers, which extend their processes up to:
