Superior cervical ganglion (C1-C4 levels)

Middle cervical ganglion (C5-C6 levels)

Inferior cervical ganglion (C7-C8 levels)

Cardiac plexus : arise from T3-T6. Long postganglionic fibers.

- Thoracic and lumbar plexus Prevertebral ganglia These nerves, which innervate abdominal viscera, arise at preaortic single ganglia:

Celiac ganglion & Superior mesenteric ganglion (T7-T11 preganglionic fibers)

Inferior mesenteric ganglion (T12-L3 preganglionic fibers)

Dermal smooth muscle innervation

In parallel, along the whole of the spinal cord, we will have sympathetic motor neurons which will innervate blood vessels and dermal smooth muscle (ex: sweat glands) all over the body. These portions aren’t just restricted to areas in the abdomen, but are found all over. Therefore, the preganglionic neurons arising from the intermediolateral horn will extend up or down along the sympathetic trunk. When they reach the level they are ought to innervate, they connect with their corresponding postganglionic neuron in the sympathetic trunk ganglia. This postganglionic neuron will then accompany somatic neurons which go to innervate skeletal muscle found at the same portion as the dermis (remember: each portion of muscle arises from the same somite as its corresponding portion of skin. In the somites we find myotome and dermatome) . Depending on where it is located, it will accompany the ones taking the dorsal ramus (to the back) or ventral ramus (to the hypaxial skeleton: muscles and dermis in the abdomen and limbs).

Adrenal medulla - Chroma�n cells

Adrenal Medulla: provided by T5-T9 preganglionic

fibers

The adrenal medulla cells are modified

postganglionic sympathetic neurons, known as

chromaffin cells.

These cells release adrenaline (also known as epinephrine) into the blood mainstream.

Adrenaline synthesis requires large concentrations of cortisol, which is provided by venous supply from the adrenal cortex

[PDF p. 250]

B. PARASYMPATHETIC SYSTEM

Characteristics

  • CNS: Brainstem and lumbosacral spinal cord (S2-S4)

Autonomic ganglion: Next to the target effector. Postganglionic PS neurons originate very close to their target organ

As a consequence, we will find that in the Parasympathetic system:

Preganglionic fibers are very long, extend all the way to the target organ - Postganglionic fibers are short

Levels of innervation

Brain Stem (Face ⇒ splenic colic flexure)

Long Preganglionic fibers

- Edinger-Westphal Nuclei, part of the oculomotor nucleus

(III)

- Superior and inferior Salivatory nuclei (VII)

- Dorsal vagal nuclei (X) → vagus nerve

- Ambiguus nucleus (IX and X)

Short Postganglionic fibers

- Pupil (III)

- Lacrimal glands (VII)

- Salivary glands (VII)

○ Thoraco-abdominal organs down to the splenic colic flexure (X)

Sacral spinal cord, S2-S4 (Splenic colic flexure ⇒ Pelvic organs)

Long Preganglionic fiber Different pelvic nerves innervating abdominal and pelvic organs from the splenic colic flexure downwards

Short Postganglionic fiber (Target organ)

SYMPATHETIC VS PARASYMPATHETIC

SYMPATHETIC

  • Intermediolateral cell columns

Sympathetic trunk

Cervical ganglia

Prevertebral ganglia

PARASYMPATHETIC

Brainstem parasympathetic nuclei

Cranial nerves (specially X, vagus nerve)

Sacral spinal cord (S2-S4)

[PDF p. 251]

AUTONOMIC SYSTEM SYNAPSE

Synaptic terminals

Both sympathetic and parasympathetic have axonal varicosities instead of regular synaptic terminals, which are found in the neuromuscular junction of somatic synapses.

Varicosities

Axonal enlargement

Neurotransmitters are released and diffuse, along a big synaptic cleft

Allows for neurotransmitter action on up to 10 µm of distance

Neurotransmitters

Preganglionic terminals (both S and PS) Acetylcholine is the neurotransmitter released both by Sympathetic and Parasympathetic preganglionic neurons. They are cholinergic fibers. Other neurotransmitters: Enkephalin, Substance P, Neurotensin. They have a modulatory effect. Detected by Nicotinic receptors (N2) in postganglionic neurons.

Postganglionic sympathetic terminals Release Adrenaline and Noradrenaline. They are adrenergic fibers. This is one of the reasons why the adrenal medulla is considered to be part of the sympathetic system. Chromaffin cells, upon Ach release by the preganglionic fiber, release Adrenaline (80%) and Noradrenaline (20%) ( also known as epinephrine and norepinephrine). Detected by metabotropic receptors (𝛼1, 𝛼2,..) in effector organs.

However, there are exceptions: sweat glands and piloerector muscles. Rather than releasing adrenaline, upon stimulation, the postganglionic neurons innervating these structures release Ach . Once again, we fin cholinergic fibers (in the case of sweat glands: muscarinic cholinergic receptors in effector muscle; M3)

Postganglionic parasympathetic terminals In the case of PS, the neurotransmitter released continues to be Acetylcholine. They are postganglionic parasympathetic cholinergic fibers. Ach detected by muscarinic receptors in effector organs (M1, M2, M3,…)

Receptors

Cholinergic receptors

  • Nicotinic: ligand gated channels, which upon binding, effectuate by allowing ion transport. They turn into ionic channels. Location: sympathetic and parasympathetic ganglia. Located on postganglionic neurons, receiving the Ach stimulation from preganglionic

  • Muscarinic: G-protein coupled receptors (GPCRs). Act through second messengers, triggering either the opening of ionic channels along the cell (not restricted to the area of binding) or other mechanisms (protein synthesis, transcription,…). Location: Parasympathetic target effectors, Sweat glands and piloerector

muscles

[PDF p. 252]

Types of Muscarinic cholinergic receptors:

    M1: CNS, stomach secretion

    - M2: heart

    - M3: smooth muscle

    M4: pancreas, lungs

    M5: salivary glands, pupil sphincter

Adrenergic receptors Location: Sympathetic target effectors

● Alpha receptors:

- 𝛼 1 receptors: postsynaptic. Smooth muscle contraction ⇒ G protein coupled mechanism of phosphatidylinositol degeneration = IP3 = Ca2+ release and muscle

contraction

- 𝛼 2 receptors: Pre & Postsynaptic (autoreceptor) Smooth muscle relaxation ⇒ G protein coupled mechanism of adenylate cyclase inhibition. Inhibit release of NA from adrenergic neurons.

● Beta receptors

- 𝜷 1, 𝜷 2 and 𝜷 3: postsynaptic Activating receptors, act through adenylate cyclase (AC) stimulation = ↑cAMP

Sensory branch of the autonomic nervous system

Conscious visceral pain (travels with sympathetic fibers, except for pelvic pain)

Sense of taste (parasympathetic fibers)

  • Unconscious viscerosomatic and viscerovisceral reflexes

Superior integration reflexes

Glutamate, angiotensin II, arginine, vasopressin, bombesin, cholecystokinin, galanin, substance P, enkephalin, oxytocin, somatostatin, vasoactive intestinal polypeptide

  • Reticular formation: the reticular formation is a region in the pons involved in regulating the sleep-wake cycle and filtering incoming stimuli to discriminate irrelevant background stimuli.

Nucleus of the solitary tract

Originates in the medulla oblongata . Participates in the integration of esophageal motility and emetic reflex (vomiting)

with the reticular formation.

Consists of a relay nucleus for sensitive input.

Sense of taste: anterior region

Gastrointestinal information: middle position

Cardiovascular input: Posteromedial

Respiratory input: Posterolateral

AUTONOMIC REFLEXES

The ANS integrates set reflexes in response to particular stimuli. The integration of these reflexes is the assumed area where the afferent and efferent pathways connect.

Spinal cord

  • Brainstem

  • Central autonomic network

Optic reflexes Pupillary reflexes

  • Midriasis (pupil enlargement in response to sympathetic activation or absence of light: broadens field view and more light entry, to detect potential threats farsight))

  • Miosis (pupil reduction in response to PS activation or excess light: reduced light entry, allows relaxed, up close focus on details(nearsight))

Accomodation

Diameter change in the eye’s lens in order to focus on something which is closer or more

distant

➢ Accomodation

    - Acts on the retina

    Visual input: blurry image. Afferent impulse is

transmitted through corticotectal pathway to Edinger Westphal nucleus

    Efferent impulse exits said nucleus through CIII

(oculomotor cranial nerve), preganglionic neuron

    Reaches ciliary ganglion → postganglionic neuron to

ciliary muscle and pupil sphincter

[PDF p. 254]

➢ Midriasis

    Sympathetic activation: preganglionic neuron arises at levels

T1-T3 intermediolateral cell column

    Goes up to the superior cervical ganglion, where

postganglionic neuronal soma is found

    Postganglionic extends its process along the carotid plexus

(network of intersecting sympathetic nerves which run parallel to the carotid artery into the head)

    Enters trigeminal ophthalmic branch and goes through

superior orbital fissure

    Through ciliary nerves, innervates pupil dilator muscle

➢ Miosis

    Parasympathetic activation: **retinotectal pathway**

    Afferent information is transmitted to the olivary

pretectal nucleus

    From there, relayed into the Edinger Westphal

nucleus

    Oculomotor cranial nerve (CIII) arises at said

nucleus. This preganglionic neuron carries motor information to the ciliary ganglion, where the postganglionic will arise

    Postganglionic travels with ciliary nerves to the pupil

sphincter muscle

Baroreceptor reflex

[PDF p. 255]

Gastrocolic reflex Upper GI tract

Stimulus: food, tastes

Sensory neuron detects distension info and travels up to the nucleus of the solitary tract (NTS), accompanying PS nerves to brainstem

Relay info into Dorsal motor vagus nucleus (DMV) from which glossopharyngeal (to mouth) and vagus nerve (to abdominal viscera) will exit

  • Effect: gastric acid secretion

Lower GI tract

  • Stimulus: rectal distension

Sensory neuron detects distension information and travels along PS nerves to sacral spinal cord (S2-S4)

From here, information is relayed and travels up the spinal cord to the brain, to be processed

In response, motor response is carried down spinal cord and transmits information to pelvic splanchnic nerves at S2-S4 level

  • Effect: colon contractions

Urinary reflex

Temperature regulation

Thermoreceptors : skin and anterior hypothalamus

Cold temperature → heat preservation

Posterior Hypothalamus and Sympathetic Branch

Thyroid glands and behavioral network

Increased capillary tone, decreased peripheral blood flow (so as not to lose heat through convection on skin surface) and increased flow to vital organs circulation.

Muscle spasms.

Hot temperature → heat loss

Anterior Hypothalamus, Preoptic area, Sympathetic branch, behavioral network

Decreased capillary tone and increased peripheral blood flow, to lose heat through

convection

  • In addition, sweat (water carries out a lot of energy with it in the form of sweat)
